# Unit u10 briefs: Chapters 46 to 50

Part VIII (Equity, Valuation and Investment Decisions): Chapters 46 and 47.
Part IX (Due Diligence): Chapters 48, 49 and 50.

Brief writer: senior curriculum architect, u10. Date: October 3, 2026. Revised October 3, 2026 after blueprint review round 1 (revision log at the end of this file). The rulings in `bible/ownership-resolutions.md` (R-001 to R-141), Case Bible Annexes P and TR, and the figure ledgers are already applied in the text below; a writer needs no other file to follow this brief, apart from the fact sheets and ledgers it cites for the values themselves.

Conventions used in this file:

- Chapter files are LaTeX (decision D-008): `chapters/NN-slug.tex`. Labels follow style sheet Section 3.2. Chapter titles are the `architecture.md` titles verbatim (R-113, D-028); every list heading takes the serial comma (R-141).
- "Owned by Ch N" means the concept is taught in full in Chapter N (architecture.md and the rulings); this unit cross-references it with `\cref` and never re-teaches it. Cross-references give the home label from the anchor registry or the glossary canon wherever one exists.
- Every number in an Illustrative example or exercise below was computed in Python (scripts in the u10 scratchpad: `lib.py`, `ex46*.py`, `ex47*.py`, `ex48.py`, `ex49.py`, `ex50.py`; results reproduced here). Writers must still recompute every figure they print. Every example and exercise that models a project, financing or contract names its place, date and parties (style sheet A.10); fictional parties are marked "(fictional)" once in the specification and never in the printed text, where the status label "(Illustrative)" does that job.
- Running-case numbers are never computed here. They are cited as "from Case Bible ledger: <ID and figure name>" (model outputs, values in `model/figure-ledger-case-p.md`, `-t.md`, `-r.md`) or "Case Bible input" (values stated in case-bible.md Parts 1 to 3 or in Annexes P and TR, printable as given). Simple arithmetic on inputs follows D-013 and is shown.
- Market norms marked "indicative" follow decision D-011: dated, placed, framed with drivers, and signed off by the domain-expert reviewer. Where a fact sheet now verifies a norm, the brief cites the sheet and its date instead.
- Excel formulas containing `%`, `#`, `\`, `{` or `}` are marked "excel block" (style sheet A.9); all others may go inline in `\xl{}`.
- The acquisition contract is always "share purchase agreement", never abbreviated; SPA means only the commodity sale and purchase agreement (R-084, R-137).

## 46.2 Purpose and capabilities

Purpose. Equity is the residual claim on a project, and every decision an owner makes (whether to keep developing, what to pay for a stake, when to sell, whether a refinancing is worth it) turns on what that residual is worth at a given moment and to a given investor. Chapter 8 defined equity IRR and project IRR, and Chapter 43 showed how the model computes them; this chapter teaches what they mean and how to use them. The reader learns to state a return so it can be compared, to choose a hurdle rate that fits the project's stage and risk, to see where in the development cycle value is created and who captures it, and to value an operating project by discounting each kind of cash flow at the rate its risk deserves. The chapter ends with the reader able to read a valuation report and attack its weakest assumption.

What you will be able to do (printed as the `\section*{What you will be able to do}` list):

- Compute and reconcile project IRR, equity IRR, NPV, cash yield, and money multiple for one project, and say which metric answers which question (Capability 12).
- Set a hurdle rate for a project at any stage from development to operations, and defend each premium in it (Capabilities 12 and 15).
- Value a project at each development milestone and price a stake sold at financial close (Capability 12).
- Value an operating project by risk bucket and bridge enterprise value to equity value (Capabilities 12 and 4).

## 46.4 Locked table of contents

Opening (no heading). See 46.5 below.

`\section*{What you will be able to do}` (four items above).

**46.1 Four measures of what equity earns** (`sec:46.1`)

- 46.1.1 Project IRR and equity IRR as investment measures (`ssec:46.1.1`). Applies the definitions of ssec:8.2.1 (equity IRR, project IRR and the shareholder-loan inclusion rule, which this chapter cites and does not restate) to Example 46.1, the 46.2 MWac Ma'an solar plant: 72% gearing turns an 8.77% project IRR into a 15.19% equity IRR. Adds the investment-use points Ch 8 does not cover: development fees received by sponsors (ssec:26.2.3) sit outside the project company's IRR but belong in the sponsor's own return, and a sponsor that also earns O&M or asset-management fees must say whether its quoted return includes them. Example 46.1 and Equation 46.1 (the measurement convention).
- 46.1.2 NPV at a hurdle rate, and why IRR is not a price (`ssec:46.1.2`). Uses Example 46.1 to show equity NPV of USD 3.67 million at 11.0%: the amount an investor with an 11.0% hurdle could pay above book equity. Explains why two investors agree on IRR but disagree on price (different hurdles), cross-referencing the IRR traps in Ch 5 (sec:5.5 and fw:irr-four-questions) rather than re-teaching them.
- 46.1.3 Cash yield and payback (`ssec:46.1.3`). Cash yield by year from Example 46.1 (17.2% in year 1, 15.2% in year 5, 35.5% in year 16 after the debt is repaid), why income funds and pension investors ask for it, what it hides (the return of capital inside each distribution), and how lock-ups (ssec:37.4.1) break it. Payback of 6.29 years for equity against 9.04 for the project. Ch 43 (ssec:43.2.4) already built these rows in the model; this subsection teaches what they mean.
- 46.1.4 Money multiples and the IRR-multiple tension (`ssec:46.1.4`). MOIC of 3.76x in Example 46.1; why a fund manager paid on IRR prefers early exits while a long-hold pension investor prefers multiple; Exhibit 46.1 compares the five metrics on the example.
- 46.1.5 Stating a return so it can be compared (`ssec:46.1.5`). The measurement conventions this chapter owns (R-013): nominal or real, currency, pre- or post-tax, shareholder-loan treatment, at project or holding level, gross or net of fund fees (forward reference to ssec:47.5.3 for fund fees). A one-paragraph checklist of the seven labels every quoted return needs. No example; uses the PF2 equity-return data from 46.5 as the illustration of a properly labeled return ("nominal, including fees").

**46.2 The cost of equity and hurdle rates by stage** (`sec:46.2`)

- 46.2.1 From CAPM to a project hurdle (`ssec:46.2.1`). Example 46.2: relevering an asset beta for a contracted operating wind portfolio in Iowa at October 2, 2026 market inputs (Ch 8 owns the relevering method and ssec:8.4.1 the required-return premium; here they are applied) gives a 9.56% cost of equity. Explains, citing `t-capm-inputs`, that a simple CAPM at 2026 inputs gives about 7.7% to 9.4% for betas of 0.6 to 1.0, below the 10% to 13% equity IRRs bid on project-financed UK PPP deals, and that the gap is what CAPM misses for projects: finite life, binary development risk, construction risk, leverage, illiquidity, concentrated counterparty exposure. Cross-reference sec:15.8 for how each risk bearer prices risk.
- 46.2.2 Stage premia and what each point pays for (`ssec:46.2.2`). Exhibit 46.2: a hurdle ladder from early development to operations with the risk each step retires, in two layers. Sourced anchors from `t-infra-equity-returns` (each printed with its source, date and basis): HICL's weighted average portfolio discount rate of 8.5% (March 31, 2026; listed UK core, implied equity risk premium about 3.5%); UK PFI secondary-market required returns of about 12% (2003), just above 7% (2005), just above 5% (2006 to 2007) and around 8% after the financial crisis (NAO 2012); UK PFI greenfield equity at financial close typically 12% to 15% (NAO 2012); PF2 expected returns 10.3% to 12.4% (NAO, via `uk-pfi`); private-fund gross targets of 9% to 13% (core and core-plus), 15% to 16% (value-add) and 20% or more (opportunistic) from Brookfield's 2025 10-K, labeled as gross targets across all its private funds, not infrastructure-only or net. Indicative development rungs, which no fact sheet verifies (D-011: nominal USD post-tax equity, contracted power in OECD markets, 2024 to 2026; domain reviewer to sign off): late-stage development (permits and offtake secured) 14% to 18%; early-stage development 20% to 30%; emerging-market country premium 2 to 6 points on top. Examples 46.2 to 46.4 use rates inside this ladder (9.6%, 11.5%, 14%, 18%, 25%). Explains why stage premia are not risk premia in the CAPM sense: they compensate for the probability of total loss of development spend, which a probability-weighted valuation (46.3) handles explicitly; double counting both is the most common development-valuation error. Never print the undated "core 5% to 8%, core-plus 8% to 12%" bands (`t-infra-equity-returns` "Do not state").
- 46.2.3 Country, currency, and contract-quality premia (`ssec:46.2.3`). How sponsors add a country premium for emerging markets, why a USD-indexed tariff paid in local currency still carries convertibility risk (forward reference to Ch 59), and how contract quality (take-or-pay versus merchant) moves the hurdle more than technology does. Uses Example 46.5's two rates for one asset (contracted years 7.0%, merchant years 10.0%) to show a contract-quality step of 3.0 points with no change in technology or country.
- 46.2.4 Hurdles in practice, from committee targets to bid and hold rates (`ssec:46.2.4`). The investment committee hurdle versus the rate used to bid; why sponsors bid at a lower rate than they hold for (the expectation of refinancing and sell-down gains), forward reference to sec:47.2 on how this feeds the winner's curse. Case P anchor: Kilnworth's bid target equity IRR of 16.0% on its 2016 bid model (Case Bible input, Annex P 4.7) against an FC base equity IRR of 13.2% in July 2018; from Case Bible ledger: P-F64 (the sequential bid-to-close IRR bridge, run from the 16.0% bid model down to the 13.2% FC base: the ledger's sequential steps (base rate, debt terms, PRI cover and WHT gross-up, soft mini-perm sweep, capex, VAT facility interest, tax, FX, IRR dating), summing to the gap with no residual), printed step by step with the ledger's labels, order, direction and values only. The lesson stated as a mechanism: a bid rate is only as good as the bid model it is computed on. The bid scene itself is Ch 47's (sec:47.8).

**46.3 Development economics and where value is created** (`sec:46.3`)

- 46.3.1 The development budget as an option premium (`ssec:46.3.1`). Development spend buys the right, not the obligation, to build. Case P: from Case Bible ledger P-F01, the approved development budget of USD 14.80 million (2015) against actual costs to close of USD 21.43 million (2015 3.12, 2016 6.87, 2017 7.64, 2018 3.80), reimbursed at close together with a USD 11.20 million development fee, split USD 7.84 million to Kilnworth and USD 3.36 million to Talmé (Case Bible input, Annex P 1.14.2; the fee itself is taught at ssec:26.2.3). Explains why most development spend on projects that fail is written off, and why developers run portfolios.
- 46.3.2 Valuation at each milestone with the value staircase (`ssec:46.3.2`). Framework 46.1 and Example 46.3 (Illustrative 180 MW wind project in Zhambyl region, Kazakhstan, valued in January 2027): value at each milestone from probability of reaching the next, time to get there, the stage hurdle, and the spend required. Exhibit 46.3: the staircase as a bar chart. Equation 46.2.
- 46.3.3 Who captures the step-ups (`ssec:46.3.3`). Developers, farm-in investors, and the development premium: Example 46.4 prices a 30% stake sold at financial close across buyer hurdles and finds the bargaining zone. Cross-references sec:32.4 for the development premium and Ch 32 for the legal form of farm-downs (R-068). Case P anchor (Case Bible inputs, Annex P 1.14.2): the ABDB fund paid a USD 4.85 million development premium for 15% at close, buying 10 points from Kilnworth (USD 3.2333 million) and 5 points from Talmé (USD 1.6167 million), not strictly pro rata to their 70:30 stakes; stated without recomputation and used to ask what hurdle the premium implies (answered qualitatively only, because the implied hurdle needs model cash flows).
- 46.3.4 Value created in construction and at COD (`ssec:46.3.4`). The re-rating at completion: the same cash flows discounted at an operating rather than a construction hurdle. Short Illustrative calculation reusing Example 46.4's cash flows: at COD (end of year 2) the distribution stream is worth USD 120.59 million at the 11.5% construction-stage hurdle and USD 138.57 million at the 9.6% operating hurdle of Example 46.2, a re-rating of USD 17.98 million on 100% of the equity with no change in any cash flow. Ties to why construction-stage investors sell at COD.

**46.4 Valuing an operating project** (`sec:46.4`)

- 46.4.1 Unlevered and levered DCF, and the equity bridge (`ssec:46.4.1`). Enterprise value from unlevered post-tax cash flow at an unlevered rate; equity value from distributions at a levered rate; the bridge EV minus net debt (including shareholder-loan treatment, reserve cash and hedge liabilities). Equation 46.3. Why the two routes disagree when the levered rate is not consistent with the gearing path (gearing falls as debt amortizes; a constant levered rate is an approximation; cross-reference Ch 8 for APV). Real case paragraph: Indiana Toll Road (see 46.9).
- 46.4.2 Valuation by risk bucket (`ssec:46.4.2`). Framework 46.2 and Example 46.5: contracted years at 7.0% and merchant years at 10.0% give EV of USD 148.67 million; a single blended 8.0% gives USD 151.06 million and hides that the merchant tail is 21.7% of value. Exhibit 46.4. Hedges are valued inside their bucket, not as a separate asset: a fixed-volume or fixed-shape hedge moves volumes from the merchant to the hedged bucket for its term, and its mark-to-market, where needed for the equity bridge, uses the swap valuation method of ssec:6.8.3 (R-006, R-138); this is the treatment the Case R installment applies to R1's swap and R4's shape hedge.
- 46.4.3 Terminal value, finite lives, and residual value (`ssec:46.4.3`). This chapter owns terminal value as a valuation method (R-014; bold `\term{terminal value}` here, canon definition). Teaches: valuing to the end of useful life or concession as the default; why "no terminal value beyond useful life" is the prudent base case; the tail rate, a higher discount rate applied to long-dated, fully merchant cash flows within the asset's life (the Case R rule of Annex TR R.7: a 10.50% unlevered rate for cash flows after 2040, applied from the valuation date, not a capitalization rate); exit multiples only as cross-checks; and life-extension or repowering options valued at zero in the base case and shown as a probability-weighted upside line. Short Illustrative calculation reusing Example 46.5: if certification allowed a five-year life extension (years 13 to 17) with merchant cash flows of USD 14.80, 14.36, 13.93, 13.51 and 13.10 million (falling 3% a year), their PV is USD 16.93 million at the 10.0% merchant rate and USD 13.87 million at an 11.5% tail rate; at a 0.6 probability of certification the upside line is USD 10.16 million or USD 8.32 million, 6.8% or 5.6% of the USD 148.67 million bucket EV, which is why base cases exclude it and bid models that include it need a named owner for the assumption. One-sentence forward reference: sec:65.5 owns the conventions lenders and bidders apply to tail value at contract or asset end. Real case paragraph: Dulles Greenway (see 46.9).
- 46.4.4 Multiples as cross-checks, and why they mislead (`ssec:46.4.4`). Example 46.6: two operating solar plants offered in the same 2026 Andalusian auction with identical EBITDA of EUR 21.4 million and 12 versus 24 years of remaining life are worth 7.54x and 10.53x EBITDA at 8.0%. EV/MW and EV/EBITDA as sanity checks in auctions only after adjusting for remaining life and contract cover.
- 46.4.5 A sensitivity hierarchy for operating-asset value (`ssec:46.4.5`). The ranked list of value drivers for an operating renewables asset (discount rate, price and capture in merchant years, volume, opex, availability, tax), with the direction and size of each, computed on Example 46.5: contracted rate plus 1 point, EV USD 144.72 million (minus 2.66%); contracted rate minus 1 point, USD 152.84 million (plus 2.81%); merchant rate plus 1 point, USD 145.95 million (minus 1.83%); merchant rate minus 1 point, USD 151.68 million (plus 2.02%); merchant cash flow minus 10%, USD 145.44 million (minus 2.17%). A short untitled table inside the section. Explains, citing eq:14.2 (operating leverage), why a 10% revenue fall in merchant years cuts merchant cash flow by more than 10% when opex is fixed (Example 46.5 states cash flows directly, so the table applies the 10% to cash flow). Links to Ch 43 sensitivities.

**46.5 What secondary sales reveal about equity value in UK PFI** (`sec:46.5`) (Real case section; heading names deal and lesson.)

- 46.5.1 The M25 equity sale and returns on early equity (`ssec:46.5.1`). From fact sheet `uk-pfi`: M25 DBFO signed 2009; Skanska and Atkins sold their combined 50% for GBP 330 million in 2016-17; NAO estimated an annual return of around 31% on the GBP 100 million they had invested. Context from `t-infra-equity-returns` (NAO 2012): primary investors selling early typically achieved exit returns of 15% to 30% a year, while secondary buyers' required returns fell from about 12% (2003) to just above 5% (2006 to 2007). Lesson: value created between financial close and stable operations is captured by whoever holds equity through that de-risking.
- 46.5.2 Refinancing and the jump in projected equity IRR (`ssec:46.5.2`). Norfolk and Norwich: 2003 refinancing raised projected equity IRR from 16% to 60% (projected, not realized; fact sheet "do not state" rule). Forward reference to Ch 58 for gain-sharing and Ch 63 for refinancing mechanics.
- 46.5.3 Equity competitions and disclosed returns (`ssec:46.5.3`). Midland Metropolitan equity funding competition (winning expected return 8.6% reduced the price of project equity from 12% to 10%); PSBP batches 10.3% to 12.4% expected; 2021 data 7.5% to 12.4% (nominal, including fees). Lesson: competition for operating-stage equity compresses hurdles toward the operating rung of Exhibit 46.2.

**46.6 Walkthrough: reading a valuation report on an operating portfolio** (`sec:46.6`). Step-by-step tour of an Illustrative independent valuation report for a fund's December 31, 2025 NAV (see 46.7 below).

**46.7 Case R: valuing A1 by risk bucket** (`sec:46.7`). See 46.10 below.

**46.8 Practitioner's notebook** (`sec:46.8`). See 46.11 below.

**46.9 Judgment drill** (`sec:46.9`). See 46.12 below.

**46.10 A valuation sets your ceiling, and the bid must beat other bidders' ceilings** (`sec:46.10`). Close. Crystallizes: price is NPV at your hurdle; value step-ups go to whoever holds risk through them; bucket discounting keeps merchant risk visible. Last paragraph poses the problem Ch 47 solves: in an auction the winner is the bidder whose valuation is highest, which is not the same as the bidder whose valuation is right; how should a bidder adjust?

**46.11 Exercises** (`sec:46.11`). **46.12 Solutions to exercises** (`sec:46.12`). `\section*{Sources}`.

Note on Section 46.3.4 figure: using Example 46.4's distribution stream (USD 14.28 million rising 1.0% a year for 22 years, first at end of year 3 from financial close), PV at COD (end of year 2) is USD 120.59 million at 11.5% and USD 138.57 million at 9.6%; re-rating USD 17.98 million. The 9.6% is Example 46.2's 9.56% cost of equity rounded to the hurdle the investment committee uses.

Note on Section 46.4.3 figure: five-year extension cash flows 14.80 × 0.97^k (k = 0 to 4), discounted from years 13 to 17: PV USD 16.93 million at 10.0%, USD 13.87 million at 11.5%; times 0.6: USD 10.16 million and USD 8.32 million.

## 46.5 Opening

Real deal moment from `uk-pfi`: in 2016-17 two of the four shareholders in the M25 motorway DBFO, signed in 2009 with capital investment of around GBP 1 billion, sold their combined 50% for GBP 330 million, and the UK National Audit Office estimated that the sellers had earned around 31% a year on the GBP 100 million they had put in. Same road, same contract, same traffic: the difference between that return and the 10% to 12% equity returns the government was then accepting on new PF2 deals is the value created between financial close and stable operation, and the chapter's question is who creates it, who captures it, and how to measure it before you bid. (Writer: open on the sale and the NAO number, not on a definition; no em dash stacking.)

## 46.6 Worked examples

All Illustrative unless stated. Status label in title.

**Example 46.1 Five return metrics on one solar plant (Illustrative)** (`ex:46.1`, in 46.1.1, reused in 46.1.2 to 46.1.4)

- Scenario: Wadi Rum Sun Power PSC (fictional), a 46.2 MWac solar plant near Ma'an, Jordan, owned by a Jordanian developer and a Gulf-based IPP (both fictional, unnamed in the text), selling to Jordan's state single buyer under a 20-year USD-denominated PPA with a flat nominal tariff; valued at financial close on June 30, 2026. The tariff, tax rate and loan terms are Illustrative and are not statements of the real single buyer's PPA terms or of Jordanian tax law. Simplifications stated in the example: construction compressed to a single date (all capex at t = 0); annual periods; tax losses not arising; no reserves; no terminal value.
- Inputs: capex USD 41.7 million; year-1 revenue USD 6.38 million, falling 0.5% a year (module degradation, flat tariff); opex USD 0.93 million in year 1, rising 2.0% a year; corporate tax 20% (illustrative rate, not a statement of Jordanian law); straight-line tax depreciation over 20 years (USD 2.085 million a year); gearing 72%, so debt USD 30.0 million (30.024) and equity USD 11.7 million (11.676); debt is a 15-year annuity at 6.25%, debt service USD 3.142 million a year.
- Computed: year-by-year EBITDA, unlevered tax, project cash flow, interest, principal, levered tax, equity cash flow. Year-1 line: EBITDA 5.450; unlevered tax 0.673; interest 1.877; principal 1.266; levered tax 0.298; equity cash 2.010.
- Key results: project IRR (post-tax, unlevered) 8.77%; equity IRR 15.19%; project NPV at 8.0% USD 2.34 million; equity NPV at 11.0% USD 3.67 million; cash yield 17.2% (year 1), 16.7%, 16.2%, 15.7%, 15.2% (year 5), 35.5% (year 16, first year after the debt is repaid; equity cash 4.150); total distributions USD 43.93 million; MOIC 3.76x; equity payback 6.29 years; project payback 9.04 years.
- Teaching points: leverage raises IRR and cash yield but the multiple is dominated by the post-debt tail; payback ignores the tail entirely; NPV is the only metric that is a price.
- Equation 46.1 (`eq:46.1`, caption "Equity cash flow convention for IRR measurement", R-013): the convention this chapter owns for measuring equity IRR, citing the definition in ssec:8.2.1: the rate $r_E$ solving $\sum_{t=0}^{T} (\mathrm{CF}^{E}_t)/(1+r_E)^t = 0$ with $\mathrm{CF}^{E}_t$ = distributions plus shareholder-loan interest and principal minus equity contributions, post-tax at project-company level, before shareholder withholding tax, nominal, in the currency of the equity. Excel `=IRR(J20:AD20)` on a Returns-sheet row (writer states the layout: equity cash flows in row 20, years in columns J to AD).

**Example 46.2 Building a cost of equity for a contracted operating asset (Illustrative)** (`ex:46.2`, in 46.2.1)

- Scenario: Prairie Crest Wind Holdings LLC (fictional), a portfolio of three operating onshore wind farms in Iowa, United States, contracted to utilities under long-term PPAs, valued in USD, nominal, post-tax, by a pension fund's investment team on October 2, 2026.
- Inputs (market inputs dated and sourced from `t-capm-inputs`): risk-free rate 5.67%, the US 20-year Treasury par yield on October 2, 2026 (the sheet notes that long infrastructure cash flows often justify a 20- or 30-year rate rather than the 10-year, which stood at 5.28%); equity risk premium 4.09%, Damodaran's implied US premium on September 1, 2026 (trailing 12-month cash-yield basis); asset beta 0.35 and debt beta 0.15 (Illustrative); gearing 75% (D/E = 3.0).
- Computed: levered beta $\beta_E = \beta_A + (\beta_A - \beta_D) \times D/E = 0.35 + 0.20 \times 3 = 0.95$; cost of equity $= 5.67\% + 0.95 \times 4.09\% = 9.56\%$ (9.5555%). Check with the 10-year rate: $5.28\% + 0.95 \times 4.09\% = 9.17\%$.
- Teaching points: the result sits on the operating rung of Exhibit 46.2 (HICL's 8.5% portfolio discount rate is the listed-core anchor); rounded to a 9.6% committee hurdle, adding a 1.9-point construction premium gives the 11.5% construction-stage hurdle used in Examples 46.3 and 46.4. The choice of risk-free tenor moves the answer by 0.39 points, as much as raising beta by about 0.1; Damodaran's premium was estimated against a shorter Treasury rate, so pairing it with the 20-year yield is a stated judgment, not a rule. State that Ch 8 owns the relevering formula; this example only applies it.

**Example 46.3 The value staircase for a wind project (Illustrative)** (`ex:46.3`, in 46.3.2)

- Scenario: Shu Valley Wind LLP (fictional), a 180 MW onshore wind project in Zhambyl region, Kazakhstan, developed by Steppe Renewables Development (fictional), a mid-sized independent developer, valued in January 2027 at the "permits and grid offer" milestone, looking back to the site stage and forward through four milestones: site secured; permits and grid offer; PPA awarded (in a government renewable energy auction); financial close.
- Inputs: equity NPV at financial close at an 11.5% construction-stage hurdle $V_{\mathrm{FC}}$ = USD 28.7 million. Stage table:

| Stage starting at | Probability of reaching next milestone | Years to next | Stage hurdle | Spend in stage (USD m) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| PPA awarded | 0.85 | 1.0 | 14% | 3.4 |
| Permits and grid offer | 0.50 | 1.5 | 18% | 2.6 |
| Site secured | 0.60 | 1.0 | 25% | 1.9 |

- Equation 46.2 (`eq:46.2`): $V_k = p_k \times V_{k+1}/(1+h_k)^{\tau_k} - S_k$, computed backward from financial close. Spend is treated as paid at the start of the stage (simplification stated).
- Key results (USD million): PPA awarded: gross 21.40, value 18.00; permits and grid: gross 7.02, value 4.42; site secured: gross 2.12, value 0.22. Cumulative development spend 7.9.
- Teaching points: the largest step-up is the PPA award (4.42 to 18.00); at site stage the project is worth barely more than nothing, which is why early development is funded by developers' portfolios and not by project-by-project capital; using both a high stage hurdle and a low probability double counts (46.2.2).

**Example 46.4 Pricing a 30% stake sold at financial close (Illustrative)** (`ex:46.4`, in 46.3.3; extended in 46.3.4)

- Scenario: Steppe Renewables Development (fictional), the developer from Example 46.3, sells 30% of Shu Valley Wind LLP at financial close (planned for the second quarter of 2028) to Kestrel Ridge Infrastructure Fund II (fictional), a European core-plus fund.
- Inputs: total equity contribution USD 68.3 million (simplification: contributed at financial close); distributions begin at the end of year 3, USD 14.28 million (14.2835) in the first year, growing 1.0% a year for 22 years. These are set so that PV at 11.5% is USD 97.0 million and NPV is the USD 28.7 million used in Example 46.3.
- Computed: equity IRR of the whole equity 15.82%; NPV of 100% and value of 30% at buyer hurdles.

| Hurdle | PV of distributions (USD m) | NPV of 100% (USD m) | 30% of NPV (USD m) |
|---|---|---|---|
| 10.5% | 106.10 | 37.80 | 11.34 |
| 11.0% | 101.40 | 33.10 | 9.93 |
| 11.5% | 97.00 | 28.70 | 8.61 |
| 12.0% | 92.87 | 24.57 | 7.37 |
| 12.5% | 88.98 | 20.68 | 6.21 |
| 13.0% | 85.33 | 17.03 | 5.11 |

- Teaching points: the developer (11.5%) values the 30% premium at USD 8.61 million; a fund at 11.0% can pay up to USD 9.93 million; bargaining zone USD 8.61 million to USD 9.93 million plus reimbursement of 30% of development costs (30% of USD 7.9 million = USD 2.37 million). A buyer at 12.5% cannot meet the seller's value; the sale happens only if the seller needs capital, which is when developers accept below their own value. Cross-reference Ch 32 for how the premium is paid (at close, deferred, or via a development fee).
- Extension for 46.3.4: PV at COD (end of year 2) of the same stream is USD 120.59 million at 11.5% and USD 138.57 million at 9.6%; the COD re-rating on 100% of equity is USD 17.98 million.

**Example 46.5 Valuing an operating asset by risk bucket (Illustrative)** (`ex:46.5`, in 46.4.2; Exhibit 46.4 shows the table)

- Scenario: Bitterbrush Mesa Solar LLC (fictional), an operating 140 MW solar plant in Maricopa County, Arizona, United States, valued on December 31, 2026 for a possible sale by its owner, Copperline Renewables (fictional), with 12 years of remaining useful life: seven years left on a fixed-price PPA with Sonoran Valley Electric (fictional utility), then merchant. Unlevered post-tax cash flows in USD million, nominal.
- Inputs: years 1 to 7 contracted cash flow USD 21.6 million a year; years 8 to 12 merchant expected cash flow 17.3, 17.0, 16.6, 16.1, 15.5; contracted rate 7.0%; merchant rate 10.0%; comparison single rate 8.0%; net debt for the bridge: debt USD 96.4 million, cash USD 7.8 million.
- Key results: PV contracted USD 116.41 million; PV merchant USD 32.26 million; EV by bucket USD 148.67 million; EV at a single 8.0% USD 151.06 million (overstates by USD 2.39 million); implied single rate equal to bucket EV 8.33%; merchant share of EV 21.7%; merchant PV at 8.0% would be USD 38.60 million; equity value by bridge USD 60.07 million.
- Equation 46.3 (`eq:46.3`): equity value $= \mathrm{EV} - D + \mathrm{Cash}$, with the rule for shareholder loans (treated as equity when valuing the sponsor's whole interest) and for reserve cash (DSRA cash counts only if it will be released before the end of the debt).

**Example 46.6 EV/EBITDA against remaining life for two solar plants (Illustrative)** (`ex:46.6`, in 46.4.4)

- Scenario: two operating solar plants in Andalusia, Spain, offered in the same 2026 auction by Guadalquivir Solar Partners (fictional): Plant A (Écija, commissioned 2013, 12 years of remaining life) and Plant B (Carmona, commissioned 2025, 24 years).
- Inputs: each with constant unlevered post-tax cash flow equal to EBITDA of EUR 21.4 million (simplification: no tax, no capex, stated); remaining lives 12 and 24 years; discount rate 8.0%.
- Results: EV EUR 161.27 million (7.54x) and EUR 225.32 million (10.53x). Teaching point: a buyer applying a "market multiple" of 9.0x would pay EUR 192.6 million for the short-life asset and overpay by EUR 31.33 million.

## 46.7 Walkthrough

**Walkthrough: reading a valuation report on an operating portfolio** (Section 46.6). The artifact is an Illustrative independent valuation report dated February 2026, prepared by Harcourt Valuation Advisory (fictional) for the December 31, 2025 NAV of Ostwind Infrastructure Income Fund (fictional), covering five operating wind and solar assets in Germany and Poland (EUR). Steps the reader follows:

1. Find the valuation basis and date: fair value, going-concern, as of the period end; check whether it is EV or equity value and whether shareholder loans are inside.
2. Read the discount-rate table: rates by asset and by risk bucket, nominal or real, levered or unlevered; compare each with the hurdle ladder in Exhibit 46.2 and with disclosed market evidence (46.5).
3. Trace the revenue split by bucket for each year; identify the year in which merchant cash flow exceeds 50% of the total.
4. Check the price curve source and date (forward reference to Ch 48 for challenging a price curve) and the capture-rate assumption.
5. Check volumes: P50, degradation, availability, curtailment (cross-reference Ch 45).
6. Check the life assumption and any terminal or repowering value against the rules of ssec:46.4.3 (no value beyond useful life; tail rate for long-dated merchant years; extensions as a probability-weighted upside line).
7. Reconcile EV to equity: debt at fair value or face value, swap mark-to-market (method in ssec:6.8.3), reserve cash, tax equity interests.
8. Read the sensitivity table and rank drivers using the 46.4.5 hierarchy; pick the weakest assumption and quantify it.
9. Compare to transaction evidence: multiples per MW and per EBITDA, adjusted for remaining life (Example 46.6).
10. Write three questions for the valuer. Model answer in the chapter: a short marked-up page with margin notes.

## 46.8 Frameworks

- **Framework 46.1: The value staircase** (`fw:value-staircase`), original. Purpose: value a development project at any milestone and decide whether the next tranche of development spend is worth it. Steps: (1) list milestones from today to financial close; (2) estimate the equity NPV at financial close at the construction hurdle; (3) for each stage, estimate probability of reaching the next milestone, time, spend, and a stage hurdle that excludes failure risk (because the probability carries it); (4) roll back with Equation 46.2; (5) compare each value with the spend needed to reach it; (6) identify the step-up you are buying and who could buy it cheaper. Demonstrated in Example 46.3 and Exercise 46.12.
- **Framework 46.2: Risk-bucket valuation** (`fw:risk-bucket-valuation`), original (practice is widespread; the book's formulation is original). Purpose: value an asset whose cash flows carry different risks over time. Steps: (1) classify each year's cash flow into contracted, hedged, merchant (and storage merchant if relevant), using the definitions in Ch 20, moving hedged volumes into the hedged bucket for the hedge's term; (2) assign each bucket a rate consistent with the hurdle ladder, with a tail rate for long-dated merchant years where the investment committee uses one (ssec:46.4.3); (3) discount each stream separately; (4) report value by bucket and the merchant share; (5) compute the implied single rate as a cross-check; (6) stress the merchant bucket's rate and cash flow separately. Demonstrated in Example 46.5 and the Case R installment.
- Established tools used (credited, not owned): CAPM (Sharpe 1964; owned by Ch 8); NPV and IRR (Ch 5).

## 46.9 Real cases

| Fact sheet slug | Use in Ch 46 | Transferable lesson |
|---|---|---|
| `uk-pfi` | Opening (M25 sale, about 31% NAO-estimated return on GBP 100 million); 46.5.2 (Norfolk and Norwich projected IRR 16% to 60%, projected only); 46.5.3 (Midland Metropolitan 12% to 10%; PSBP and 2021 PF2 IRRs) | Value created by de-risking accrues to equity held through it; competition for de-risked equity compresses hurdles; disclosed returns need full labels (46.1.5) |
| `dulles-greenway` | 46.4.3 short paragraph: Macquarie bought TRIP II in 2005 for USD 617.5 million; in 2020 the Virginia SCC hearing examiner found the investors' unearned authorized return unlikely ever to be substantially recovered; distributions locked up until at least the end of 2028 | A ramp-up asset with accreting debt can carry an equity value that depends on a tail that never arrives; value the lock-up years at zero distributions |
| `indiana-toll-road` | 46.4.1 one paragraph (with opening of Ch 47 doing the bid story): concession bought in 2006 for about USD 3.8 billion, sold out of Chapter 11 in 2015 for USD 5.725 billion with 66 years left | Enterprise value and equity value diverge when the capital structure fails; the asset can be worth more while the equity is worth nothing |
| `t-infra-equity-returns` (topic sheet) | Exhibit 46.2 anchors (HICL 8.5%, NAO greenfield 12% to 15%, NAO secondary 12% to 5% to 8%, Brookfield gross targets); 46.5.1 exit returns of 15% to 30% | Required returns fall by stage and by competition for de-risked equity; gross targets, asset discount rates and net fund IRRs are different numbers |
| `t-capm-inputs` (topic sheet) | Example 46.2 inputs (October 2, 2026 20-year and 10-year Treasury yields; Damodaran implied premium September 1, 2026); 46.2.1 CAPM-against-bid-IRR gap | A market CAPM explains only the operating rung; project hurdles add stage, leverage and illiquidity premia |

Facts only from the fact sheets; observe each sheet's "do not state" list (for example: Norfolk and Norwich IRRs are projected; do not quote the PAC "unacceptable face" phrase; do not present undated "core 5% to 8%" bands or call Brookfield's targets infrastructure-specific or net; do not state a Kroll premium history beyond "5.5% as of February 2024").

Fact sheets used are all delivered. No fact sheet verifies development-stage hurdles; the two development rungs of Exhibit 46.2 stay D-011 indicative (see the unit's Open requests list).

## 46.10 Running-case installment

**Section 46.7 Case R: valuing A1 by risk bucket.** Case beat (Case Bible Part 6, row 46): November to December 2021, Lattimer values Mesa Corta Renewables (R1 to R5, sponsor interests) for the A1 bid. Story state: diligence under way; ends with the bid price set. Characters: Rafael Quintanilla (fund partner), Thandeka Mabuza (principal, recently arrived from the ABDB).

Scene outline (inside `casescene`, second argument "Lattimer, Houston, November 2021"):

1. Rafael and Thandeka review the bucket valuation printout on November 30, 2021, two days before binding bids are due (December 2, 2021; Annex TR R.1). Rafael argues the West solar capture assumption should sit in the base case; Thandeka asks "What's the counterfactual?" (her habit, once) about what the portfolio would earn if the Calloway Mesa vPPA counterparty defaulted.
2. Friction: Rafael wants to apply the hedged rate to R4's fixed-shape volumes beyond 2026; Thandeka points out the hedge ends in 2026 (Case Bible input) and the volumes are merchant from 2027.
3. They disagree on whether the post-2040 rate of 10.50% applies to R5's cash flows; the narration resolves it with the Case Bible rule (Annex TR R.7): the 10.50% is an unlevered tail rate for cash flows after 2040 within each asset's useful life, applied from the valuation date; no terminal value is taken beyond any asset's useful life; R1's repowering option is valued at zero; there is no separate post-2040 levered rate. Taught as a tail rate (ssec:46.4.3), not a capitalization rate.
4. The scene ends without a moral, on Rafael choosing the bid number; narration outside the box notes that his capture assumption later proves to be the high case (Case Bible Section 4.3), with forward reference to sec:47.10 for the auction and Ch 63 for what that cost.

Figures to display (outside the box, Exhibit 46.5 `exh:46.5`, caption "Case R A1 valuation by asset and risk bucket at bid (USD m) \caser"):

- Discount rates by bucket, Case Bible input (Section 3.8, as amended by Annex TR R.7): contracted 6.75% unlevered and 8.25% levered; hedged 7.75% and 9.50%; merchant 9.25% and 11.50%; storage merchant 10.25% and 12.50%; tail rate after 2040 10.50% unlevered, no levered equivalent.
- From Case Bible ledger: R-F04, base case, by asset and bucket (R1 to R5 and platform costs), the bucket totals (contracted, hedged, merchant), value before the purchase-price tax shield, the tax shield at the price paid, A1 enterprise value, price paid, value less price and breakeven price; low and high cases by asset with their enterprise values, value less price and breakeven prices. Writers print the ledger values (for orientation only, not to be recomputed: base EV USD 439.7 million against the USD 446.3 million price, value less price USD (6.6) million, breakeven USD 438.2 million; low EV USD 332.8 million; high EV USD 579.8 million). The low case is a deliberately severe stress and is labeled so (D-014).
- Case Bible inputs: A1 price USD 446.3 million (Case Bible 3.6 as amended by R-C04 and D-014; the v1.0 value of USD 1,184.6 million is superseded and never printed), made up of a USD 436.0 million locked-box headline price plus a USD 10.3 million ticker (Annex TR R.1; Ch 47 teaches the mechanism); transaction costs USD 14.9 million (Section 3.6); asset table and hedge book (Sections 3.3, 3.4).
- D-013 arithmetic the narration may show from R-F04: the merchant bucket's share of the pre-shield value (164.8 of 355.1, 46.4% in the base case), and the share of value that rests on the tax shield (84.6 of 439.7, 19.2%).
- Narration explains the bucket classification rules (Case Bible Part 9, note 6), that R1's fixed-volume swap and R4's fixed-shape hedge are valued inside the hedged bucket rather than as separate assets (R-006, R-138; method ssec:6.8.3), and why tax-equity cash to Castellan in R3 and R5 is deducted before Mesa Corta's share (Ch 32 owns tax equity).

## 46.11 Practitioner's notebook (outline)

Checklist:
- Every quoted return carries seven labels: metric, currency, nominal or real, pre- or post-tax, level (project, holdco, fund), gross or net of fees, and date.
- The valuation states EV or equity, includes or excludes shareholder loans, and states the life assumption.
- Each risk bucket has its own rate, and the merchant share of value is reported.
- The development valuation uses probabilities or stage premia for failure risk, not both.

Red flags:
- An equity IRR quoted without the gearing and debt terms behind it.
- A multiple-based valuation with no adjustment for remaining life.
- A single blended rate on an asset whose contract ends partway through its life.
- Distributions assumed during years when the lock-up test would fail on the lenders' case.
- Development value that rises with spend rather than with milestones.

Rules of thumb and their limits:
- The PPA award or concession signature is usually the largest single step-up in development value (Example 46.3); it fails where the PPA is weak (sub-investment-grade offtaker without support) and the real step-up comes at financial close.
- Each 1 point on the discount rate moves the value of a flat 20-year contracted cash flow at 7% by about 7% to 8% (minus 7.32% for plus 1 point, plus 8.27% for minus 1 point); the effect grows with duration and shrinks for shorter lives (Example 46.5 moves less than 3% because its life is 12 years).
- Operating contracted equity trades near the operating rung of Exhibit 46.2; the rung moves with long-term rates and with the depth of the buyer pool.

Common mistakes:
- Discounting equity distributions at an unlevered rate, or project cash flows at a levered rate.
- Treating shareholder-loan interest as a cost rather than an equity return.
- Including DSRA cash in equity value at face when it will be released only at the final debt repayment.
- Comparing a nominal bid IRR with a real hurdle.

Questions experts ask:
- What is the value today if the project never refinances and never sells down?
- Which year's cash flow carries the most value, and which bucket is it in?
- At what discount rate does the deal stop working, and who in the market has a cost of capital below that?
- What did the last three comparable transactions imply, after adjusting for life and contract cover?

## 46.12 Judgment drill

The situation (second person, with numbers and deadline): You are the investment director of Steppe Renewables Development, which owns 100% of Shu Valley Wind, the permitted 180 MW project in Zhambyl region with a grid offer in hand (the Example 46.3 project, now at the "permits and grid offer" milestone). On Monday, February 15, 2027, Nordhavn Pension Infrastructure (fictional), a pension-backed fund, offers USD 6.1 million today for 49% plus a pro rata share of future development spend, with your firm keeping development management. Your board meets on Friday, February 19, 2027. Your stage valuation (Example 46.3) puts 100% at USD 4.42 million.

Reasoning outline: compute what 49% is worth on your numbers (USD 2.17 million); the offer is 2.8 times that, implying the fund either uses a lower stage hurdle, a higher probability of PPA award, or values portfolio access; test which assumption makes 49% worth USD 6.1 million; weigh what the fund's money buys (it funds 49% of the USD 3.4 million and USD 2.6 million future spend, cuts the developer's exposure to a lost tender) against what it costs (49% of the PPA step-up); consider governance (veto on bid tariff), the fund's own hurdle at financial close, and a tag or drag on any later sale. Expert answer: accept if the fund's price holds after the PPA award only through a deferred-consideration ratchet that keeps part of the upside, or counter at a smaller stake; do not sell 49% at a price that only reflects today's milestone.

What would change the answer: a tender with a low ceiling tariff (reduces the PPA step-up; selling now is better); a second strong project in the developer's pipeline needing the same cash (selling now is better); evidence that the fund will insist on a lower bid tariff to secure an award (selling is worse); a PPA probability well above 0.50 from a tender with few qualified bidders (selling is worse).

## 46.13 Exercise set (15)

Tier 1, concept checks (4):
- 46.1: Which of project IRR, equity IRR, NPV, cash yield, MOIC answers each of five investor questions (matching).
- 46.2: Explain why the equity IRR in Example 46.1 exceeds the project IRR and state the condition under which leverage would lower equity IRR.
- 46.3: Explain the double-counting error when a stage hurdle and a probability of failure are both applied, with a one-line numerical illustration.
- 46.4: Label a quoted return ("12% IRR on the PF2 schools batch") with the seven labels from 46.1.5 using the fact sheet data.

Tier 2, calculation and drafting (7):
- 46.5: Recompute Example 46.1's equity IRR, NPV at 11.0% and MOIC at 65% gearing (same 15-year annuity at 6.25%, same tax treatment). Inputs: as Example 46.1 with debt USD 27.105 million and equity USD 14.595 million. Answers: equity IRR 13.62%; equity NPV at 11.0% USD 2.75 million; MOIC 3.30x. Common wrong answer: holding debt service at USD 3.142 million instead of resizing it to the smaller loan.
- 46.6: Compute the levered cost of equity at 70% and 80% gearing with Example 46.2's inputs (risk-free 5.67%, premium 4.09%, asset beta 0.35, debt beta 0.15), and comment. Answers: 70% (D/E 2.333): levered beta 0.817, cost of equity 9.01%; 80% (D/E 4.0): levered beta 1.15, cost of equity 10.37%. Comment: moving from 70% to 75% gearing adds 0.55 points (9.01% to 9.56%) and from 75% to 80% adds 0.82 points, because D/E, not gearing, enters the relevering formula and D/E accelerates as gearing rises.
- 46.7: Rework Example 46.3 with the probability of a PPA award (the "permits and grid offer" stage) at 0.35 instead of 0.50, and find the probability at which the site-stage value is zero. Answers: PPA-awarded value unchanged at USD 18.00 million; permits-and-grid gross 4.92, value USD 2.32 million; site gross 1.11, value USD (0.79) million (not worth developing from the site stage); site value is zero at a PPA-award probability of 0.467.
- 46.8: For Example 46.4, find the buyer hurdle at which 30% is worth exactly the seller's USD 8.61 million premium plus the USD 2.37 million cost reimbursement (USD 10.98 million in total). Answer: 10.62% (PV of distributions USD 104.90 million; NPV of 100% USD 36.60 million). Common wrong answer: solving for 30% of PV rather than 30% of NPV.
- 46.9: Revalue Example 46.5 with the merchant rate at 11.0% and merchant cash flows 10% lower; state the merchant share of EV. Answers: PV contracted USD 116.41 million; PV merchant USD 26.58 million; EV USD 142.99 million, minus 3.82%; merchant share 18.6%.
- 46.10: Build the equity bridge for Kauri Point Wind Ltd (fictional), a 168 MW operating wind farm in the Manawatū region, New Zealand, valued in NZD at December 31, 2026: EV NZD 392.6 million (changed from 397.3 in round 2 because 397.3 is Example 21.4's NSR revenue, D-134); senior debt NZD 268.3 million (final maturity in year 15); DSRA cash NZD 14.1 million, released only at year 14 under the facility agreement; shareholder loans NZD 37.9 million at face; swap liability (mark-to-market owed by the project) NZD 6.2 million; levered equity discount rate 10.8%. Value 100% of the sponsor interest and split it between shareholder loans and share capital. Answers: PV of the DSRA release NZD 3.35 million (14.1/1.108^14); sponsor interest = 392.6 − 268.3 − 6.2 + 3.35 = NZD 121.45 million; with shareholder loans valued at face NZD 37.9 million, share capital NZD 83.55 million (recomputed in Python). Common wrong answer: adding DSRA cash at face (NZD 132.2 million), or deducting the shareholder loans as if they were third-party debt.
- 46.11: Draft a three-sentence valuation-basis paragraph for Copperline Renewables' board paper on the Example 46.5 sale (December 31, 2026) that states every label required by 46.1.5. Model answer states EV or equity, currency, nominal, post-tax, unlevered rates by bucket, treatment of shareholder loans and DSRA, life assumption and the valuation date.

Tier 3, case problems and model tasks (4):
- 46.12: Pampa Norte Energía SpA (fictional) is developing a 60 MW solar plant with a 30 MW, 4-hour battery near María Elena, Antofagasta region, Chile, valued in USD in March 2027. Equity NPV at financial close at an 11.5% construction hurdle is USD 13.27 million. Stages (probability of reaching the next milestone; years; stage hurdle; spend at the start of the stage, USD million): PPA signed to financial close 0.84, 0.9, 13.5%, 1.27; environmental approval (RCA) and grid agreement to PPA signed 0.48, 1.25, 17%, 0.86; interconnection study complete to RCA and grid agreement 0.58, 1.5, 22%, 0.64; today to interconnection study complete (a usable study result) 0.72, 0.5, 25%, 0.29 (the study's cost). Value the project at each milestone and advise whether to fund the USD 0.29 million interconnection study. Answers (gross, then value, USD million): PPA signed 9.946, 8.676; RCA and grid 3.422, 2.562; study complete 1.103, 0.463; today 0.298, 0.008. The study is worth funding only at the margin: today's value is about USD 8,000. Breakeven PPA-award probability 0.476; at 0.36 today's value is USD (0.229) million. Advice: fund it only with a plan to lift the PPA-award probability (a corporate PPA with a named mining offtaker) or to cut later spend; otherwise put the money into a project higher up the staircase. Common wrong answer: comparing the USD 13.27 million value at financial close with the USD 0.29 million study cost.
- 46.13: Case R: using Case Bible discount rates (Section 3.8 as amended by Annex TR R.7) and R-F04 (base, low and high), write a one-page note to Lattimer's investment committee explaining why the bucket method is preferred to a single blended rate for A1. Starting data: ledger R-F04 and R-F05 only; no new running-case figures may be computed beyond D-013 arithmetic. Model answer computes, by D-013 arithmetic, each bucket's share of pre-shield value in the three cases (base: contracted 149.9, hedged 40.4, merchant 164.8 of 355.1, merchant 46.4%; low: merchant 59.4 of 248.2, 23.9%; high: merchant 303.5 of 495.2, 61.3%), shows that the swing between low and high EV (USD 332.8 million to USD 579.8 million) comes almost entirely from the merchant bucket while contracted and hedged values barely move (149.1 to 150.5; 39.7 to 41.2), and argues that a single rate would hide this concentration and would have to change with every price case.
- 46.14: Model task (macro-free): on the Returns sheet of `model/Case_P_Model.xlsx` (or the Chapter 43 build-along workbook, `model/build/Ch43_*.xlsx`), add four rows below the existing equity cash flow row (row 8, dates in row 7, first period in column J): cumulative contributions, cumulative distributions, cash yield by period (distributions over cumulative contributions, annualized for semiannual periods), MOIC, and a "value at hurdle" row using a hurdle input cell on Inputs. Report the formulas in an excel block. Check: with the hurdle cell at 16.0%, the value-at-hurdle row reproduces the sheet's existing "Equity NPV at 16.0%, at financial close" line and the ledger value P-F16. Answer key gives the formulas; values come from the workbook, not from the brief.
- 46.15: Using the `uk-pfi` and `t-infra-equity-returns` facts, write a 300-word note to a ministry explaining why early PFI equity earned high secondary returns and what PF2 changed, separating projected from realized returns. Model answer uses the M25 sale (about 31% a year, NAO estimate), the NAO's 15% to 30% early-exit returns, the fall in secondary required returns from about 12% (2003) to just above 5% (2006 to 2007), and the PF2 expected returns of 10.3% to 12.4%, each labeled projected or realized.

## 46.15 Glossary entries owned

Entries follow `bible/glossary-canon.md`. Removed from this chapter by ruling: "project IRR" and "equity IRR" (home ssec:8.2.1, R-013, R-130); this chapter uses them without bold and cites ssec:8.2.1. Added by ruling: "terminal value" (R-014).

| Term | Abbreviation | Definition | Home section |
|---|---|---|---|
| hurdle rate | – | The minimum return an investor requires before committing capital to a given project at a given stage; the rate at which it discounts to set a price. | 46.1.2 |
| cash yield | – | Cash distributed to equity in a period divided by the equity invested, used by income investors as a measure of current return. | 46.1.3 |
| money multiple | MOIC | Total cash returned to equity divided by total equity invested, ignoring timing. | 46.1.4 |
| stage premium | – | The addition to an operating-asset hurdle that investors require for holding a project at an earlier stage of development or construction. | 46.2.2 |
| bid rate | – | The discount rate a sponsor uses to price a competitive bid, usually below the rate at which it would hold the asset, reflecting expected refinancing and sell-down gains. | 46.2.4 |
| value staircase | – | The book's framework for valuing a development project at each milestone by rolling back from financial close with probabilities, time, stage hurdles and remaining spend. | 46.3.2 |
| development value | – | The probability-weighted present value of a development project's equity at a milestone, net of the spend needed to reach financial close. | 46.3.2 |
| enterprise value | EV | The value of a project's unlevered cash flows to all capital providers, before deducting debt. | 46.4.1 |
| equity value | – | Enterprise value less net debt and other senior claims, or equivalently the present value of distributions to equity at a levered rate. | 46.4.1 |
| equity bridge | – | The reconciliation from enterprise value to equity value through debt, cash, reserves, hedges and shareholder loans. | 46.4.1 |
| risk-bucket valuation | – | Valuation that discounts contracted, hedged and merchant cash flows separately, each at a rate matched to its risk. | 46.4.2 |
| terminal value | – | The value attributed to a project's cash flows beyond the explicit forecast horizon or the end of its main contract, limited for a finite-life asset to what it can still earn and the probability of earning it. | 46.4.3 (R-014) |
| EV/EBITDA multiple | – | Enterprise value divided by a year's EBITDA, used as a transaction cross-check; misleading for assets with different remaining lives. | 46.4.4 |

## 46.16 Neighbors

Before: Chapter 45, Sector-specific modeling. Completes the modeling course by showing what changes in the model for particular sectors: energy yield and P-values in the model, degradation and augmentation, availability and curtailment for renewables; traffic ramp-up for toll roads; mining reserves, grades and recoveries; LNG and commodity-linked revenues; PPP payment mechanisms and deductions. Its running-case work builds Case T's traffic ramp-up model (Pellow and Ridgeway against actuals) and Case R's yield and capture model, which Chapter 46 relies on for the volumes and capture prices behind the A1 valuation.

After: Chapter 47, Bidding, acquisitions, and infrastructure funds. Takes the valuation tools of Chapter 46 into competition: how to price a bid in a tender, why the winner tends to be the most optimistic bidder and how to correct for it, how operating assets are bought and sold (auction process, the share purchase agreement, warranty and indemnity insurance, locked-box versus completion-accounts pricing), how to build a portfolio, and how infrastructure funds are structured, paid and behave. Running-case installments: Case P's 2016 tariff bid, Case T's 2014 BAFO and the winner's curse, Case R's 2021 A1 auction.

## 47.2 Purpose and capabilities

Purpose. Chapter 46 taught what an asset is worth to one investor. In practice assets change hands through competitions: governments tender tariffs and concessions, sellers run auctions, and funds compete for capital from their own investors. This chapter teaches the reader to price a bid that wins without overpaying, to recognize and correct the winner's curse, to run or respond to a sale process and negotiate the share purchase agreement (pricing mechanism, warranties, insurance), to assemble assets into a portfolio, and to understand how infrastructure funds are structured and paid and how that shapes what they bid and when they sell.

What you will be able to do:

- Build a bid tariff or bid contribution from a target equity return, and test it for the winner's curse (Capabilities 9, 12, 15).
- Choose between a locked-box and a completion-accounts price mechanism and compute the price under each (Capabilities 12, 6).
- Negotiate the key protections in a share purchase agreement, including warranty and indemnity insurance, and issue a buy-side diligence request list (Capabilities 6, 7, 12).
- Explain how an infrastructure fund's fees, preferred return and carried interest turn gross returns into net returns, and predict the behavior they produce (Capability 12).
