## 47.4 Locked table of contents

Opening (no heading). `\section*{What you will be able to do}`.

**47.1 How competitive tenders set the price** (`sec:47.1`)

- 47.1.1 Bid variables and how bidders read an evaluation formula (`ssec:47.1.1`). Tariff, state contribution, upfront fee and availability payment as bid variables; how a levelized-tariff formula with fixed evaluation parameters (levelization per ssec:5.3.3) rewards back-ended or front-ended tariff shapes when the bidder's own discount rate differs from the evaluation rate. Case P's evaluation basis (Case Bible input: levelized over 25 years at a 10.0% USD discount rate, 70% dispatch, reference gas USD 5.50/MMBtu) as the running illustration; Case T's bid variable (state construction contribution with maximum tolls fixed; the three-stage evaluation of Annex TR T.2, with traffic forecasts not scored). The procurement stages (RFQ, prequalification, RFP, BAFO) are named descriptively; one-sentence forward references to ssec:58.2.1 (the IPP tender form) and ssec:58.2.4 (evaluation on a levelized tariff) (R-046).
- 47.1.2 Building the bid tariff from a target return (`ssec:47.1.2`). Example 47.1 (Illustrative 200 MWac solar plant, Bukhara region, Uzbekistan, 2026 tender): solving the flat tariff that gives a 10.0% equity IRR when debt is sized by DSCR on P90 and capped by gearing. Equation 47.1 (bid tariff solved for a target equity IRR; it cites eq:5.11 for the levelized tariff and does not re-derive it, R-001, R-116). Exhibit 47.1: tariff at 9%, 10%, 11% equity IRR and for capex minus 5% and debt rate plus or minus 50 bps.
- 47.1.3 What sits behind a record tariff (`ssec:47.1.3`). Real cases from `gulf-iwpp` (IWPP described in one line, independent water and power producer under a long-term offtake with a state single buyer, with a forward reference to ssec:69.3.2, R-067; Sharakat RO water tariffs falling from SAR 3.71/m³ and SAR 4.30/m³ in 2009 to 2011 to SAR 1.55/m³ to SAR 1.99/m³ for plants commissioned 2021 to 2024; Al Dhafra PV at USD 1.35 cents/kWh in 2020; Hassyan IWP USD 0.365/m³ in 2023; Taweelah RO financed at about 85% debt on a soft mini-perm) and `reipppp` (BW1 bids at the caps, PV tariffs about two-thirds lower by BW3). Teaches the levers that produce low bids: leverage, tenor, refinancing assumptions, low equity hurdles of strategic bidders, repeat-bidder cost curves. States, per the fact sheet, that no independent analysis verifies whether Gulf record tariffs are loss-making; the winner's curse there is a hypothesis to test.
- 47.1.4 Bid costs, bid security, and committed finance (`ssec:47.1.4`). The bidder's cost of bidding (development, advisors, lender due diligence before award), bid bonds (REIPPPP ZAR 100,000 per MW, doubled at preferred bidder), bank letters confirming committed finance, and how each disciplines over-optimistic bids. Lender perspective: what an arranger commits to in a pre-bid term sheet and what it reserves. Case T anchor (Annex TR T.2, book inputs): ARD 20.0 million bid security per bidder, and committed-finance letters with agreed term sheets from four banks plus an underwriting commitment with a coupon cap of 5.10% for the BIFA bonds; NILO gave only an eligibility letter, which is why close slipped to May 27, 2015. Cross-reference ssec:58.2.3 for bid security as a procurement design tool (R-086).

**47.2 The winner's curse** (`sec:47.2`)

- 47.2.1 The winner as the most optimistic bidder (`ssec:47.2.1`). Common-value auction logic in plain terms; Example 47.2 (Illustrative 42 km toll road concession in Colombia, 2026) computes the expected overestimate of the winning bidder from the expected maximum of N normal errors. Equation 47.2. Exhibit 47.2: expected bias for 2, 3, 4, 5 and 8 bidders.
- 47.2.2 Where optimism hides in a bid (`ssec:47.2.2`). Traffic and demand, energy yield, capex and schedule, opex, refinancing terms, terminal value and life extension, tax structuring; ranked by how often each drives losses in transport and power tenders. Forward reference to Ch 48 for challenging the underlying studies; short pointer to the Sydney Cross City Tunnel outlier forecast (taught in Ch 48 opening) and to the S&P evidence that toll-road year-1 traffic averaged about 0.73 to 0.77 of forecast (`t-traffic-forecast-accuracy`; taught in ssec:48.5.3).
- 47.2.3 Bid shading and the expected-value bid (`ssec:47.2.3`). Example 47.3 (Illustrative 2027 bid for a 500 MWac solar tender in Samarkand region, Uzbekistan): choosing the tariff that maximizes expected NPV when the probability of winning falls as the tariff rises. Equation 47.3. Exhibit 47.3: expected NPV by tariff.
- 47.2.4 The bid discipline check (`ssec:47.2.4`). Framework 47.1 in a `framework` box, then a demonstration on Example 47.1. Glossary home of "walk-away price" (R-121).
- 47.2.5 Indiana Toll Road and a winning bid financed to the limit (`ssec:47.2.5`). Real case from `indiana-toll-road`: 2006 bid of about USD 3.8 billion; about USD 3.4 billion initial debt on a nine-year interest-only bullet loan with a 20-year accreting swap at pre-set rates from 3% rising to 11.3%; 2014 prepackaged Chapter 11 with secured claims around USD 6 billion including the swap liability of about USD 2.15 billion; 2015 sale to IFM for USD 5.725 billion. Lesson: the curse can fall on the capital structure rather than the asset; a bid that needs refinancing gains and rising-rate hedges to clear the hurdle is a bid on interest rates.

**47.3 Buying and selling operating assets** (`sec:47.3`)

- 47.3.1 The auction process from teaser to completion (`ssec:47.3.1`). Stages, timings, documents and who prepares them: the teaser, the confidentiality agreement (NDA) and the information memorandum are defined here (bold `\term{}` homes, R-087), the virtual data room is used and cross-referenced to sec:85.5, and vendor due diligence reports with reliance to the buyer are cross-referenced to ssec:48.1.1. Exhibit 47.4: Illustrative auction timeline (TikZ timeline). What the seller's adviser controls (information, timetable, competitive tension) and what the buyer can do (pre-emptive offer, exclusivity request, staple financing decision). Lender role: acquisition financing and the staple package. Case R pointer (Annex TR R.1): the A1 auction ran from teasers in August 2021 (14 confidentiality agreements) through 9 first-round bids on October 12, 2021 and 3 binding bids on December 2, 2021 to signing on December 9, 2021.
- 47.3.2 Bilateral deals, pre-emption rights, and consents (`ssec:47.3.2`). Rights of first refusal and first offer among co-shareholders, tag-along and drag-along (cross-reference Ch 26), change-of-control consents from lenders, offtakers and governments (forward reference to sec:63.6 and fw:stake-sale-consent-map for the finance-document consents, minimum holdings and sequencing that Ch 63 owns, R-085). Case P pointer: Groupe Talmé's missing tag-along right (Case Bible input) as an illustration of what a 25% shareholder loses.
- 47.3.3 The share purchase agreement (`ssec:47.3.3`). Always written in full; SPA is reserved for the commodity contract (sec:21.2, R-084). Parties, sale shares and shareholder loans, price and payment, conditions precedent (regulatory, merger control, consents), conduct between signing and completion, warranties (title, capacity, accounts, contracts, permits, litigation, tax), disclosure letter, specific indemnities, limitations (de minimis, basket, cap, time limits), and the seller's position when it is a closed-end fund that must distribute the proceeds. Exhibit 47.5: share purchase agreement term map with seller-friendly and buyer-friendly positions and where they usually land (indicative, European and US infrastructure M&A, 2022 to 2026, D-011, except the W&I rows, which cite `t-ma-wi-insurance`).
- 47.3.4 Locked box versus completion accounts (`ssec:47.3.4`). Definitions, mechanics, risks to each side; Example 47.4; Equation 47.4; Clause 47.1 (permitted leakage and leakage indemnity, Illustrative) as a `clausevariants` group with three variants (47.1a seller-friendly, 47.1b lender-friendly, 47.1c buyer-friendly) and a landing paragraph (specification in 47.7 below); Framework 47.2 (choosing the pricing mechanism). The fact sheet's Faster share purchase agreement (February 2018, locked-box date December 31, 2017; leakage defined as dividends, payments and transfers of value to the sellers) may be cited as a real example of the leakage definition, paraphrased (`t-ma-wi-insurance`). Never state a share of deals using locked boxes (the sheet's "Do not state").
- 47.3.5 Warranty and indemnity insurance (`ssec:47.3.5`). Buy-side policy, limit, retention (W&I insurance sense, R-083), premium, exclusions (known matters, forward-looking warranties, leakage and price adjustments, certain tax), how W&I changes the seller's cap (Clause 47.2, Illustrative); Example 47.5. Market parameters from `t-ma-wi-insurance`, each dated: Howden's 2024 buy-side guide (limits typically 10% to 30% of EV; retentions 0.25% to 1% of EV for operational deals; one-off premiums 0.70% to 1.50% of the limit; cover 2 to 3 years for general warranties and 7 years for title and tax), Aon's EMEA retentions falling from 0.71% (2021) to 0.27% (2025), and claims data (about 18% of North American policies bound 2019 to 2023 notified; financial-statement breaches the largest loss driver). Never present Howden's ranges as 2026 pricing.
- 47.3.6 Buying a construction-stage asset (`ssec:47.3.6`). Who carries construction risk between signing and COD: deposit, payment at COD, long-stop date, price adjustments for capacity and delay, deferred consideration and earn-outs on performance; why sellers keep construction risk when buyers are income funds. Case R pointer: A3 (Case Bible input: 20% deposit at signing, seller bears construction risk, long-stop June 30, 2025); A3 itself is taught in Ch 73, so Ch 47 states only the inputs and the D-016 point that A2 and A3 were modest overpayments Lattimer accepted for portfolio reasons (values in Ch 73).

**47.4 Portfolio construction** (`sec:47.4`)

- 47.4.1 Diversifying cash yield (`ssec:47.4.1`). Example 47.6 (Illustrative 2026 portfolio review at a Toronto-based fund): portfolio mean and volatility of cash yield across contracted solar in Spain, merchant wind in Sweden and a toll road in Chile; correlation, and why diversification works less well in stress.
- 47.4.2 Concentration limits and correlation in stress (`ssec:47.4.2`). Limits by asset, technology, market, offtaker, country, and currency in fund documents; correlated failure under scarcity events (cross-reference Ch 20, Winter Storm Uri) and under a single sovereign's crisis.
- 47.4.3 Platforms versus single assets (`ssec:47.4.3`). Buying a platform (team plus pipeline plus assets), holdco leverage across a portfolio (cross-reference Ch 31; Case R holdco), and the platform premium.

**47.5 How infrastructure funds think, invest, and are paid** (`sec:47.5`)

- 47.5.1 Fund structures and strategies (`ssec:47.5.1`). Closed-end limited partnerships, open-ended funds, listed vehicles (cross-reference Ch 32 for yieldcos), separately managed accounts, co-investment; core, core-plus, value-add, opportunistic; target returns by strategy from `t-infra-equity-returns` (Brookfield's gross targets by strategy, December 2025, labeled gross and across all its private funds) and fee models by wrapper from `t-infra-fund-terms` (closed-end: management fees of 1.00% to 2.00% on committed capital and about 15% to 20% carry, per a 2024 SEC filing; Brookfield's 20% carry over a preferred return of "typically 8%"; evergreen vehicles at 1.25% of NAV with 12.5% over a 5% hurdle; continuation funds and the USD 233 billion 2025 secondary market). Never state a single market-standard fee (sheet's "Do not state"). Case R pointer: Lattimer Energy Transition Fund II terms (Case Bible input: USD 2,380.0 million commitments, 1.40% management fee on commitments during the investment period, 15% carry over an 8% preferred return, 10-plus-2-year term, target net IRR 11% to 13%).
- 47.5.2 Fees, preferred return, and carried interest (`ssec:47.5.2`). Example 47.7 (Illustrative 2019-vintage Delaware fund): a European whole-fund waterfall with an 8% compounded preferred return, full GP catch-up and 15% carry. Exhibit 47.6: the waterfall by year. American (deal-by-deal) waterfall and clawback in prose. Glossary home of "capital commitment (fund)" is ssec:47.5.1 (R-083).
- 47.5.3 Gross and net returns, and what investors actually earn (`ssec:47.5.3`). Gross-to-net spread from Example 47.7 (10.69% gross, 8.32% net); NAV and its marks; why net IRR, not gross, is the number LPs compare. Realized evidence from `t-infra-equity-returns`: Andonov, Kräussl and Rauh (2021) found a mean net IRR of 9.9% (Preqin) or 5.6% (Burgiss) and a PME of 0.93 for 2002 to 2018 closed-end infrastructure funds; EDHEC (October 3, 2026) found median exit prices about 23% above last carrying value.
- 47.5.4 How fund economics shape behavior (`ssec:47.5.4`). Deployment pressure late in the investment period, exit timing before fund term, the incentive to hold NAV marks, fee drag on long-hold assets, and the alignment problem when a sponsor's vehicle depends on growth. The 10-plus-1-plus-1 fund life and continuation funds as the way managers hold assets beyond it, with the conflict this creates (`t-infra-fund-terms`). Real case `sunedison-terraform` (short; Ch 32 owns yieldcos): drop-down growth model with IDRs and Class B control, margin loan on TERP shares, Chapter 11 on April 21, 2016; Brookfield sponsorship in 2017. Lesson: a vehicle whose fees or distributions require continuous acquisitions will bid for growth.

**47.6 Walkthrough: the bid committee pack for a tariff tender** (`sec:47.6`). See 47.7 below.

**47.7 Walkthrough: a locked-box share purchase agreement, clause by clause** (`sec:47.7`). See 47.7 below.

**47.8 Case P: the 2016 tariff bid** (`sec:47.8`).

**47.9 Case T: the BAFO that won the Merrick Link** (`sec:47.9`).

**47.10 Case R: the A1 auction** (`sec:47.10`).

**47.11 Practitioner's notebook** (`sec:47.11`). Includes Exhibit 47.10, the buy-side acquisition diligence request list (see 47.11 below). **47.12 Judgment drill** (`sec:47.12`).

**47.13 Winning the asset starts the diligence** (`sec:47.13`). Close. Crystallizes: bid on a disciplined valuation corrected for the curse; price mechanism allocates the period between valuation and completion; fund economics explain who bids what. Last paragraph poses the problem Part IX answers: every bid and acquisition rests on advisors' reports (engineer, market, traffic); how do you read them, and how do you know when they are wrong?

**47.14 Exercises** (`sec:47.14`). **47.15 Solutions to exercises** (`sec:47.15`). `\section*{Sources}`.

Example numbering (binding), in order of appearance: 47.1 bid tariff (47.1.2), 47.2 winner's bias (47.2.1), 47.3 expected-value bid (47.2.3), 47.4 locked box versus completion accounts (47.3.4), 47.5 W&I recovery (47.3.5), 47.6 portfolio cash-yield volatility (47.4.1), 47.7 fund waterfall (47.5.2).

## 47.5 Opening

Real deal moment from `indiana-toll-road`: in January 2006 a Cintra and Macquarie consortium won a 75-year lease of the 157-mile Indiana Toll Road with a bid of about USD 3.8 billion; it financed the bid with about USD 3.4 billion of debt on a nine-year interest-only bullet loan and an accreting swap whose pre-set rates rose to 11.3% by 2023; in September 2014 the concession company filed a prepackaged Chapter 11, and in 2015 IFM Investors bought the same lease for USD 5.725 billion. The winning bid was too high for the capital structure it was built on and not obviously too high for the road. Open on the 2014 filing and the 2015 sale price side by side; the question the chapter answers is how a bidder can win without the win itself being the loss.

## 47.6 Worked examples

**Example 47.1 Solving the bid tariff for a solar tender (Illustrative)** (`ex:47.1`, in 47.1.2)

- Scenario: Kyzylkum Solar Consortium (fictional; a Gulf developer with a Chinese EPC contractor) bids for a 200 MWac solar PV plant in Bukhara region, Uzbekistan, in a government tender with bids due in June 2026, under a 25-year USD-denominated PPA with the state single buyer and a flat nominal tariff; the tender evaluates the levelized tariff (ssec:5.3.3), which equals the flat tariff here. Tender terms and loan terms are Illustrative, not statements of Uzbek tender rules.
- Inputs: P50 net capacity factor 27.4%, so P50 output 480.05 GWh in year 1; one-year P90 at 92.1% of P50; degradation 0.4% a year; capex USD 117.4 million (at t = 0; construction compressed, stated); opex USD 2.31 million in year 1, rising 2.0% a year; debt 18 years, all-in fixed rate 6.40%, sculpted to a DSCR of 1.30x on one-year P90 CFADS, capped at 80% gearing; tax holiday for the PPA term (simplification stated; tax enters in Exercise 47.9); no reserves; equity contributed at t = 0; target equity IRR on P50 10.0%.
- Computed: solve the tariff for the target equity IRR (Excel Goal Seek or a data table, cross-reference Ch 13); report debt, gearing, binding constraint, year-1 CFADS and debt service.
- Key results: tariff USD 29.46/MWh at 10.0%; debt USD 80.98 million (gearing 68.98%, DSCR constraint binds, gearing cap slack); equity USD 36.42 million; year-1 CFADS USD 11.83 million (P50) and USD 10.72 million (P90); year-1 debt service USD 8.24 million; year-1 P50 DSCR 1.44x. Tariff at 9.0% target: USD 28.75/MWh; at 11.0%: USD 30.11/MWh. Capex 5% lower: USD 28.29/MWh. Debt rate 5.90%: USD 28.88/MWh; 6.90%: USD 30.04/MWh.
- Equation 47.1 (`eq:47.1`, caption "Bid tariff solved for a target equity IRR", R-001, R-116): the tariff $p^{*}$ such that $\mathrm{IRR}\big(-E_0, \mathrm{CF}^{E}_1(p^{*}), \ldots, \mathrm{CF}^{E}_{25}(p^{*})\big) = 10.0\%$, where equity cash flow depends on the tariff through revenue and through the DSCR-sculpted debt; the text cites eq:5.11 for the levelized tariff the tender evaluates and does not re-derive it. Excel: a one-variable data table or Goal Seek on the tariff input cell with the equity IRR cell as the target (macro-free); the levelized check is `=SUMPRODUCT(J12:AH12,J9:AH9)/SUMPRODUCT(J10:AH10,J9:AH9)` with revenue in row 12, energy in row 10 and discount factors in row 9 (writer states the layout).
- Teaching points: 1 point of equity IRR is worth about USD 0.65 to 0.71/MWh; 50 bps of debt rate is worth about USD 0.58/MWh; 5% of capex is worth USD 1.17/MWh. The bidder who wins by USD 0.70/MWh has either a 1-point lower hurdle or a cheaper loan.

**Example 47.2 How much the winner overestimates (Illustrative)** (`ex:47.2`, in 47.2.1)

- Scenario: in a 2026 tender for a 42 km toll road concession near Bogotá, Colombia (Illustrative; the procuring agency is unnamed), N consortia independently estimate the traffic revenue, whose true value is common to all; each estimate is unbiased with a standard error of 15% of true value.
- Computed: expected maximum of N standard normal draws (writer shows the integral and gives the values; Ch 9 owns the normal distribution).
- Key results: expected bias of the highest estimate: 2 bidders 0.564 sigma (8.5%); 3 bidders 0.846 (12.7%); 4 bidders 1.029 (15.4%); 5 bidders 1.163 (17.4%); 8 bidders 1.424 (21.4%).
- Equation 47.2 (`eq:47.2`): $\mathrm{E}[\text{winner's error}] = \sigma \times \mathrm{E}[\max(Z_1,\ldots,Z_N)]$.
- Teaching points: the bias grows with the number of bidders and with forecast uncertainty; it is zero only if all bidders share the same error. Correction: shade the estimate by the expected bias for the expected field, not for the realized field.

**Example 47.3 The expected-value bid (Illustrative)** (`ex:47.3`, in 47.2.3)

- Scenario: Kyzylkum Solar Consortium, the Example 47.1 bidder, prices its March 2027 bid for a 500 MWac solar tender in Samarkand region, Uzbekistan (a different, larger and costlier site than Example 47.1, stated), knowing its win probability falls as its tariff rises.
- Inputs: breakeven tariff at the bidder's 10.0% hurdle on its own (shaded) case USD 36.85/MWh; equity NPV gained per USD 1/MWh above breakeven USD 2.94 million; win probability $P(t) = 1/(1+e^{(t-39.00)/0.90})$ estimated from past tender results; sunk bid cost USD 1.7 million.
- Computed: expected NPV $= P(t) \times (t - 36.85) \times 2.94 - 1.7$ for tariffs USD 37.00 to 41.00/MWh.
- Key results (tariff: P(win), NPV if win, expected NPV, USD million): 37.00: 0.902, 0.44, (1.30); 37.50: 0.841, 1.91, (0.09); 38.00: 0.752, 3.38, 0.84; 38.50: 0.635, 4.85, 1.38; 39.00: 0.500, 6.32, 1.46; 39.50: 0.365, 7.79, 1.14; 40.00: 0.248, 9.26, 0.59; 40.50: 0.159, 10.73, 0.01; 41.00: 0.098, 12.20, (0.51). Optimum USD 38.83/MWh, expected NPV USD 1.48 million.
- Equation 47.3 (`eq:47.3`).
- Teaching points: bidding at breakeven to maximize the chance of winning destroys value in expectation; the win-probability curve is itself an estimate and the most uncertain input.

**Example 47.4 Locked box against completion accounts (Illustrative)** (`ex:47.4`, in 47.3.4)

- Scenario: Glenmara Renewables Fund I (fictional), a closed-end fund near the end of its term, sells 100% of Knockanore Wind DAC (fictional), an operating 74.4 MW onshore wind farm in County Cork, Ireland, to Corrib Life Assurance (fictional), an Irish insurer; share purchase agreement signed in March 2026. Figures in EUR million.
- Inputs: agreed enterprise value EUR 186.3 million; locked-box date January 31, 2026 (management accounts reviewed by the buyer's accountants); at that date debt EUR 121.9 million and cash EUR 9.2 million (net debt EUR 112.7 million); completion on July 1, 2026, 151 days later; ticker 7.0% a year (simple, actual/365) on the locked-box equity price; a dividend of EUR 3.4 million paid to the seller after the locked-box date (leakage, not permitted). Completion-accounts alternative: net debt at completion EUR 110.9 million; working-capital target EUR 2.6 million, actual EUR 1.9 million.
- Key results: locked-box equity price EUR 73.6 million; ticker EUR 2.13 million; leakage deduction EUR 3.4 million; price paid EUR 72.33 million. Completion accounts price EUR 186.3 − 110.9 − 0.7 = EUR 74.70 million. Seller's total receipts including the dividend: locked box EUR 75.73 million; completion accounts EUR 78.10 million. Reconciliation: difference EUR 2.37 million = cash generated after the locked-box date (EUR 5.2 million: net debt fall of 1.8 plus the 3.4 dividend) minus ticker (2.13) minus working-capital shortfall (0.7).
- Equation 47.4 (`eq:47.4`): locked-box price $= (\mathrm{EV} - \mathrm{ND}_{\mathrm{LB}}) \times (1 + k \times d/365) - \text{leakage}$, with the ticker accrued on the equity price (convention stated; some deals accrue on EV or use a daily cash amount).
- Teaching points: the ticker is a negotiated proxy for the cash the business earns between the locked-box date and completion; buyers resist tickers above expected cash generation, sellers resist completion accounts because the buyer prepares them.

**Example 47.5 What a W&I policy pays (Illustrative)** (`ex:47.5`, in 47.3.5)

- Inputs: Example 47.4 deal (Knockanore Wind, Corrib Life as insured buyer, policy incepting at signing in March 2026); buy-side W&I limit 10% of EV (EUR 18.63 million); retention 0.5% of EV (EUR 0.93 million), aggregate, no per-claim de minimis; premium 1.05% of limit (EUR 0.196 million, one-off); a breach of the tax warranty discovered after completion costs the buyer EUR 2.47 million. All three parameters sit inside the operational-deal ranges in Howden's 2024 guide (`t-ma-wi-insurance`); the text says the 2025 market was softer, with lower retentions.
- Key results: policy pays EUR 1.54 million; buyer bears the retention EUR 0.93 million unless the share purchase agreement makes the seller liable for the retention; a claim for a matter disclosed in the data room pays nothing.
- Teaching point: W&I lets a selling fund distribute proceeds without a long escrow; the buyer trades seller recourse for insurer recourse and inherits exclusions, including leakage and price adjustments, which stay with the leakage indemnity (Clause 47.1).

**Example 47.6 Diversifying a portfolio's cash yield (Illustrative)** (`ex:47.6`, in 47.4.1)

- Scenario: the 2026 annual portfolio review of Larchmont Core-Plus Infrastructure LP (fictional; Toronto-based manager), which holds three assets. Cash-yield statistics are Illustrative estimates from 2016 to 2025 annual data.
- Inputs: contracted solar in Extremadura, Spain (weight 45%, mean cash yield 7.2%, standard deviation 3.1%), merchant onshore wind in the Swedish SE3 price zone (30%, 10.6%, 12.4%), a toll road in central Chile (25%, 8.9%, 8.7%); correlations solar-wind 0.35, solar-road 0.05, wind-road 0.15.
- Key results: portfolio mean 8.65%; portfolio standard deviation 5.18% against a weighted average of 7.29%; alternative weights 25/50/25: mean 9.325% (prints 9.33%), standard deviation 7.17%.
- Teaching point: diversification lowers volatility only while correlations hold; in a scarcity event or a sovereign crisis correlations rise toward 1.

**Example 47.7 A fund waterfall from gross to net (Illustrative)** (`ex:47.7`, in 47.5.2)

- Scenario: Harrowgate Infrastructure Partners III, L.P. (fictional), a 2019-vintage Delaware limited partnership investing in North American contracted power and midstream assets, with a 10-year term; year 0 is 2019. Fee and carry terms are Illustrative and inside the ranges `t-infra-fund-terms` reports (1.00% to 2.00% on commitments; 15% to 20% carry); the text says no single market standard exists.
- Inputs: capital commitments USD 742.6 million; investments at year 0, 1, 2 of USD 213.4, 236.8 and 188.9 million (USD 639.1 million); management fee 1.25% of commitments in years 0 to 4 (USD 9.2825 million a year, called from LPs) and 1.0% of invested cost in years 5 to 9 (USD 6.391 million a year, deducted from distributions); investment proceeds years 2 to 10: 18.4, 41.7, 52.3, 58.6, 61.2, 63.9, 66.0, 70.4, 948.6 (year 10 includes exit); European waterfall, annual: return of contributions, then 8% compounded preferred return, then 100% catch-up to the GP until it has 15% of profit, then 85:15.
- Key results: gross IRR 10.69%; gross MOIC 2.16x; LP contributions 222.68, 246.08, 198.18, 9.28, 9.28 (years 0 to 4); total fees USD 78.37 million; carried interest USD 99.54 million, all paid in year 10; LP distributions 18.40, 41.70, 52.30, 52.21, 54.81, 57.51, 59.61, 64.01, 849.06 (years 2 to 10); LP net IRR 8.32%; LP net MOIC 1.82x; LP profit USD 564.09 million; gross-to-net spread 2.37 points. Exhibit 47.6 shows the year-by-year waterfall.
- Variant for teaching (stated in text, computed by the brief): with exit proceeds of USD 807.3 million the gross IRR is 9.28%, net 7.87%, and no carry is paid; carry starts once year-10 proceeds exceed USD 819.2 million, so the preferred return is the line between those outcomes.

## 47.7 Walkthroughs

**Walkthrough: the bid committee pack for a tariff tender** (Section 47.6). Artifact: an Illustrative 14-page bid committee pack for the Example 47.1 tender. Steps: (1) the tender's evaluation formula and how the bid tariff maps to it; (2) the base case and its sources (yield report, EPC price, debt term sheet); (3) the equity return at the proposed tariff and the hurdle; (4) the bid discipline check (Framework 47.1) line by line; (5) the win-probability estimate and the expected-value tariff (Example 47.3); (6) the walk-away tariff; (7) the conditions attached to approval (EPC price validity, committed-finance letter, bid bond); (8) the questions a committee member asks, with model answers.

**Walkthrough: a locked-box share purchase agreement, clause by clause** (Section 47.7). Artifact: Illustrative extracts of the share purchase agreement for the Example 47.4 deal (Knockanore Wind; Glenmara Renewables Fund I as seller, Corrib Life Assurance as buyer, Irish law, signed March 2026). Steps: definitions (Locked Box Date, Locked Box Accounts, Leakage, Permitted Leakage); price and ticker clause; Clause 47.1 permitted leakage and leakage indemnity in its three variants with annotations and the landing paragraph; warranties schedule and the disclosure letter; Clause 47.2 seller limitation with W&I; conditions and long-stop date; conduct-of-business covenants between signing and completion; completion deliverables. Each clause annotated with the seller's and buyer's positions and where it usually lands.

Clauses (drafted originally, Illustrative; no reproduction of any published form, and the Faster share purchase agreement in `t-ma-wi-insurance` is paraphrased only):

- Clause 47.1 Permitted leakage and leakage indemnity, share purchase agreement (`cl:47.1`), set as a `clausevariants` group in ssec:47.3.4 and reused in the 47.7 walkthrough. Letters follow style sheet Section 7 (a sponsor-side, b lender, c counterparty); in an acquisition the selling sponsor is the "sponsor" side and the buyer is the counterparty:
  - `cl:47.1a` (Illustrative, seller-friendly): Leakage limited to dividends, returns of capital and payments to the Seller's Group outside the ordinary course; Permitted Leakage includes the asset-management fee at the contracted rate, scheduled shareholder-loan interest and any payment disclosed in the Locked Box Accounts or the disclosure letter; claims must be notified within 6 months of Completion; liability limited to the amount received by the Seller's Group, net of tax actually paid on it; no interest.
  - `cl:47.1b` (Illustrative, lender-friendly, protecting the buyer's acquisition lenders): as 47.1c on scope, plus an express right for the Buyer to assign the benefit of the leakage indemnity to its lenders by way of security, a requirement that leakage repayments are paid into the Buyer's secured account, and a cap no lower than the purchase price; ties to the acquisition finance of ssec:47.3.1 and to sec:63.6 for lender requirements in stake sales.
  - `cl:47.1c` (Illustrative, buyer-friendly): Leakage defined widely (any payment, transfer of value, waiver, assumption of liability or transaction cost borne by the Target for the benefit of the Seller's Group, including tax on any of them); Permitted Leakage a closed list with euro caps (the asset-management fee capped at EUR 0.31 million for the locked-box period); euro-for-euro repayment with interest at the ticker rate; claims within 12 months; joint liability of the seller's general partner where the seller is a fund that will distribute.
  - Landing paragraph (ordinary prose, no label): in European infrastructure share purchase agreements of 2022 to 2026 (indicative, D-011), leakage usually lands close to 47.1c on scope and close to 47.1a on time limits (6 to 12 months), with the permitted-leakage list closed and capped; lenders' assignment rights (47.1b) are usually granted because acquisition finance requires them. State that `t-ma-wi-insurance` verifies no share or frequency for these positions.
- Clause 47.2 Limitation of seller liability where a W&I policy is in place, share purchase agreement (`cl:47.2`): single clause with annotations (seller cap EUR 1 for general warranties, fundamental warranties up to the price, fraud carved out, insurer's subrogation against the seller limited to fraud), mirroring Annex TR R.1's A1 terms in structure but with Example 47.4's figures.

## 47.8 Frameworks

- **Framework 47.1: The bid discipline check** (`fw:bid-discipline-check`), original. Purpose: stop a bid team from winning by assumption. Steps: (1) restate the bid on the lenders' banking case, not the sponsor case; (2) compute the return with no refinancing gain and no terminal value beyond contract; (3) compare each key assumption with the independent advisor's and with the most conservative competitor you can infer; (4) apply the winner's-curse shading for the expected field (Example 47.2); (5) state the walk-away tariff and who approved it; (6) list what must be true for the bid to clear the hurdle, and assign each to an owner. Demonstrated on Example 47.1 in 47.2.4, used in the Case P and Case T installments.
- **Framework 47.2: Choosing the price mechanism** (`fw:pricing-mechanism-choice`), original. Purpose: choose locked box or completion accounts. Steps (decision questions): reliable audited or reviewed accounts at a recent date; stable, predictable cash generation; seller's need for price certainty and quick distribution; buyer's ability to police leakage; length of the gap to completion; working-capital volatility. Output: locked box, completion accounts, or a hybrid (locked box with a net-debt true-up).
- Established concepts credited: winner's curse (Capen, Clapp and Campbell 1971, oil lease auctions); common-value auctions.

## 47.9 Real cases

| Slug | Use | Lesson |
|---|---|---|
| `indiana-toll-road` | Opening; 47.2.5 | A winning bid financed with short bullet debt and an accreting swap turns asset risk into structure risk |
| `gulf-iwpp` | 47.1.3 | Standardized tenders with creditworthy single buyers and repeat bidders drive tariffs down through leverage, tenor and refinancing assumptions; record tariffs are not proof of loss-making bids |
| `reipppp` | 47.1.3, 47.1.4 | Bid security, committed-finance letters and non-negotiable documents discipline bids; prices fall as competition and learning rise |
| `sunedison-terraform` | 47.5.4 | Vehicles whose economics depend on acquisition growth bid for growth; sponsor distress spreads to the vehicle |
| `sydney-tunnels` | 47.2.2 pointer only (taught in Ch 48) | The winning forecast was the highest of all forecasts |

| `t-ma-wi-insurance` (topic sheet) | 47.3.4, 47.3.5, Example 47.5, Exhibit 47.5 W&I rows, notebook | Locked box allocates the period after the accounts date to the buyer, policed by a leakage covenant; W&I moves warranty risk from a distributing seller to an insurer, with exclusions the buyer inherits |
| `t-infra-fund-terms` (topic sheet) | 47.5.1, 47.5.2, 47.5.4 | Closed-end fund lives push managers to sell or roll assets into continuation funds; evergreen vehicles charge on NAV with lower performance fees |
| `t-infra-equity-returns` (topic sheet) | 47.5.1, 47.5.3 | Gross targets, net IRRs and PME tell different stories; realized net returns of closed-end infrastructure funds have been below buyout's |
| `t-traffic-forecast-accuracy` (topic sheet) | 47.2.2 pointer only (taught in ssec:48.5.3) | Competitive bidding selects the most optimistic traffic forecast |

Observe each sheet's "do not state" list (for example: no single "market standard" fund fee; no share of deals using locked boxes; Howden's ranges are not 2026 pricing; no infrastructure-specific W&I claims named; Gulf record tariffs are not proven loss-making).

All fact sheets this chapter needs are delivered; no open requests.

## 47.10 Running-case installments

**Section 47.8 Case P: the 2016 tariff bid.** Story date September 2016 (pricing committee September 19, 2016; bids received September 27, 2016; preferred bidder December 8, 2016). Characters: Tomasz Wierzbicki; Devesh Raval (CFO, presenting the pricing committee's view; Annex P 2.2 row 47); Philippa Carrow (chief executive, chairs the investment committee); Kunal Mehrotra (analyst, builds the bid model; no lines needed); Abdoulaye Ndao-Sylla appears only through the RFP's short construction period. Scene (Kilnworth, London, September 19 to 20, 2016): Devesh presents the pricing committee's recommended capital charge of USD 15.05/kW-month, which Kilnworth's bid model puts at a 17.6% equity IRR; Tomasz argues for USD 14.36/kW-month after intelligence that a Gulf-based bidder will bid aggressively, converting the committee's numbers into "What does that cost us per kilowatt-month?" (his habit, once); Devesh answers with "Show me the downside first" (his habit, once); Philippa closes with "What would make us walk away?" (her habit, once) and the investment committee accepts 14.36 at a bid-model IRR of 16.0% (Annex P 4.7). Narration outside the box: the evaluation basis (Case Bible inputs: 25 years, 10.0% USD discount rate, 70% dispatch, reference gas USD 5.50/MMBtu); the tariff components (Case Bible inputs: capital charge USD 14.36/kW-month, fixed O&M USD 2.31/kW-month, variable O&M USD 3.86/MWh, fuel pass-through); Kilnworth's bid-stage cost estimate of USD 655 million before financing costs (Annex P 4.7); the result (preferred bidder). Figures (Exhibit 47.7): from Case Bible ledger: P-F06 (levelized tariff of the winning bid under the RFP formula, split into capacity, VOM and fuel); P-F62 (levelized tariffs of the four bids: winner, runner-up, third and fourth; the pricing committee's tariff at USD 15.05/kW-month against the submitted tariff); P-F64 (bid-to-close IRR bridge, from the 16.0% bid model to the 13.2% FC base in the ledger's nine sequential steps with no residual; cited in one sentence with a pointer back to ssec:46.2.4, no step values printed here). The bid IRRs are Kilnworth's 2016 bid-model values and are printed as such; the FC base IRR is a different number (P-F64). Narration applies Framework 47.1 and sets the gap to the runner-up (4.6%, Annex P 4.7) against Example 47.2's expected bias for four bidders (15.4% at a 15% forecast error); by D-013 arithmetic from P-F62, even the pricing committee's tariff would have ranked first, so the cut to 14.36 left money on the table against the runner-up. The narration states that comparison and does not say the committee's tariff "would have lost" (see the Central changes list in the revision log). Kunal's model omits the VAT facility interest (his "where wrong"); one sentence, with a pointer to the P-F64 bridge. The chapter does not judge the outcome (later chapters do).

**Section 47.9 Case T: the BAFO that won the Merrick Link.** Story date August 2014 (BAFO requested July 1, 2014; received August 15, 2014). Characters: Callum Petrie, Sasha Hrytsenko. Scene (Holbrook's bid room, Port Ellery, August 2014): Callum asks Sasha for the high value-of-time case; Sasha presents Pellow's three cases as "sensitivity ranges" and talks elasticities (habit, once); Pellow recommends the central case; Callum says "Close enough to win" (habit, once) and chooses the high case. Narration, Case Bible inputs (Case Bible 2.3 and 2.6; Annex TR T.2 and T.3): the three-stage evaluation in which traffic forecasts were not scored; the bid variable is the state contribution with maximum tolls fixed; MMP asked ARD 287.4 million, Northgate ARD 361.0 million, reference ARD 410.0 million; the third consortium withdrew in January 2014 over Merrick Ridge Tunnel ground risk; equity target 11.4%; Pellow's car value-of-time cases (ARD per vehicle-hour, 2014 prices) low 17.10, central 19.80, high 22.45, giving mature 2019 traffic of 51.2, 54.6 and 58.4 thousand trips a day; Ridgeway's banking level 52.6; Northgate's own forecast 53.9 with its stated bid-model equity IRR of 11.0%; Partnerships Brannock's adviser's August 2014 note calling Pellow's value of time "at the upper end of the plausible range" (paraphrase; Maggie Dunleavy filed it). From Case Bible ledger (Exhibit 47.8): T-F02 (bid-base equity IRR 12.3% at ARD 287.4 million and NPV at 11.4%; banking-case and downside IRRs; the contribution needed for 11.4% on the banking case; the extended gap rows: reference less winner, Northgate less winner, reference less Northgate, in ARD million and percent); T-F21 (equity IRR at ARD 287.4 million on Pellow's low and central value-of-time cases); T-F04 (Northgate's traffic line, for the exhibit's comparison). Narration applies Example 47.2 and Framework 47.1: the lowest contribution came from the most optimistic traffic case, and at Pellow's own recommended central case the bid only just met the 11.4% target (T-F21). Forward references to sec:48.8 (the traffic studies) and Ch 64 (restructuring).

**Section 47.10 Case R: the A1 auction.** Story date December 2021 (binding bids December 2, 2021; exclusivity December 4; share purchase agreement signed December 9, 2021). Characters: Rafael Quintanilla, Thandeka Mabuza. Scene (Lattimer, New York, December 1, 2021, the eve of binding bids): final-round bid call; Rafael asks "What's the shape?" (habit, once) of R4's hedge; debate over raising the headline above the bucket valuation of Section 46.7 to beat the other two bidders; Thandeka points to the expected-bias arithmetic for three bidders (Example 47.2: 0.846 standard errors). Narration, Case Bible inputs (Case Bible 3.2, 3.6, 3.9; Annex TR R.1): the two-round auction (14 confidentiality agreements, 9 first-round bids, 4 admitted, 3 binding bids); Lattimer's first-round indication of USD 395 million to USD 415 million, raised for the binding round on the high-case West solar capture; the locked box at September 30, 2021, cash-free and debt-free, headline USD 436.0 million plus a 5.00% simple ticker for 173 days (USD 10.3 million), total consideration USD 446.3 million (the USD 1,184.6 million v1.0 figure is superseded and never printed; D-014); permitted leakage of up to USD 0.4 million of asset-management fees; competing headlines of USD 421.5 million (Bidder B, a Canadian pension-backed platform, asking a 10% escrow for R3 flip risk) and USD 409.0 million (Bidder C, a US utility's unregulated arm, excluding the Uri indemnity); the buy-side W&I policy (limit USD 118.0 million, retention 0.5% of EV, USD 2.2 million, falling to 0.25% after 12 months, premium 1.20% of the limit, USD 1.42 million, inside the USD 14.9 million transaction costs; general warranties 3 years, fundamental and tax 7 years; seller's liability USD 1 for general warranties); the specific indemnity for Winter Storm Uri settlement adjustments (capped at USD 5.0 million, USD 5.0 million escrow for 18 months, excluded from W&I as a known matter); conditions (antitrust waiting period, Castellan's consent as tax equity investor in R3 and R5, change-of-control consents under the R2 PPA and R5 vPPA); closing March 22, 2022, long-stop June 30, 2022; Fund II terms. D-013 arithmetic the narration may show: the W&I limit is 26.4% of the USD 446.3 million consideration (inside the 10% to 30% range of `t-ma-wi-insurance`); Lattimer's headline beat Bidder B by USD 14.5 million (3.4%). From Case Bible ledger (Exhibit 47.9): R-F04 (base EV against the USD 446.3 million price, value less price and breakeven, with low and high); R-F05 (sources and uses at closing: purchase price, transaction costs, fees and OID; opco term loan, holdco TLB and fund equity). Narration teaches how the acquisition financing was committed for the bid (forward reference to Ch 31 for the holdco TLB already taught, and to sec:63.6 for consent mechanics) and closes with the D-016 point that Lattimer later accepted modest overpayments on A2 and A3 for portfolio reasons (Ch 73). BF-u10-1 is closed by Annex TR R.1; the writer prints these terms and invents none.

## 47.11 Practitioner's notebook (outline)

Checklist (bid approval):
- Bid tariff restated on the banking case with no refinancing gain.
- Walk-away price approved in writing before the final round.
- EPC price validity and committed-finance letter cover the bid validity period.
- Winner's-curse shading documented for the expected number of bidders.
- Every assumption above the independent advisor's case is listed with its owner.

Checklist (acquisition):
- Price mechanism chosen with Framework 47.2; leakage definition and permitted-leakage list agreed.
- W&I scope matched to the diligence actually performed; known issues covered by specific indemnity or price.
- Consents mapped: lenders, offtaker, government, co-shareholders' pre-emption (execution in sec:63.6).
- Buy-side request list (Exhibit 47.10) issued with the first-round letter and closed out item by item before the binding offer.

Exhibit 47.10 (`exh:47.10`, appended label; caption "Buy-side acquisition due diligence request list for an operating project company (Illustrative)"). A numbered list of 30 items, each with a one-line reason, grouped under six heads. The writer sets it as an `exhibit` table (columns: No., Item requested, Why the buyer needs it, Workstream and chapter). It is the home exhibit that matter file 93's "due-diligence request lists" row cites for acquisitions (with Exhibits 48.10 to 48.13, 49.7 to 49.12 and 50.6). Items:
- Corporate and title: (1) share register, constitutional documents and all share charges (proves the seller owns unencumbered shares; lenders' share pledge must be released or consented); (2) shareholders' agreement and side letters (pre-emption, tag and drag, reserved matters, Ch 26); (3) group structure chart to ultimate owners (KYC and sanctions screening, sec:49.6); (4) board and shareholder minutes for three years (undisclosed commitments, disputes, related-party approvals).
- Price and leakage: (5) locked-box accounts or latest audited and management accounts with the auditor's management letter (the price is set on them; financial-statement breaches drive most W&I losses, `t-ma-wi-insurance`); (6) list of every payment to the seller's group since the accounts date (leakage); (7) intercompany and shareholder-loan agreements with balances (what is bought and how it is priced); (8) working-capital and net-debt definitions with monthly history for 24 months (completion-accounts or ticker calibration).
- Contracts and revenue: (9) offtake contracts, hedges and amendments with all notices and disputes (the revenue the bid assumes); (10) change-of-control and assignment clauses across all material contracts (consents as conditions, sec:63.6); (11) O&M, LTSA and asset-management agreements with fee schedules and termination rights (cost base and permitted leakage); (12) EPC warranty and defects-liability status, open claims and bonds (residual construction claims); (13) grid connection agreement and curtailment history (volume risk).
- Finance documents: (14) facility agreement, intercreditor, accounts and security documents (change-of-control default, prepayment cost, distribution conditions); (15) compliance certificates, DSCR history and any waivers or reservation-of-rights letters (lock-up risk; a missing waiver letter is a red flag); (16) hedge confirmations with current mark-to-market (equity bridge, ssec:46.4.1); (17) DSRA and other reserve balances and LC instruments (cash trapped or released at completion).
- Operations and technical: (18) availability, generation and outage data since COD against budget (P50 reset; Ch 48); (19) the lenders' IE monitoring reports and any reservation (what the lenders' engineer has already flagged); (20) major maintenance plan and spare-parts inventory (capex the price must absorb); (21) insurance schedule, claims history and broker's letter (insurability and claims, sec:49.2).
- Permits, E&S, tax and disputes: (22) permits and licenses with expiry and conditions (term against the hold period, Example 49.1 method); (23) land rights and registrations (security and continuity); (24) ESIA, ESAP status, grievance log and the IESC's latest report (E&S liabilities, Ch 50); (25) tax returns, audits, rulings and incentive certificates for five years (tax warranty and indemnity scope); (26) transfer-pricing documentation for related-party contracts (tax leakage, sec:49.4); (27) litigation, claims and regulatory correspondence (specific indemnities); (28) employee and pension liabilities, including transfer rules (cost and change-of-control payments); (29) data-room index and Q&A log (what was disclosed against the warranties, sec:85.5); (30) vendor due diligence reports and their reliance terms (what the buyer may rely on, ssec:48.1.1).

Red flags:
- The bid works only if the debt is refinanced within five years.
- Your forecast is the highest you have seen for the asset.
- The ticker exceeds the cash the business generates.
- A data room that excludes lender correspondence or waiver letters.
- A fund near the end of its investment period with uncalled commitments.

Rules of thumb and their limits:
- In a contracted renewables tender, 1 point of equity IRR moves the tariff by about 2% to 2.5% (Example 47.1); less where capex is a smaller share of tariff (gas plants with fuel pass-through).
- Buy-side W&I limits typically sit at 10% to 30% of EV, with retentions of 0.25% to 1% of EV for operational deals (Howden 2024 guide, `t-ma-wi-insurance`); the 2025 market was softer, so check current terms before quoting them.
- An 8% preferred return is "typical" in closed-end funds (Brookfield's 2025 10-K, `t-infra-fund-terms`); evergreen core vehicles use a lower hurdle (5% in the two US vehicles in the sheet) with a 12.5% performance fee. The limit: fee terms vary by manager and wrapper, and no single market standard is verified.

Common mistakes:
- Shading the bid for the curse after already using the lowest-cost assumptions everywhere else.
- Treating the bid bond as the cost of walking away from a won tender (the real cost is reputation and future qualification).
- Using completion accounts in a deal where the buyer cannot prepare reliable accounts at completion.
- Comparing fund gross IRRs with listed-vehicle total shareholder returns.

Questions experts ask:
- Who else is bidding, and what is their cost of capital?
- What assumption would have to be wrong for us to regret winning?
- What has the seller not put in the data room?
- How is the manager paid, and when does it need to sell?

## 47.12 Judgment drill

Situation: You lead Ras Hadd Power Partners' (fictional) bid for a 300 MWac solar IPP tender in Ad Dhahirah governorate, Oman (Illustrative; the state procurer is unnamed and the terms are not its actual RFP). Your committee-approved walk-away tariff is USD 17.40/MWh at a 9.0% equity IRR on the banking case with no refinancing. At 08:30 on Thursday, March 11, 2027, the deadline day, the arranger offers a 22-year soft mini-perm that would let you bid USD 16.90/MWh at the same IRR if a refinancing at 50 bps lower margin happens in year 6. Your CEO wants to win; two rivals are known to use soft mini-perms. Bids close at 15:00 Gulf Standard Time.

Reasoning outline: quantify what the refinancing assumption is worth (USD 0.50/MWh, which on Example 47.1's sensitivity of about USD 0.70/MWh per point is roughly 0.7 points of equity IRR that must come from a refinancing nobody has committed to) and what happens if the refinancing does not occur (margin step-ups and cash sweeps of the soft mini-perm, taught in Ch 36 and Ch 37, push the IRR below the walk-away basis); compare with the winner's-curse bias for three bidders; check whether the committee approval covers a change in financing basis; consider the reputational cost of winning and failing to close against losing. Expert answer: do not bid below the approved walk-away without committee re-approval; if time allows, seek a revised approval explicitly for the mini-perm structure with a downside case; otherwise bid at the approved tariff.

What would change the answer: a refinancing market with deep, repeated evidence for the asset class (moves toward accepting); an offtaker that allows refinancing gains without sharing (moves toward accepting); a tender that penalizes failure to close with exclusion (moves against); a fund near the end of its investment period (pressure to accept, which the drill names as the bias to resist).

## 47.13 Exercise set (16)

Tier 1 (4):
- 47.1: Explain why the winner's bias in Example 47.2 increases with the number of bidders.
- 47.2: State three reasons a seller prefers a locked box and two reasons a buyer resists it.
- 47.3: Distinguish a European from an American waterfall and say which favors LPs.
- 47.4: Name the bid variable in Case P and in Case T and explain how each shifts risk.

Tier 2 (8):
- 47.5: Rework Example 47.1 at a 1.25x DSCR on one-year P90 CFADS and an 85% gearing cap; find the tariff for a 10.0% equity IRR. Answer: USD 29.33/MWh; debt USD 83.72 million (gearing 71.3%; the DSCR still binds); equity USD 33.68 million; year-1 debt service USD 8.53 million. Common wrong answer: assuming the looser gearing cap binds and sizing debt at 85% of capex.
- 47.6: Compute the expected winner's bias for six bidders at 12% forecast uncertainty (Example 47.2's Bogotá toll road). Answer: E[max of six standard normals] 1.267, bias 15.2% of true value (against 19.0% at 15%).
- 47.7: Recompute Example 47.3 with a bid cost of USD 3.2 million and a flatter win curve (scale USD 1.50/MWh, midpoint unchanged at USD 39.00). Answers (tariff: P(win), expected NPV, USD million): 38.00: 0.661, (0.97); 39.00: 0.500, (0.04); 39.50: 0.417, 0.05; 40.00: 0.339, (0.06). Optimum USD 39.46/MWh with expected NPV USD 0.05 million: the bid is barely worth making, and the lesson is that a costly bid into a field with uncertain win odds can have no positive-value tariff at all.
- 47.8: Price Example 47.4's locked box with a ticker set as a daily cash amount of EUR 31,500 instead of 7.0% a year on the equity price, and compare. Answer: ticker EUR 4.76 million (151 days); price paid 73.6 + 4.76 − 3.4 = EUR 74.96 million, EUR 2.63 million more than at 7.0% and EUR 0.26 million more than completion accounts; the daily amount tracks the business's cash generation (EUR 5.2 million over the period, less working-capital movement) better than an interest-style rate.
- 47.9: Add 15% corporate income tax with straight-line tax depreciation of capex over 25 years to Example 47.1 (no tax holiday; taxable income stays positive in every year on both P50 and P90, so no loss carryforward arises; sculpt on one-year P90 CFADS after P90 cash tax) and re-solve the tariff for 10.0%. Answer: USD 30.55/MWh; debt USD 82.03 million (gearing 69.9%; DSCR binds); year-1 P50 CFADS after tax USD 11.99 million. Cross-reference ssec:7.11.4 for the tax rules.
- 47.10: Example 47.5's policy (limit EUR 18.63 million; aggregate retention EUR 0.93 million; no per-claim de minimis) meets two claims in sequence: a EUR 0.6 million accounts-warranty claim, then a EUR 21.0 million title claim. Compute the recoveries. Answer: first claim pays nil (it erodes the retention, leaving EUR 0.33 million); second pays min(21.0 − 0.33, 18.63) = EUR 18.63 million, the full limit; the buyer bears EUR 2.97 million of the EUR 21.6 million total loss. Note that a title warranty would normally sit under a higher fundamental-warranty limit or the seller's fundamental-warranty liability up to the price (Clause 47.2); with that cover, the EUR 2.04 million above the limit (21.0 − 0.33 − 18.63) is recoverable from the seller.
- 47.11: Draft a permitted-leakage clause for the Knockanore sale (Example 47.4) where the seller's affiliate Glenmara Asset Services receives a monthly management fee of EUR 61,800; give the seller-friendly and buyer-friendly versions and annotate. Model answer: the buyer-friendly version caps the fee at the contracted rate for the five months from January 31 to July 1, 2026 (EUR 309,000) and excludes any success or termination fee; the seller-friendly version permits "fees under the Asset Management Agreement in the ordinary course".
- 47.12: Recompute Example 47.7's waterfall with 20% carry over the same 8% preferred return and no catch-up; state the carry and the LP net IRR. Answers: carry USD 25.87 million (20% of distributions after LPs receive contributions plus the compounded preferred return); LP net IRR 9.07%; LP net MOIC 1.93x; against 15% carry with full catch-up (8.32%), the LPs gain 0.75 points. Common wrong answer: applying 20% to all profit, which is the full-catch-up result.

Tier 3 (4):
- 47.13: Case T: using T-F02 (including its extended gap rows), T-F21 and Annex TR T.2 and T.3, write the one-page note Partnerships Brannock should have produced in August 2014 on the gap between the winning contribution and the reference and Northgate bids, applying Framework 47.1 and Example 47.2. Model answer cites the 29.9% gap to the reference and 20.4% to Northgate (ledger), the traffic cases behind each bid (58.4 against 53.9 against Ridgeway's 52.6), the equity IRR at Pellow's central case (T-F21) against the 11.4% target, and recommends asking for the bidder's banking-case return before appointment.
- 47.14: Case P: using P-F06, P-F62 and the RFP evaluation inputs, show how a front-loaded capital charge would have scored under the 10.0% evaluation rate. Inputs (Illustrative two-profile comparison, ignoring indexation, stated): Profile A is the flat USD 14.36/kW-month for 25 years; Profile B is USD 16.90/kW-month in years 1 to 12 and a level charge X in years 13 to 25, chosen so that both profiles have the same present value at 10.0%. Answers: X = USD 6.71/kW-month; both profiles score the same levelized capital charge; to a bidder discounting at its 16.0% bid-model IRR, the PV of the capital charges per kW is USD 1,050.65 for Profile A and USD 1,126.48 for Profile B (7.2% more). Lesson: an evaluation rate below bidders' cost of capital rewards front-loading, which the government then pays for in the early, cash-rich years; the evaluation design is Ch 58's (ssec:58.2.4).
- 47.15: Case R: draft Lattimer's December 2, 2021 binding bid letter for A1 (headline locked-box price and ticker, locked-box date, conditions, financing certainty with the opco term loan and holdco TLB commitments, W&I terms, the Uri specific indemnity and escrow, exclusivity request), using Case Bible and Annex TR R.1 inputs only. Model answer reproduces the R.1 terms and states nothing the Bible does not.
- 47.16: Model task (macro-free): build a bid tariff solver sheet (inputs block, 25-year operations timeline, sculpted debt on one-year P90 CFADS with the gearing cap, equity cash flow, equity IRR) that reproduces Example 47.1, then solve the tariff with a one-variable data table or Goal Seek (manual, not a macro). Answer key: USD 29.46/MWh; debt USD 80.98 million; equity IRR check 10.00%; the Exhibit 47.1 sensitivities reproduced from the same sheet.

## 47.15 Glossary entries owned

Entries follow `bible/glossary-canon.md`. Removed by ruling: "levelized tariff" (home ssec:5.3.3, R-001). Renamed or split by ruling: "share purchase agreement" (R-084), "retention (W&I insurance)" and "capital commitment (fund)" (R-083). Added by ruling: "teaser" and "confidentiality agreement" (R-087). "Bid security" is not entered here (home ssec:58.2.3, R-086).

| Term | Abbreviation | Definition | Home section |
|---|---|---|---|
| bid variable | – | The single quantity on which a competitive tender ranks compliant bids, such as the tariff, the public contribution or the upfront concession fee. | 47.1.1 |
| committed-finance letter | – | A lender's letter submitted with a bid confirming that financing is approved on stated terms, subject only to listed conditions. | 47.1.4 |
| winner's curse | – | The tendency of the winning bidder in a common-value competition to be the one that most overestimated the asset's value. | 47.2.1 |
| bid shading | – | Lowering a bid below one's own unbiased value estimate to correct for the winner's curse. | 47.2.3 |
| walk-away price | – | The highest price (or lowest tariff) a bidder's committee has approved, beyond which the team must not bid. | 47.2.4 |
| information memorandum (sale) | IM | The seller's marketing document describing an asset for sale, issued to bidders after a confidentiality agreement. | 47.3.1 (one entry covers sale and financing IMs; Ch 55 cross-references, R-087) |
| non-binding offer | – | A first-round indicative bid in an auction, stating price and key assumptions without commitment. | 47.3.1 |
| binding offer | – | A final-round offer in an auction with a marked-up share purchase agreement and committed financing, capable of acceptance. | 47.3.1 |
| teaser | – | A short, non-confidential description of an opportunity circulated to gauge interest before a confidentiality agreement is signed. | 47.3.1 (R-087) |
| confidentiality agreement | NDA | An agreement under which a recipient of non-public information about an asset or financing undertakes to keep it confidential and to use it only for the stated purpose. | 47.3.1 (R-087) |
| share purchase agreement | – (never abbreviated) | The contract by which a seller transfers shares in a project company (or, as an asset purchase agreement, its assets) to a buyer, setting price, conditions, warranties, indemnities and limitations. | 47.3.3 (R-084) |
| disclosure letter | – | The seller's document that qualifies the warranties by disclosing known exceptions. | 47.3.3 |
| specific indemnity | – | A seller's promise to reimburse a known, identified risk in full, outside the warranty limits. | 47.3.3 |
| locked box | – | A price mechanism fixing the equity price on balance-sheet figures at a past date, with the buyer protected by a covenant against value leaving the company afterward. | 47.3.4 |
| leakage | – | Value transferred from the target to the seller or its affiliates after the locked-box date, such as dividends or fees, which the seller must repay unless permitted. | 47.3.4 |
| permitted leakage | – | Payments to the seller after the locked-box date that the share purchase agreement expressly allows and the price already reflects. | 47.3.4 |
| ticker | – | An amount added to a locked-box price for the period from the locked-box date to completion, compensating the seller for cash the business earns. | 47.3.4 |
| completion accounts | – | A price mechanism under which the final price is adjusted for net debt and working capital shown in accounts prepared at completion. | 47.3.4 |
| warranty and indemnity insurance | W&I | Insurance, usually bought by the buyer, that pays losses from breaches of the seller's warranties above a retention and up to a limit. | 47.3.5 |
| retention (W&I insurance) | – | The amount of loss the insured bears before a warranty and indemnity policy pays. | 47.3.5 (R-083) |
| deferred consideration | – | Part of the purchase price paid after completion, often conditional on a stated event. | 47.3.6 |
| earn-out | – | Deferred consideration whose amount depends on the asset's performance after completion. | 47.3.6 |
| concentration limit | – | A cap in a fund's or lender's rules on exposure to one asset, technology, market, counterparty or country. | 47.4.2 |
| limited partner | LP | An investor in a fund partnership whose liability is limited to its commitment. | 47.5.1 |
| general partner | GP | The manager of a fund partnership, which makes investment decisions and earns fees and carried interest. | 47.5.1 |
| capital commitment (fund) | – | The amount a limited partner agrees to provide to a fund when called. | 47.5.1 (R-083) |
| core, core-plus, value-add | – | Fund strategy labels ordered by rising risk and target return, from operating contracted assets to development and repositioning. | 47.5.1 |
| management fee | – | The GP's annual fee, charged on commitments during the investment period and usually on invested capital afterward. | 47.5.2 |
| preferred return | – | The compound annual return LPs must receive before the GP earns carried interest. | 47.5.2 |
| carried interest | carry | A fund general partner's share of fund profits above the preferred return. | 47.5.2 (fund sense, R-083) |
| catch-up | – | The waterfall tier that pays the GP a high share of distributions after the preferred return until it has received its full carry percentage of profit. | 47.5.2 |
| European waterfall | – | A whole-fund distribution waterfall in which carry is paid only after LPs recover all contributions and the preferred return. | 47.5.2 |
| American waterfall | – | A deal-by-deal waterfall in which carry is paid on each realized investment, usually with a clawback. | 47.5.2 |
| gross IRR | – | A fund's return on its investments before fees and carried interest. | 47.5.3 |
| net IRR | – | The return to LPs after fees, expenses and carried interest. | 47.5.3 |
| net asset value | NAV | The fund's estimate of the fair value of its investments less liabilities at a reporting date. | 47.5.3 |

## 48.2 Purpose and capabilities

Purpose. Every number in a project's base case comes from somebody's report: capex and schedule from the independent engineer, output from the yield consultant, reserves from the competent person, prices from the market advisor, traffic from the traffic consultant. Lenders size debt on these reports and investors bid on them, yet few people who rely on them know how to read them. This chapter opens Part IX by setting out how a diligence program is scoped and who relies on whom, then teaches the reader to read and challenge the technical, resource, market and traffic reports: what the advisor does, how to scope the work, how to read the report, and where reports have been wrong. The reader leaves able to direct these advisors and to say, with numbers, where a report's conclusion is weaker than it looks.

What you will be able to do:

- Scope a diligence program for a project financing, write an advisor's scope letter and request list, and challenge any advisor's report with a fixed protocol (Capability 7).
- Test an independent engineer's capex, contingency and schedule conclusions with simple probabilistic arithmetic (Capabilities 7, 2).
- Rework an energy yield, reserves, price-curve or traffic forecast for a changed assumption and show the effect on revenue and debt capacity (Capabilities 7, 5).
- Explain why traffic and demand forecasts in competitive bids are biased upward and how lenders protect themselves (Capabilities 7, 9, 15).
