## 48.4 Locked table of contents

Opening (no heading). `\section*{What you will be able to do}`.

**48.1 How diligence is organized and who relies on whom** (`sec:48.1`)

- 48.1.1 Advisors, reliance, and duty of care (`ssec:48.1.1`). Sponsor-commissioned reports with lender reliance letters, lender-appointed advisors, vendor due diligence in sales; liability caps (multiples of fees) and why lenders still rely; independence and conflicts. Four-lens prose: sponsor pays and wants speed; lenders need reliance; host government in PPPs runs its own studies; contractors see the IE as the lenders' eyes during construction.
- 48.1.2 Scoping a diligence program (`ssec:48.1.2`). Framework 48.1 (the diligence scope matrix): workstreams by row (technical, resource, market, traffic, legal, insurance, model, tax, E&S, integrity, counterparty), columns for commissioner, reliance, timing relative to bid, mandate, credit approval and financial close, key questions, and bring-down. Exhibit 48.1 fills it for an Illustrative onshore wind financing (Kozani Ridge Wind, a 112 MW project in Western Macedonia, Greece, financed by a Greek and a German bank in 2026; fictional). Then the scope letter as the instrument that turns a matrix row into a mandate: scope and exclusions, deliverables and their dates against the close calendar (bid, mandate, credit approval, signing, financial close, bring-down), reliance and its cap, independence statement, fee and change mechanism; the IE walkthrough (48.6) writes one. Forward reference to Ch 49 and Ch 50 for the other workstreams and to sec:87.1 for running advisors day to day.
- 48.1.3 The report challenge protocol (`ssec:48.1.3`). Framework 48.2 introduced here and applied in every later section and in Ch 49 and Ch 50.

**48.2 The independent engineer** (`sec:48.2`)

- 48.2.1 What the independent engineer reviews (`ssec:48.2.1`). Scope at financing, standard report structure, the difference between a "fatal flaw" review and a full due diligence report; IE versus owner's engineer.
- 48.2.2 Technology risk and equipment bankability (`ssec:48.2.2`). Proven technology tests (fleet hours, reference plants, scale-up factor), applying the first-of-a-kind and nth-of-a-kind distinction of ssec:14.5.1; Case P anchor from Annex P 4.1: Gwen found the Bergmark BT-9F mature, with 41 units in commercial operation in 2018, a fleet leader at about 62,000 equivalent operating hours and no open serial defect; real case `ivanpah` (first commercial-scale US solar tower plant, up to USD 1.6 billion FFB loan with DOE guarantee in April 2011, units struggled to meet guaranteed energy production, PG&E forbearance in December 2015 and PPA amendments in 2017): what an IE can and cannot certify about a first-of-a-kind plant.
- 48.2.3 Testing the construction budget and contingency (`ssec:48.2.3`). Example 48.1 (Illustrative 96 MW run-of-river hydro in Gandaki Province, Nepal, at credit approval in 2026): three-point estimates by package (the three-point estimate is defined in ssec:15.3.2), P80 and P90, and the effect of correlated geology. Equation 48.1 (PERT mean and standard deviation). Exhibit 48.2.
- 48.2.4 Testing the schedule and the probability of delay (`ssec:48.2.4`). Critical path, float, interface risks, the IE's delay distribution; Example 48.2 (Illustrative 281,000 m³/day RO desalination plant at Barka, Oman, under construction in 2026): expected delay cost against delay LDs. Exhibit 48.3. Case P anchor (Annex P 4.1): Gwen called the 33-month schedule "achievable but tight", with 38 days of float on a critical path through steam turbine erection and HRSG pressure parts.
- 48.2.5 Reviewing operating assumptions (`ssec:48.2.5`). Availability profiles against outage plans, degradation, heat rate, opex benchmarks, lifecycle and major maintenance timing; what an IE benchmarks against and the limits of benchmark data.
- 48.2.6 The IE after financial close (`ssec:48.2.6`). Short: monitoring, drawdown certificates (earned value against planned value, both spelled out, taught at ssec:61.4.2), completion certification and operations reporting, with forward references to Ch 61 and Ch 62.

**48.3 Resource and reserves diligence** (`sec:48.3`)

- 48.3.1 Wind and solar energy yield (`ssec:48.3.1`). Reviewing an energy yield assessment (defined in sec:9.10): reading the loss chain and the uncertainty stack; Example 48.3 (Illustrative 160 MW wind farm in Rio Grande do Norte, Brazil, 2026 financing); Equation 48.2. Challenging wake, curtailment and long-term correction. Exhibit 48.4. Cross-references eq:9.5 for combining uncertainties, ssec:9.6.2 for ten-year P90 and Ch 45 for modeling.
- 48.3.2 Hydrology and geothermal resource (`ssec:48.3.2`). Flow records, climate trend adjustment, firm versus average energy; geothermal well productivity and decline, make-up drilling. Real case `sarulla` (330 MW, Indonesia; financial close May 16, 2014 with an approximately USD 1.17 billion limited-recourse loan package; generation fell because of wellfield problems and equipment failures; 2022 remediation framework with lenders; make-up drilling from 2026): resource risk persists into operations, so resource diligence must specify reserves for make-up wells.
- 48.3.3 Mineral and hydrocarbon reserves (`ssec:48.3.3`). Applies the classifications of ssec:12.1.1 and ssec:12.3.1 (not re-taught) to diligence: the lender's checklist from `t-reserves-codes` (which code was used: PRMS, JORC, NI 43-101 or S-K 1300; who the competent or qualified person is and whether independent; what price deck was used; what study stage and its accuracy band; whether inferred resources have crept into the economics); cut-off grade and price assumptions; reserve tail (defined at ssec:45.6.3) as a lender test; Example 48.4 (Illustrative copper mine in Arequipa region, Peru, 2026 financing). Equation 48.3. Cross-reference Ch 75 (the RBL tail test, ssec:75.3.2) and Ch 78 (mining sizing, ssec:78.7.2).
- 48.3.4 Fuel and feedstock supply adequacy (`ssec:48.3.4`). Matching certified reserves and deliverability to the supply contract's quantity and term. Case P link: inputs from Annex P 1.7.4 (Sombé West certified 2P reserves of 1,140 bcf in 2017 at 1,040 Btu/scf; Bélanou GSA 72,400 MMBtu a day for 22 years; SEKA's existing contract 38,000 MMBtu a day from 2019 to 2038) and, from Case Bible ledger, P-F61 (2P reserves against the Bélanou GSA and SEKA contract quantities, in bcf, and the coverage ratio). One sentence on Gwen's reservation R3 (first production not yet achieved in May 2018; Annex P 4.1).

**48.4 Market and price-curve diligence** (`sec:48.4`)

- 48.4.1 How a market advisor builds a price curve (`ssec:48.4.1`). Fundamental dispatch models, demand and supply build-out scenarios, fuel and carbon inputs, policy assumptions; base, low and high cases; dating and vintage of curves.
- 48.4.2 Capture prices, cannibalization, and basis in the curve (`ssec:48.4.2`). How the advisor converts a baseload curve into an asset's realized price; cross-reference Ch 11 and Ch 20; the questions to ask about the capture-rate decline.
- 48.4.3 Comparing two advisors' curves (`ssec:48.4.3`). Example 48.5 (Illustrative 150 MW solar plant in Badajoz, Extremadura, Spain, refinancing in 2026): merchant-tail revenue and value under two curves. Exhibit 48.5.
- 48.4.4 Commodity markets beyond power (`ssec:48.4.4`). LNG, metals and petrochemical price decks used in reserve and offtake diligence; lenders' price decks versus sponsors'; short.
- 48.4.5 Which curve the lenders size on (`ssec:48.4.5`). Low case for merchant tails, the role of the banking case, and how the market advisor's low case enters sizing; cross-reference Ch 36.

**48.5 Traffic and demand studies** (`sec:48.5`)

- 48.5.1 How a traffic study is built (`ssec:48.5.1`). Four-step model in outline (trip generation, distribution, mode choice, assignment), toll choice, calibration to counts; investment-grade versus planning-grade studies; cross-reference Ch 12 and Ch 45.
- 48.5.2 Value of time, elasticity, and diversion (`ssec:48.5.2`). Uses the terms of ssec:12.4.1 and ssec:12.4.2 without re-defining them. Example 48.6 (Illustrative 31 km toll bypass around Georgetown, Williamson County, Texas, 2026 investment-grade study): logit diversion and the effect of a sponsor value of time 22% above the survey's. Equation 48.4. Exhibit 48.6 (diversion curve chart).
- 48.5.3 Ramp-up and land-use assumptions (`ssec:48.5.3`). Ramp-up as a separate assumption from mature traffic; land-use and housing timetables as the most common hidden driver; how lenders haircut sponsor forecasts. The evidence from `t-traffic-forecast-accuracy`: in Standard & Poor's 2002 to 2005 traffic risk studies (anonymized samples of 32 to 104 toll projects), actual year-1 traffic averaged about 0.73 to 0.77 of the forecast at financial close with a standard deviation of about 0.26, and the 2005 update found the mean still 0.77 to 0.80 in years 2 to 5; Bain (2009) put the systematic optimism bias of toll-road forecasts at about 20% on top of the random error that toll-free forecasts carry. The lesson from S&P: adjust the base case for bias before running sensitivities (error and bias are different problems), and size liquidity for large, persistent shortfalls. Cite the S&P work as rating-agency research and Bain (2009) as the peer-reviewed synthesis (sheet's "Do not state").
- 48.5.4 Sydney's Cross City and Lane Cove tunnels and the winning forecast (`ssec:48.5.4`). Real case section from `sydney-tunnels`: Exhibit 48.7 compares the winning bidder's CCT forecast (about 70,000 at opening; 87,088 by June 2006) with the RTA's (82,347), lower rival forecasts, and actual traffic under 25,000 at full toll; receivers December 27, 2006; LCT receivership January 19, 2010; RTA's 2010 review conceded the models were built for peak-hour design, not toll revenue, and ignored ramp-up. Short Dulles Greenway paragraph (`dulles-greenway`: opened September 29, 1995 at a USD 1.75 toll; traffic short of projections because the corridor developed slowly; toll cut to USD 1.00 in March 1996; default almost immediately). Lessons stated as mechanisms.
- 48.5.5 Demand studies beyond roads (`ssec:48.5.5`). Airports, ports, rail patronage, data-center occupancy: what a demand study rests on and the equivalent of the value-of-time question in each; short.

**48.6 Walkthrough: reading an independent engineer's report** (`sec:48.6`).

**48.7 Case P: Gwen Treharne's report and the one-in-eight call** (`sec:48.7`).

**48.8 Case T: two traffic studies for one road** (`sec:48.8`).

**48.9 Practitioner's notebook** (`sec:48.9`). Includes Exhibits 48.10 to 48.13, the request lists for the technical, resource and reserves, market, and traffic and demand workstreams (see 48.11 below). **48.10 Judgment drill** (`sec:48.10`).

**48.11 Engineering reports measure physical risk, and documents hide the rest** (`sec:48.11`). Close. Crystallizes: every base-case input traces to a report; the challenge protocol asks the same questions of each; forecasts in competitive bids skew high. Last paragraph poses the next problem: a perfect engineering report will not save a project whose security is unenforceable, whose insurance does not respond, whose model misstates tax, or whose sponsor turns out to be sanctioned (Ch 49).

**48.12 Exercises** (`sec:48.12`). **48.13 Solutions to exercises** (`sec:48.13`). `\section*{Sources}`.

## 48.5 Opening

Real deal moment from `sydney-tunnels`: Sydney's 2.1 km Cross City Tunnel opened on August 28, 2005, ahead of schedule, on a winning bidder's forecast of about 70,000 vehicles a day at opening rising to 87,088 by June 2006; at full toll it carried fewer than 25,000, and receivers were appointed on December 27, 2006. The NSW Auditor-General later found that the winning bid's projections were higher than every other projection and should have been scrutinized. Open on the gap between 87,088 and 25,000 and on the fact that every lender had a traffic report; the chapter teaches what the reports said, what they rested on, and how to read the next one.

## 48.6 Worked examples

**Example 48.1 Testing a hydro contingency with three-point estimates (Illustrative)** (`ex:48.1`, in 48.2.3)

- Scenario: Rato Pakha Hydropower Ltd (fictional), a 96 MW run-of-river hydropower plant in Gandaki Province, Nepal, with a 5.8 km headrace tunnel, sponsored by a Nepali developer and an Indian IPP (both fictional) and financed by two DFIs in USD; the lenders' IE, Annapurna Technical Advisers (fictional), reviews the budget before credit approval in September 2026. Sponsor budget USD 185.8 million (sum of most-likely estimates) plus contingency USD 11.7 million (total USD 197.5 million). The IE builds three-point estimates (USD million):

| Package | Low | Most likely | High | PERT mean | PERT sd |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Headworks and civil works | 58.2 | 63.9 | 79.4 | 65.53 | 3.53 |
| Headrace tunnel (5.8 km) | 41.6 | 47.3 | 71.8 | 50.43 | 5.03 |
| Powerhouse and E&M equipment | 44.7 | 46.1 | 50.3 | 46.57 | 0.93 |
| Transmission line (38 km, 132 kV) | 11.4 | 12.6 | 16.9 | 13.12 | 0.92 |
| Owner's costs and development | 14.8 | 15.9 | 18.2 | 16.10 | 0.57 |

- Equation 48.1 (`eq:48.1`): PERT mean $= (a + 4m + b)/6$, standard deviation $= (b - a)/6$; total standard deviation from the sum of variances (independent) or with a correlation term.
- Key results: expected cost USD 191.75 million; independent case: standard deviation USD 6.31 million, P80 USD 197.06 million, P90 USD 199.84 million; sponsor budget USD 197.5 million sits at about P82 (probability 0.819 of not exceeding); contingency needed for P80 USD 11.26 million. With a correlation of 0.6 between headworks and tunnel (same geology): standard deviation USD 7.82 million, P80 USD 198.33 million, probability of staying within budget 0.769.
- Teaching points: the most-likely sum understates expected cost by USD 5.95 million because the distributions are skewed; independence understates risk where packages share a cause; the IE's contingency opinion should state its confidence level and correlation assumption.

**Example 48.2 What a delay distribution costs (Illustrative)** (`ex:48.2`, in 48.2.4)

- Scenario: Barka Coastal Water Company SAOC (fictional), a 281,000 m³/day reverse-osmosis desalination plant at Barka, Oman, selling water to the state single buyer (unnamed) under a 20-year water purchase agreement, under construction in August 2026 with a Spanish EPC contractor (fictional); the lenders' IE assigns probabilities to delay bands beyond the guaranteed completion date. Contract terms are Illustrative.
- Inputs: delay bands and probabilities (band midpoint in days): 0 to 30 days, 0.42 (15); 31 to 90, 0.30 (60.5); 91 to 180, 0.16 (135.5); 181 to 270, 0.08 (225.5); more than 270, 0.04 (330 assumed). Cost to the project company of each day of delay USD 212,400 (interest, fixed costs, lost margin net of savings). EPC delay LDs USD 185,000 a day, capped at 120 days.
- Key results: probability of delay above 180 days 0.12 (about one in eight); expected delay 77.4 days; expected delay cost USD 16.43 million; expected LD recovery USD 10.74 million; expected uncovered cost USD 5.69 million; by band, the uncovered cost is USD 0.41 million (15 days), 1.66 (60.5), 6.58 (135.5), 25.70 (225.5), 47.89 (330).
- Teaching points: LDs cover the likely delays; the tail is uncovered and is what the lenders' contingency, DSU insurance (Ch 27) and sponsor support (Ch 26) must answer; the IE's probability estimate drives the size of each.

**Example 48.3 Challenging a wind yield report (Illustrative)** (`ex:48.3`, in 48.3.1)

- Scenario: Serra dos Ventos Energia S.A. (fictional), a 160 MW wind farm in Rio Grande do Norte, Brazil, seeking a 2026 financing from a Brazilian development bank and two commercial banks. Sponsor's yield consultant: gross output 612.4 GWh a year; the lenders appoint their own consultant (fictional, unnamed).
- Inputs: sponsor losses (multiplicative): wake 8.3%, availability 3.0%, electrical 2.1%, environmental 1.2%, curtailment 2.5%, performance 1.0%. Lenders' consultant revises wake to 10.6% and curtailment to 4.0%. Uncertainties (independent, % of net): wind measurement 4.5, long-term correction 3.0, vertical extrapolation 3.5, wake model 3.0, other 2.0, interannual variability 6.0 (one-year; divided by the square root of 10 for ten-year).
- Equation 48.2 (`eq:48.2`): net $= \text{gross} \times \prod_i (1 - L_i)$.
- Key results: sponsor net P50 508.58 GWh (total loss 16.95%); lenders' net P50 488.19 GWh (20.28%), 4.01% lower; combined uncertainty 9.51% one-year, 7.62% ten-year; P90 one-year: sponsor 446.57 GWh, lender 428.67; P90 ten-year: 458.89 and 440.50.
- Teaching points: two loss lines account for a 4% revenue cut that flows almost one for one into P90 debt capacity; ask for the wake model's validation against operating neighbors and for curtailment grounded in grid studies.

**Example 48.4 Reserve life against loan tenor (Illustrative)** (`ex:48.4`, in 48.3.3)

- Scenario: Minera Loma Cobrecita S.A.C. (fictional), an open-pit copper mine in Arequipa region, southern Peru, financed in 2026 with a nine-year amortizing loan from a club of four banks and an ECA; competent person's report under JORC (2012) by an independent consultant (fictional).
- Inputs: proved and probable ore reserves 188.2 Mt at 0.52% copper (changed from 186.4 in round 2, D-134); mill throughput 14.0 Mtpa; metallurgical recovery 88.5%. Competent person's sensitivity: at a higher cut-off grade the reserve falls to 161.9 Mt.
- Equation 48.3 (`eq:48.3`, caption "Reserve tail against final maturity"): reserve tail $= (\text{reserve life} - \text{loan life})/\text{reserve life}$, which equals the canon definition at ssec:45.6.3 (reserves remaining at final maturity as a share of original reserves) when throughput is constant; the text says so and cites ssec:45.6.3.
- Key results (Python, round 2): reserve life 13.44 years; contained copper 0.979 Mt; recovered copper 866.1 kt, 64.43 kt a year; reserve tail at a 9-year final maturity 33.1% (at 10 years 25.6%, at 11 years 18.2%); revised reserve 161.9 Mt gives life 11.56 years and tail 22.2%.
- Teaching points: lenders require a tail (indicative minimum in the 25% to 35% range, D-011, to be confirmed in Ch 78 and its fact sheet) so that reserve downgrades and price falls do not leave the loan unpaid when the ore runs out; the tail is only as good as the cut-off price behind the reserve.

**Example 48.5 Two price curves for one merchant tail (Illustrative)** (`ex:48.5`, in 48.4.3)

- Scenario: Planta Solar Guadiana S.L. (fictional), a 150 MW solar plant near Badajoz, Extremadura, Spain, refinancing in 2026, with a corporate PPA to year 10 and merchant sales in years 11 to 25; the lenders commission two market advisors (Advisor A and Advisor B, fictional) to compare curves; P50 output 287.6 GWh a year (degradation ignored, stated).
- Inputs (EUR, nominal): Advisor A baseload price EUR 61.40/MWh in year 11 rising 1.5% a year, capture rate 0.78 falling 1.0 point a year to a floor of 0.62. Advisor B baseload EUR 54.70/MWh rising 1.0%, capture 0.71 falling 1.5 points a year to a floor of 0.55. Discount rate 9.0% (merchant bucket).
- Key results: year-11 merchant revenue EUR 13.77 million (A) and EUR 11.17 million (B); PV at today of years 11 to 25 merchant revenue EUR 47.23 million (A) and EUR 35.67 million (B), a difference of EUR 11.56 million (B is 24.5% lower).
- Teaching points: the capture-rate path does more work than the baseload level; ask each advisor for its build-out assumption for solar in the zone, which drives cannibalization.

**Example 48.6 Value of time and diversion (Illustrative)** (`ex:48.6`, in 48.5.2)

- Scenario: the San Gabriel Bypass (fictional), a 31 km toll bypass around Georgetown, Williamson County, Texas, studied in 2026 for a private concessionaire, Lone Star Mobility Partners (fictional); for a typical car trip the bypass saves 14 minutes at a toll of USD 3.60.
- Inputs: binary logit diversion: share $= 1/(1 + e^{-\theta(\mathrm{VOT} \times \Delta t/60 - \text{toll})})$ with $\theta = 0.85$ per USD; sponsor's value of time USD 21.40 per hour; a revealed-preference survey finds USD 17.54 (21.40/1.22), so the sponsor's value is 22% above the survey's; the example rounds the survey value to USD 17.50.
- Equation 48.4 (`eq:48.4`).
- Key results: value of time saved USD 4.99 (sponsor) and USD 4.08 (survey); generalized-cost advantage USD 1.39 and USD 0.48; diversion share 76.6% and 60.1%, a 21.5% fall in toll-road traffic from one parameter.
- Teaching points: the value of time is the single most sensitive and least observable input in a toll forecast; demand for a forecast with the higher value means demand for a higher bid; the Case T installment links to the Case Bible's statement that Pellow used a car value of time 22% above what a later survey found (Annex TR T.3: ARD 22.45 against 18.40 per vehicle-hour in 2014 prices). Note for the writer: "22% above the survey" and "22% less" are different numbers (a value 22% below 21.40 would be 16.69); print "22% above".

## 48.7 Walkthroughs

**Walkthrough: reading an independent engineer's report** (Section 48.6). Artifact: an Illustrative IE report for Aboadze Bay Power (fictional), a 486 MW combined-cycle plant in Ghana's Western Region burning domestic gas, prepared in March 2026 by a European engineering firm (fictional) for a lender group led by a French bank and a DFI, walked page by page with a marked-up extract, preceded by the scope letter that commissioned it. Steps:

0. Write the scope letter first (capabilities review fix): the IE's scope (technology, design, permits interface, EPC and O&M review, schedule, budget and contingency, operating assumptions, model input reconciliation), named exclusions, deliverables with dates mapped to the close calendar (fatal-flaw memo at mandate, draft report two weeks before credit approval, final report at signing, bring-down letter at financial close), reliance to the lenders and their successors with a liability cap of three times fees, independence statement (no owner's-engineer role for the sponsor), fee with a change mechanism, and post-close monitoring as an option. The chapter prints a one-page model letter as an exhibit-free annotated list.
1. Scope and reliance page: who commissioned, who may rely, liability cap, information relied on and its dates; check it against the scope letter.
2. Executive summary: separate findings from recommendations; list every "subject to" phrase.
3. Technology section: reference plants, fleet hours, any scale-up; equipment supplier's track record.
4. Site, permits and interfaces: grid, fuel, water, access; who carries interface risk.
5. EPC contract review: scope gaps, performance guarantees against the PPA (link to Ch 22), LD adequacy.
6. Schedule: critical path, float, delay probability; compare with Example 48.2's method.
7. Budget and contingency: method and confidence level (Example 48.1).
8. Operating assumptions: availability, degradation, opex, lifecycle against the model.
9. Model input reconciliation table: every technical input in the base case with the IE's view.
10. Reservations and outstanding items: what the IE could not verify.
11. Write the five questions to put to the IE before credit approval (model answers).

## 48.8 Frameworks

- **Framework 48.1: The diligence scope matrix** (`fw:dd-scope-matrix`), original. Purpose: plan every workstream of a financing's diligence. Rows: technical, resource, market, traffic or demand, legal and regulatory, insurance, model audit, tax and accounting, E&S, counterparty credit, KYC and integrity. Columns: who commissions; who relies and how; scope questions; key outputs that feed the model and documents; timing (bid, mandate, credit approval, signing, financial close, bring-down); cost and cap. Demonstrated in Exhibit 48.1; Ch 49 and Ch 50 cross-reference it.
- **Framework 48.2: The report challenge protocol** (`fw:report-challenge`), original. Purpose: challenge any advisor's report the same way. Steps: (1) scope and reliance: what was the advisor asked and who may rely; (2) independence: who paid and what else the advisor does for that client; (3) inputs: data sources, dates and gaps; (4) method: standard or bespoke, calibrated against what; (5) the three assumptions that move the answer most and the advisor's sensitivity on each; (6) comparison with an independent view or a reference class; (7) reservations: what the advisor says it did not check; (8) the downside the report supports, stated as a number. Applied throughout Ch 48 and in Ch 49 and Ch 50.
- Established methods credited: PERT three-point estimating (US Navy, 1958); four-step transport model (urban transport planning practice).

## 48.9 Real cases

| Slug | Use | Lesson |
|---|---|---|
| `sydney-tunnels` | Opening; 48.5.4 with Exhibit 48.7 | The winning forecast was the outlier; a model built for peak-hour design was used for toll revenue; forecaster liability is a weak backstop |
| `dulles-greenway` | 48.5.3, 48.5.4 | Ramp-up depends on land development; a toll cut does not restore demand in a corridor that has not grown |
| `ivanpah` | 48.2.2 | First-of-a-kind technology: an IE can review the design but cannot certify performance with no fleet history; performance shortfall flows to the offtaker relationship and to the guarantor |
| `sarulla` | 48.3.2 | Resource risk persists into operations; diligence must size make-up drilling and set reserves |
| `eurotunnel` (optional, one sentence) | 48.5.5 | Forecasts overestimated the total cross-Channel market and ignored ferry price response |

Facts strictly from the fact sheets and their "do not state" lists (for example, never state a single "90,000 vehicles a day" CCT forecast; never state LCT forecasts of 100,000 or 187,700).

Also used: `t-traffic-forecast-accuracy` (48.5.3, notebook, Exhibit 48.13; S&P 2002 to 2005 year-1 ratios, Bain 2009; observe its "Do not state": no named roads in the S&P sample, no fixed ramp-up duration, Flyvbjerg's road results are not toll-road evidence) and `t-reserves-codes` (48.3.3, Exhibit 48.11; the codes and the lender's checklist; do not state that a new JORC code or a replacement NI 43-101 is in force). All fact sheets this chapter needs are delivered.

## 48.10 Running-case installments

**Section 48.7 Case P: Gwen Treharne's report and the one-in-eight call.** Story date May 2018 (IE report dated May 30, 2018, for the credit approval process before the July 17, 2018 financial close; Castellan's credit committee met May 22, 2018 on the draft). Characters: Gwen Treharne (IE, Calder Hartmann Engineering; engaged by Castellan in February 2017 for a pre-mandate technical review that rolled into the lenders' IE engagement after the June 19, 2017 mandate, Annex P 3.3); Pieter van Wijngaarden (lead arranger) in the scene. Scene (Castellan Bank, London, mid-May 2018, on the draft report): Pieter presses Gwen on the delay risk ("Fine. And the day it goes wrong?", his habit, once); Gwen puts odds on it in her habitual form ("I would put that at ..."), here one in four for a delay longer than three months and one in eight for longer than six months (Annex P 4.1); she words reservation R1 on the grid interface at SEKA's substation. Narration walks the report with Framework 48.2, using Annex P 4.1 as the report's content (Exhibit 48.9, `exh:48.9`, "Case P technical inputs and the IE's findings \casep"):
- Technology: Bergmark BT-9F mature (41 units in commercial operation, fleet leader about 62,000 EOH, no open serial defect).
- Schedule: 33 months to April 30, 2021 "achievable but tight", critical path through steam turbine erection and HRSG pressure parts, 38 days of float; delay LDs USD 247,300 a day capped at 18% (Case Bible input).
- Contingency: USD 38.40 million judged adequate at about the 75th percentile of her risk-based estimate on a budget of USD 710.99 million; she recommends the standby facility and contingent equity as the layer above it (link to Example 48.1's method).
- Availability: the profile (93.8, 92.9, 93.8, 90.6, 93.8, 92.9, 93.8, 88.7) reasonable, the 93.8% peak years at the upper end of fleet experience; she would bank a point lower.
- Heat rate: the 1.0% headroom between the PPA's 6,323 kJ/kWh and the EPC guarantee's 6,261 kJ/kWh is reasonable at COD, but assumed degradation (0.12% a year plus 0.8% recoverable) outruns the PPA's 0.10% a year allowance; from Case Bible ledger: P-F47 (fuel margin from heat-rate headroom, OY1 to OY25), which turns negative in the later years, shown as a line in the exhibit.
- O&M and LTSA: fixed fee and staffing reasonable, localization schedule ambitious; LTSA covers gas turbines only; out-of-LTSA overhauls and the MMRA adequate.
- Gas supply: reservation R3 (Sombé West first production not yet achieved) with P-F61 (reserve coverage) from 48.3.4.
- Reservations R1 (grid interface: SEKA's substation contract awarded only in June 2018, no protection coordination study; she recommends a joint study and agreed switching procedures), R2 (soft marine clays; enhanced concrete QA), R4 (the 7 °C cooling-water discharge limit in the hottest months), R5 (mortgage registration and corridor RAP payments open; link forward to Ch 49 and Ch 50).
One time-jump sentence, with no narration of the events: Chapter 61 tells how the actual delay compared with her estimate and what became of reservations R1 and R2. Figures: Case Bible inputs (Case Bible 1.2 to 1.4 and Annex P 4.1) plus P-F47 and P-F61. BF-u10-4 is closed by Annex P 4.1.

**Section 48.8 Case T: two traffic studies for one road.** Story date 2014 (bid and lender diligence), with a stated time jump to actuals 2019 to 2026. Characters: Sasha Hrytsenko (Pellow); Elspeth Varga (director, Ridgeway Traffic Consultants, author of the 2014 banking case; Annex TR T.19; D-119; habit "Which year's survey is that?" at most once; where wrong: she kept Pellow's Coldwater Plains housing timetable and heavy-vehicle share unchecked), who meets Sasha only through their reports; Rhys Tanaka-Bell (Calder Hartmann, Port Ellery, leading the lenders' bid-stage technical due diligence, Annex TR T.18) may appear in the scene for two lines with his habit "Show me the gantry counts" (once). Scene (Pellow's offices, Port Ellery, July 2014): Sasha explains to the bid team why his "sensitivity range" has a high, a central and a low value of time; he talks elasticities (habit, once). Narration: Exhibit 48.8 (`exh:48.8`) compares Pellow (sponsor base: mature 58.4 thousand trips a day in 2019; ramp-up 0.80, 0.90, 0.96, 1.00; growth 3.4% to 2030, 2.3% to 2040, 1.2% after) and Ridgeway (banking: 52.6; 0.72, 0.84, 0.93, 0.98, 1.00; 2.9%, 2.0%, 1.0%) and the downside (46.7), all Case Bible inputs, with Pellow's three value-of-time cases (car VoT ARD 17.10, 19.80 and 22.45 per vehicle-hour in 2014 prices, giving mature 2019 traffic of 51.2, 54.6 and 58.4; Annex TR T.3) and Northgate's own forecast basis (53.9; Annex TR T.2) as reference lines. From Case Bible ledger: T-F04 (average daily trips, Pellow, Ridgeway, downside and actual, 2019 to 2026, plus Northgate's comparison line); T-F18 (actual traffic as a ratio of each case, 2019 to 2026); T-F19 (the 2019 to 2022 shortfall against Pellow by cause, in thousand trips a day and ARD million). Then the causes stated in the Case Bible and Annex TR T.17 (Coldwater Plains housing completions about four years late; car value of time 22% above the 2021 revealed-preference survey; heavy vehicles avoiding the road under the multiplier for trips under 30 km; deferred SR 14 traffic calming; COVID-19 from 2020), each tied to the section of Ch 48 that would have caught it (48.5.2 for value of time, 48.5.3 for land use and ramp-up, Framework 48.2 step 6 for the reference class). The narration sets T-F18's 2019 ratio against the S&P year-1 mean of about 0.73 to 0.77 (`t-traffic-forecast-accuracy`) without calling either a forecast of the other. Forward reference to Ch 79 for the ramp-up story and Ch 64 for the restructuring.

## 48.11 Practitioner's notebook (outline)

Checklist (before credit approval):
- Every technical, resource, market and traffic input in the banking case traces to a named report, page and date.
- Each report carries a reliance letter to the lenders with a stated cap.
- The IE has reconciled the model's technical inputs (Walkthrough step 9).
- The yield, curve or traffic report gives the lenders' sizing case explicitly, not only P50.
- Bring-down letters are scheduled for financial close.
- Each workstream's request list (Exhibits 48.10 to 48.13) was issued with the scope letter and every item is closed, waived with a reason, or carried into the report's reservations.

Request lists (home exhibits cited by matter 93's "due-diligence request lists" row; appended labels so no existing label moves). Each is an `exhibit` table with columns No., Item requested, Why it is needed, Feeds (model input, document, or report section). Items are written for a lenders' advisor on a project financing; the writer may shorten reasons to one line but keeps every item.

Exhibit 48.10 (`exh:48.10`, "Technical due diligence request list for the independent engineer (Illustrative)"), 24 items: (1) EPC contract with all schedules, technical specification and performance guarantees (scope gaps and guarantee levels against the offtake contract); (2) EPC contractor's baseline schedule in native format with the critical path (float and delay probability, Example 48.2); (3) cost estimate by package with quantities and quotes (three-point testing, Example 48.1); (4) contingency calculation and its confidence level (adequacy); (5) geotechnical, hydrological and topographic surveys (ground and site risk); (6) technology reference list with fleet hours and serial-defect history (FOAK test, ssec:14.5.1); (7) equipment supply contracts and warranties (back-to-back with the EPC contract); (8) interface matrix for grid, fuel, water and access (who carries interface risk); (9) permits and their technical conditions (conditions that bite during construction); (10) owner's engineer reports and site-visit logs (independent read of progress); (11) O&M and LTSA contracts with fee schedules (opex and lifecycle); (12) availability and outage plan by year (model availability profile); (13) degradation and performance curves (energy and heat-rate paths); (14) opex budget with benchmarks (cost base); (15) major maintenance and lifecycle plan (MMRA sizing); (16) spare-parts strategy and critical-spare lead times (insurance indemnity period, Ch 49); (17) commissioning and completion test procedures (completion definitions, Ch 22 and Ch 61); (18) HSE plan and contractor safety record (E&S overlap, Ch 50); (19) grid studies and connection agreement (curtailment and backfeed dates); (20) climate and site design basis (flood, heat, cooling; Ch 84); (21) the sponsor's financial model technical-input sheet (reconciliation, walkthrough step 9); (22) EPC contractor and OEM financial statements (capacity to perform, Ch 49 counterparty credit); (23) insurance construction program summary (consistency with risks found); (24) list of everything the sponsor has not yet provided (reservations).

Exhibit 48.11 (`exh:48.11`, "Resource and reserves due diligence request list (Illustrative)"), 22 items in three blocks. Wind and solar: (1) on-site measurement data with logs, calibration and maintenance records (measurement uncertainty); (2) long-term reference data and correlation method (long-term correction); (3) layout, turbine or module and inverter specifications and power curves (gross output); (4) wake and loss model files and validation against operating neighbors (loss chain, Example 48.3); (5) curtailment history and the grid operator's studies (curtailment line); (6) the sponsor's energy yield assessment and its uncertainty table (P50 and P90, sec:9.10); (7) operating data of comparable plants (reference class). Hydro and geothermal: (8) flow records with gaps and the gauging method (hydrology); (9) climate-trend adjustment (firm against average energy); (10) sediment and reservoir operating rules (availability of head and flow); (11) well test data, reservoir model and decline forecasts (steam supply); (12) make-up drilling plan and budget (resource reserve, `sarulla` lesson). Mining and hydrocarbons: (13) the competent person's or qualified reserves evaluator's report and the code used (PRMS, JORC, NI 43-101 or S-K 1300; `t-reserves-codes`); (14) consent letter and independence statement of the competent person (reliance); (15) price deck and cut-off assumptions behind the reserve (reserve sensitivity, Example 48.4); (16) mine plan or field development plan with study stage and accuracy band (whether reserves are bankable); (17) drilling database and QA/QC (data quality); (18) metallurgical test work and recovery curves (recovered metal); (19) any inferred resources in the plan (excluded from lender cases); (20) reconciliation of production to reserve history (track record); (21) gas or feedstock reserve coverage against contracted quantities (48.3.4; Case P P-F61); (22) offtake specifications for product quality (penalties and rejections).

Exhibit 48.12 (`exh:48.12`, "Market and price-curve due diligence request list (Illustrative)"), 20 items: (1) the advisor's curve with base, low and high cases and its vintage date (which curve the lenders size on, 48.4.5); (2) demand forecast by year and its drivers (load growth); (3) supply build-out assumptions by technology and zone (cannibalization); (4) fuel and carbon price inputs with sources (marginal cost); (5) policy and market-design assumptions with dates (regulatory change); (6) interconnection and transmission assumptions (basis); (7) capture-rate method and history for the asset's technology and node (48.4.2); (8) basis and node-to-hub history (realized price); (9) curtailment and negative-price hours (volume and price); (10) ancillary-service and capacity revenue assumptions (non-energy revenue); (11) back-test of the advisor's earlier curves against outturn (track record); (12) comparison with at least one other curve (Example 48.5); (13) hedge and PPA terms that convert merchant into contracted revenue (bucket split, ssec:46.4.2); (14) counterparty credit for any PPA (Ch 49); (15) price-deck practice of the lenders for commodities (48.4.4); (16) forward-market liquidity and tenor (hedgeability); (17) the advisor's sensitivity on its three most important assumptions (Framework 48.2 step 5); (18) scope letter and reliance terms (who may rely); (19) independence statement (who else the advisor works for); (20) data the advisor did not have (reservations).

Exhibit 48.13 (`exh:48.13`, "Traffic and demand due diligence request list (Illustrative)"), 22 items: (1) the traffic and revenue report with its stated grade, investment or planning (48.5.1); (2) who commissioned it, when, and for what purpose (bias check, `t-traffic-forecast-accuracy`); (3) traffic counts with dates, locations and method (calibration base); (4) origin-destination and stated- or revealed-preference survey data (value of time, ssec:12.4.1); (5) the value-of-time cases and their sources (Example 48.6); (6) the diversion or toll-choice model with its parameters (Equation 48.4); (7) the land-use, housing and employment forecasts used, with their sources and dates (most common hidden driver, 48.5.3); (8) network assumptions, including competing and complementary roads and promised works (the SR 14 lesson, Case T); (9) the ramp-up profile and its reference class (S&P year-1 evidence); (10) vehicle-class mix and class multipliers (heavy-vehicle diversion); (11) toll escalation and elasticity assumptions (revenue per trip, ssec:12.4.2); (12) leakage and enforcement assumptions (collected revenue); (13) the model's calibration statistics (fit to counts); (14) the forecasts of rival bidders or the procuring authority where public (outlier test, `sydney-tunnels`); (15) the downside and lenders' case construction (sizing, Ch 36); (16) macroeconomic inputs (GDP, fuel prices); (17) the consultant's record on comparable roads (reference class); (18) for non-road demand, the equivalent driver: airline commitments, shipping-line contracts, ridership surveys, data-center pre-lets (48.5.5); (19) reliance letter and cap (48.1.1); (20) independence statement; (21) bring-down letter timing (financial close); (22) items the consultant could not verify (reservations).

Red flags:
- A report whose scope letter excludes the issue you care about.
- A traffic forecast above every other forecast you can find.
- A capex estimate with contingency stated as a flat percentage and no confidence level.
- A yield report with curtailment below the grid operator's own study.
- A price curve older than the last major policy change in the market.
- An IE who has also acted as owner's engineer for the sponsor.

Rules of thumb and their limits:
- Most-likely capex sums understate expected cost when risks are skewed (Example 48.1); the gap grows with ground risk.
- Interannual variability dominates one-year P90 for wind; measurement and model uncertainty dominate ten-year P90.
- In user-pay toll roads, first-year traffic has averaged about 0.73 to 0.77 of the forecast at financial close in S&P's 2002 to 2005 samples, and about 0.77 to 0.80 through years 2 to 5 (`t-traffic-forecast-accuracy`); the limit is that the samples are anonymized rating-agency research, ramp-up durations varied, and a new road's own data should replace the reference class as it arrives.

Common mistakes:
- Treating a reliance letter as a guarantee of accuracy.
- Accepting the sponsor's P50 and haircutting only P90.
- Using the market advisor's base case for merchant-tail sizing.
- Ignoring correlated risks in contingency analysis.

Questions experts ask:
- What would you have to see to change your conclusion?
- Which of your assumptions is furthest from the operating record of comparable assets?
- What did your firm forecast for the last three comparable projects, and what happened?
- What did you not have time to check?

## 48.12 Judgment drill

Situation: You are the credit officer at the lead arranger on Serra dos Ventos, the Example 48.3 wind farm in Rio Grande do Norte. It is Monday, October 19, 2026; credit committee meets on Thursday, October 22. The sponsor's consultant's P90 one-year is 446.6 GWh; your consultant's is 428.7 GWh. Sizing on yours cuts debt by USD 6.45 million on the modeling bank's simple sizing (Illustrative: tariff USD 48.30/MWh; debt sculpted to 1.25x on one-year P90 CFADS over 16 years at 7.20%, annuity factor 9.3227; the 17.90 GWh difference is worth USD 0.865 million of revenue a year). The sponsor says a rival bank will lend on their report.

Reasoning outline: identify which differences drive the gap (wake and curtailment); test each against evidence (neighbors' operating data, grid operator's curtailment study); apply Framework 48.2; consider splitting the difference only where evidence supports it; consider structural answers (size on the sponsor case with a cash sweep until two years of operating data confirm the yield, or a P90-based DSRA top-up). Expert answer: size on the lenders' consultant's curtailment, accept the sponsor's wake only if operating data from neighbors validate it, and offer a re-sizing mechanism after 24 months of data.

What would change the answer: curtailment backed by a firm grid reinforcement date (moves toward the sponsor); neighbors' data showing wake losses above 10% (moves toward the lenders' case); a market where lenders are competing hard for a small pipeline (pressure, not evidence; the drill names it).

## 48.13 Exercise set (17)

Tier 1 (4):
- 48.1: Distinguish an IE, an owner's engineer and a lenders' independent E&S consultant.
- 48.2: Explain why the PERT mean exceeds the most-likely estimate for skewed packages.
- 48.3: Explain why ten-year P90 is closer to P50 than one-year P90 (cross-reference ssec:9.6.2).
- 48.4: State four reasons a winning bidder's traffic forecast tends to be the highest, citing the S&P evidence in `t-traffic-forecast-accuracy`.

Tier 2 (8):
- 48.5: Recompute Example 48.1 (Rato Pakha) with the tunnel's high estimate at USD 84.0 million; restate P80 and the budget's confidence level, independent and with a 0.6 correlation between headworks and tunnel. Answers: tunnel PERT mean 52.47, sd 7.07; expected cost USD 193.78 million; independent sd USD 8.03 million, P80 USD 200.54 million, probability of staying within USD 197.5 million 0.678; correlated sd USD 9.72 million, P80 USD 201.96 million, probability 0.649. Common wrong answer: changing only the mean and keeping the old standard deviation.
- 48.6: Example 48.2 (Barka) with delay LDs capped at 180 days instead of 120; compute the expected LD recovery and the expected uncovered cost. Answers: expected delay cost unchanged at USD 16.43 million; expected LD recovery USD 12.53 million; expected uncovered cost USD 3.90 million (from USD 5.69 million); uncovered cost by band 0.41, 1.66, 3.71, 14.60, 36.79 (USD million).
- 48.7: Example 48.3 (Serra dos Ventos): compute the effect on one-year P90 of reducing measurement uncertainty from 4.5% to 3.0% (all else equal). Answers: combined one-year uncertainty 8.90% (from 9.51%); one-year P90 sponsor 450.55 GWh (from 446.57), lenders 432.50 GWh (from 428.67). Common wrong answer: subtracting 1.5 points from the combined uncertainty instead of recombining in quadrature (eq:9.5).
- 48.8: Example 48.4 (Loma Cobrecita): find the proved and probable reserve needed for a 32% reserve tail at a 10-year final maturity, holding throughput at 14.0 Mtpa. Answer: reserve life must be 10/(1 − 0.32) = 14.71 years, so reserves of 205.9 Mt (against 188.2 Mt certified).
- 48.9: Example 48.5 (Planta Solar Guadiana): compute the PVs if both advisors use A's capture path (0.78 falling 1.0 point a year to 0.62); isolate the baseload effect. Answers: Advisor B's PV with A's capture path EUR 40.97 million; baseload effect (A against B on the same capture path) EUR 6.26 million; capture effect (B's own path against A's) EUR 5.30 million; total EUR 11.56 million as in the example.
- 48.10: Example 48.6 (San Gabriel Bypass): find the value of time at which diversion falls to 50%. Answer: diversion is 50% when the time saving equals the toll: VOT = 3.60 × 60/14 = USD 15.43 per hour.
- 48.11: Draft five scope-letter questions for the market advisor on Coyote Draw Storage (fictional), a 200 MW, 2-hour merchant battery in the ERCOT West zone seeking a 2027 financing. Model answer asks for: the build-out of storage in the zone and its effect on spreads; the ancillary-service saturation path; the degradation and augmentation assumptions behind revenue; a back-test of the advisor's 2023 to 2025 battery revenue forecasts; and the low case it would expect lenders to size on.
- 48.12: Fill the diligence scope matrix (Framework 48.1) for the 2026 financing of the San Gabriel Bypass (Example 48.6), rows for technical, traffic and legal only. Model answer fills commissioner, reliance, scope questions, outputs, timing and cost and cap for each row.

Tier 3 (5):
- 48.13: Case T: using T-F04, T-F18, Annex TR T.2 and T.3 and the Case Bible's traffic inputs, write the memo Ridgeway's client lenders should have received in 2014 on Pellow's forecast, applying Framework 48.2. Model answer flags the value-of-time choice (high case 22% above what a survey later found; Pellow itself recommended the central case), the housing timetable, the heavy-vehicle multiplier and the SR 14 works, and recommends sizing on Ridgeway's case.
- 48.14: Case P: using Case Bible inputs and Annex P 4.1 only, list the ten technical inputs in the Case P banking case the IE must reconcile (net output, heat rate, availability profile, degradation, auxiliary load or part-load factor, EPC price and schedule, contingency, fixed and variable O&M, LTSA fees and intervals, major maintenance outside the LTSA) and state the source and Gwen's finding for each.
- 48.15: Model task (macro-free): build a PERT contingency sheet with a correlation input and a P-level output, reproducing Example 48.1 (answers: expected cost USD 191.75 million; sd 6.31 and 7.82; P80 197.06 and 198.33).
- 48.16: Real case: using `sydney-tunnels`, write a 400-word lender's note on what a reader of the CCT forecasts in 2002 could have flagged.
- 48.17: Write the IE and market-advisor scope letters for the 2027 financing of Coyote Draw Storage (the Exercise 48.11 battery), each with scope and exclusions, deliverables dated against a close calendar (mandate March 1, 2027; credit approval May 14; signing June 18; financial close July 30), reliance and liability cap, independence statement, fee and change mechanism. Full model answer: two one-page letters following walkthrough step 0, with the market letter adding the curve vintage, the cases to be delivered (base, low, high and a lenders' sizing case) and a back-test of earlier forecasts (capabilities review fix).

## 48.15 Glossary entries owned

Entries follow `bible/glossary-canon.md`. Removed by ruling and used here without bold, with a cross-reference to the home: first-of-a-kind (ssec:14.5.1, R-042); three-point estimate (ssec:15.3.2, R-043); energy yield assessment (sec:9.10, R-043); optimism bias (ssec:9.9.1, R-043); ore reserve and competent person (ssec:12.3.1, R-025); reserve tail (ssec:45.6.3, R-104); value of time (ssec:12.4.1) and toll elasticity (ssec:12.4.2) (R-026).

| Term | Abbreviation | Definition | Home section |
|---|---|---|---|
| due diligence | DD | The investigation lenders and investors carry out, largely through advisors, to verify a project's assumptions and identify risks before committing capital. | 48.1.1 |
| reliance letter | – | An advisor's letter allowing named lenders or investors to rely on a report prepared for another client, usually subject to a liability cap. | 48.1.1 |
| vendor due diligence | VDD | Diligence reports commissioned by a seller and made available, with reliance, to bidders and the eventual buyer. | 48.1.1 |
| bring-down letter | – | An advisor's confirmation at financial close that nothing has come to its attention since its report that changes its conclusions. | 48.1.2 |
| diligence scope matrix | – | The book's framework for planning every diligence workstream by commissioner, reliance, scope, outputs and timing. | 48.1.2 |
| report challenge protocol | – | The book's eight-step method for testing any advisor's report. | 48.1.3 |
| owner's engineer | – | The engineer retained by the project company to manage and supervise design and construction on its behalf, as distinct from the lenders' independent engineer. | 48.2.1 |
| loss chain | – | The ordered set of percentage losses applied to gross output to reach net output. | 48.3.1 |
| price curve | – | A market advisor's projection of future prices for a commodity or power product, usually in base, low and high cases. | 48.4.1 |
| investment-grade traffic study | – | A traffic and revenue forecast prepared to the standard lenders and rating agencies rely on, as distinct from a planning study. | 48.5.1 |
| diversion curve | – | The relationship between the toll road's share of a trip market and the generalized-cost advantage of using it. | 48.5.2 |

## 49.2 Purpose and capabilities

Purpose. Chapter 48 dealt with the physical project. This chapter deals with everything that makes the project a bankable legal and financial object: whether the contracts and permits exist, bind and transfer; whether the security will work; whether the insurance responds to the losses that matter; whether the model computes what it claims; whether tax will take more than the model says; whether the counterparties can pay; and whether anyone in the structure is sanctioned, corrupt or not who they say they are. For each workstream the reader learns what the advisor does, how to scope the work, how to read the report, and how to challenge it, using the report challenge protocol of Chapter 48.

What you will be able to do:

- Scope and read a lenders' legal due diligence report and legal opinions, and grade each finding by severity and cure (Capabilities 7, 3).
- Test an insurance program and a model against the risks and numbers they must carry, and quantify the gaps (Capabilities 7, 4).
- Quantify tax leakage and analyze an offtaker's and a contractor's ability to pay (Capabilities 7, 8).
- Run KYC, sanctions and anti-corruption diligence, including ownership tracing, issue the request list for each workstream, and decide what to do with a finding (Capabilities 7, 15).
