## 49.4 Locked table of contents

Opening (no heading). `\section*{What you will be able to do}`.

**49.1 Legal and regulatory due diligence** (`sec:49.1`)

- 49.1.1 What lenders' counsel reviews and why (`ssec:49.1.1`). Corporate existence, capacity and authority; the project documents; permits and land; the security package and its perfection; regulatory regime; litigation; local-law issues. Lenders' counsel versus sponsor's counsel, local counsel's role, the cost and timetable. Four lenses: the sponsor wants a short report; the lenders want every issue graded; the government is asked for consents and confirmations it has never given before; the EPC contractor and offtaker see direct-agreement requests.
- 49.1.2 The legal due diligence report and its red-flag grid (`ssec:49.1.2`). Report formats (full report, red-flag report, exceptions-only); Framework 49.1 grading each finding by severity, likelihood and cure (CP, covenant, price, structure, walk). Exhibit 49.1: an Illustrative red-flag grid with eight findings for Sine-Saloum Power SA (fictional), a 126 MW gas-engine IPP in Senegal under OHADA law, financed in 2026 (the same project as the 49.7 walkthrough's report).
- 49.1.3 Permits, licenses, and land against the loan's life (`ssec:49.1.3`). Example 49.1 (Illustrative 220 MWac solar plant in Nueva Ecija, Luzon, the Philippines, financial close March 31, 2027): a permit and land schedule tested against the loan's final maturity; transferability to lenders on enforcement; conditions that bite during construction.
- 49.1.4 Reviewing the project contracts (`ssec:49.1.4`). Running the gap scan and "Who pays if...?" trace (owned by Ch 28) as a diligence task; termination, assignment and step-in rights; mismatches between PPA, EPC and fuel contracts.
- 49.1.5 Legal opinions and their qualifications (`ssec:49.1.5`). Capacity and authority, enforceability, choice of law and submission to jurisdiction, recognition of judgments and awards (forward reference to Ch 54), security creation and perfection opinions; for civil-law security, whether the opinion covers how the security agent holds the security (trust, parallel debt or statutory agent) and whether that survives the agent's insolvency and lender transfers, using OHADA's mandatory-content rule and the French statutory agent as checklists (`t-security-civil-law`; mechanics owned by ssec:52.2.2); standard assumptions and qualifications and how to read past them; the reasoned opinion where the law is unsettled. Clause-style extract: Illustrative opinion paragraph with qualifications (Clause 49.1). No fact sheet covers opinion market practice; state no market figure about opinions.
- 49.1.6 Regulatory diligence (`ssec:49.1.6`). License conditions, tariff approval and pass-through, regulator independence, change-in-law exposure; how regulatory findings turn into change-in-law protection asks (cross-reference Ch 17, Ch 18).

**49.2 Insurance due diligence** (`sec:49.2`)

- 49.2.1 The lenders' insurance advisor's mandate (`ssec:49.2.1`). Reviewing the program against the insurance covenant, the risk register and market availability; the report and its recommendations; distinction from the sponsor's broker.
- 49.2.2 Testing cover against debt service, spare lead times, and deductibles (`ssec:49.2.2`; retitled, R-141). Sums insured against replacement value; DSU and BI daily indemnity against debt service and fixed costs; indemnity period against critical-spare lead times; deductibles; Example 49.2 (Illustrative 431 MW CCGT in Ba Ria-Vung Tau province, southern Vietnam, in operation, 2026 insurance renewal). Equation 49.1.
- 49.2.3 Insurer security, fronting, and reinsurance (`ssec:49.2.3`). Minimum insurer ratings, local fronting with offshore reinsurance, cut-through and assignment of reinsurance (cross-reference Ch 27); what the advisor checks in the wording; the market cycle as the advisor's context, from `t-insurance-market` (hard market after Lloyd's 2018 to 2020 losses, softening from 2023 to 2025 as capital returned; why lenders insist on "commercially reasonable availability" language), observing its "Do not state" list (no renewable-specific rates, no unverified reinsurance rate-on-line figures). Case P anchor: AGK's local fronting with 95% reinsured offshore and the cut-through (Case Bible Part 1, insurance program; Annex P 4.2 finding 13).
- 49.2.4 The broker's letter of undertaking and the insurance CPs (`ssec:49.2.4`). Applies the broker's letter of undertaking defined at ssec:27.6.3 as a diligence and CP item: what the broker undertakes to lenders, endorsements, evidence at financial close, and the annual renewal test.

**49.3 Model audit engagement** (`sec:49.3`)

- 49.3.1 Scope of a model audit engagement (`ssec:49.3.1`). Logic and arithmetic, inputs reconciled to documents and reports, tax and accounting treatment, scenarios and sensitivities, and the bring-down at financial close; cross-reference Ch 44 for the audit process and findings (R-140). Case P anchor: Ferrand Model Assurance's report of June 28, 2018 satisfied Castellan's credit-committee condition (Annex P 1.15.3); its findings are Ch 44's (P-F17).
- 49.3.2 Materiality and sign-off (`ssec:49.3.2`). Example 49.3 (Illustrative availability-payment hospital PPP in Ontario, Canada, model audit before a March 2027 financial close): an understatement of cash tax reduces sculpted debt by 1.83%; applying the materiality threshold (defined at ssec:44.2.1) in the engagement letter; the model auditor's opinion letter and its scope limits.
- 49.3.3 Reliance, liability caps, and the bring-down (`ssec:49.3.3`). Who the auditor works for, reliance letters, caps, and the final run at close.

**49.4 Tax and accounting due diligence** (`sec:49.4`)

- 49.4.1 Reviewing project-level tax (`ssec:49.4.1`). Incentives and holidays and their conditions; withholding taxes on interest, fees and dividends, thin capitalization and interest limitation as diligence items (rules defined at ssec:7.11.4, R-136; not re-taught); VAT recovery (VAT facility at ssec:31.5.1, refund lag at ssec:67.7.2, R-125); transfer pricing on related-party contracts (O&M, LTSA affiliates); stamp and registration duties on security. Cross-references Ch 41 (ssec:41.5.3) and forward reference Ch 67.
- 49.4.2 Quantifying leakage (`ssec:49.4.2`). Tax leakage (defined at ssec:67.1.1) turned into annual cash amounts in diligence. Example 49.4 (Illustrative 2026 financing of a 342 MW gas-fired plant in Ghana's Greater Accra Region by offshore banks; rates Illustrative, not statements of Ghanaian tax law): withholding gross-up cost and interest-limitation disallowance. Equation 49.2 (gross-up).
- 49.4.3 Tax opinions, rulings, and indemnities (`ssec:49.4.3`). When to seek an advance ruling; tax opinion scope; tax covenants and indemnities in the finance documents and SPAs.
- 49.4.4 Accounting diligence (`ssec:49.4.4`). What lenders check in historical accounts (operating assets, refinancings, acquisitions), quality of earnings, and how sponsors' accounting goals affect structures (forward reference to Ch 66).

**49.5 Counterparty credit diligence** (`sec:49.5`)

- 49.5.1 Ranking counterparties by exposure and replaceability (`ssec:49.5.1`; retitled, R-141). Ranking counterparties by exposure and replaceability: offtaker, fuel supplier, EPC contractor, LTSA provider, O&M operator, hedge banks, insurers, account banks, sponsors (equity commitments and support).
- 49.5.2 Analyzing a state-owned offtaker (`ssec:49.5.2`). Collections, cost recovery, tariff setting, arrears to other IPPs, government transfers; Example 49.5 (Illustrative single-buyer utility of a fictional West African republic, March 2027): monthly cash available for IPPs against bills, and how long a two-month LC lasts under pro rata underpayment. Forward reference to Ch 59 for sovereign and payment-security analysis.
- 49.5.3 Contractor, supplier, and hedge counterparty credit (`ssec:49.5.3`). Parent guarantees and bonds as substitutes for credit, backlog and margin trends in EPC contractors, rating triggers and replacement obligations for hedge counterparties and account banks (cross-reference Ch 22).
- 49.5.4 The counterparty credit card (`ssec:49.5.4`). Framework 49.2 in a box and demonstrated on Example 49.5's utility.

**49.6 KYC, sanctions, and anti-corruption diligence** (`sec:49.6`)

- 49.6.1 Know your customer and beneficial ownership (`ssec:49.6.1`). Who must be identified (borrower, sponsors, ultimate beneficial owners, controllers), documents, politically exposed persons, adverse media, and source of funds; why a local sponsor's family ownership and political links are routine findings, not disqualifications.
- 49.6.2 Sanctions screening and ownership tracing (`ssec:49.6.2`). Lists to screen against and the ownership tests (from `t-anticorruption-sanctions`: US OFAC 50% rule, aggregate, direct or indirect, guidance of August 13, 2014, ownership-based and not control-based although OFAC warns about control; UK "more than 50%" of shares or voting rights plus control, without simple aggregation unless holdings are under a joint arrangement, guidance updated May 12, 2026); Example 49.6 (Illustrative O&M subcontractor for a wind farm in Kazakhstan, 2026) and Framework 49.3. The legal regimes are owned by Ch 60 (sec:60.7); here they are applied as screening tests, with a one-sentence forward reference (R-088).
- 49.6.3 Anti-corruption diligence (`ssec:49.6.3`). Agents, consultants and local partners; how the project was procured; payments to officials; red flags; adequate-procedures and reasonable-procedures benchmarks (UK Bribery Act section 7 and ECCTA failure to prevent fraud, from `t-anticorruption-sanctions`, with legal detail in Ch 60). Real case paragraphs: from `bujagali`, the first project collapsed in 2003 after the sponsor withdrew amid financial distress and corruption investigations (allegation reported in 2003; do not attribute any payment to a named official); from `odebrecht-2016`, why integrity diligence covers co-sponsors and EPC contractors, not only the borrower: debarment screening, how the award was won, and the consequences when a contract is annulled for corruption (facts and "Do not state" list from the sheet). One-sentence forward reference to sec:60.6 for the anti-bribery regimes (R-088).
- 49.6.4 From findings to documents (`ssec:49.6.4`). Integrity representations, sanctions and anti-corruption covenants, information undertakings, and monitoring; forward reference to Ch 51 for drafting.
- 49.6.5 When the screen changes after close (`ssec:49.6.5`). Short paragraph using `sanctions-2022` (Nord Stream 2 lenders' write-offs) to show that sanctions diligence is a monitoring obligation; full teaching of sanctions as political risk in Ch 60.

**49.7 Walkthrough: reading a lenders' legal due diligence report** (`sec:49.7`).

**49.8 Case P: the legal due diligence report and KYC on Groupe Talmé** (`sec:49.8`).

**49.9 Practitioner's notebook** (`sec:49.9`). Includes Exhibits 49.7 to 49.12, the request lists for the six workstreams (see 49.11 below). **49.10 Judgment drill** (`sec:49.10`).

**49.11 The project's neighbors need their own diligence** (`sec:49.11`). Close. Crystallizes: each workstream converts findings into CPs, covenants, price or structure; integrity diligence follows ownership and agency; diligence is a photograph that monitoring must keep current. Last paragraph poses the problem Ch 50 answers: the communities, land, water and habitats around a project can stop it as surely as a defective pledge, and lenders have a separate rulebook for them.

**49.12 Exercises** (`sec:49.12`). **49.13 Solutions to exercises** (`sec:49.13`). `\section*{Sources}`.

## 49.5 Opening

Real deal moment from `sanctions-2022`: in 2017 five European energy companies agreed to lend half the EUR 9.5 billion cost of Nord Stream 2, a pipeline that by early 2022 was physically complete; on February 23, 2022 the United States sanctioned Nord Stream 2 AG, and within weeks the lenders wrote off their loans (Uniper reported a EUR 987 million impairment on March 9, 2022, EUR 695 million of principal and EUR 292 million of accrued interest). The loan documents, the security and the engineering were never the problem. Open on the impairment and turn to the question of what a lender's diligence can and cannot see, then into the workstreams in order of how often they decide a deal. Keep the case to the opening and 49.6.5; Ch 60 owns sanctions as political risk.

## 49.6 Worked examples

**Example 49.1 Testing permits and land against the loan (Illustrative)** (`ex:49.1`, in 49.1.3)

- Scenario: Pantabangan Sun Power Corp. (fictional), a 220 MWac solar plant in Nueva Ecija, Luzon, the Philippines, sponsored by a Philippine conglomerate and a Japanese trading house (both fictional), financed by three Philippine banks and a Japanese bank; financial close March 31, 2027; senior loan final maturity March 31, 2045; PPA (with a distribution utility, unnamed) expiry 2052. Lenders require every permit and land right needed to operate to run at least to final maturity plus two years, or to be renewable as of right.
- Inputs (schedule, all Illustrative and not statements of Philippine law): environmental compliance certificate (no expiry; 41 conditions, three of which require community programs during construction); generation license or equivalent operating authority (to 2052); water extraction permit for module washing (expires 2037; renewal at the regulator's discretion); land lease from 46 private landowners (to 2051; 3 of the 46 leases unregistered); grid connection agreement (to 2052); foreshore access permit for the transmission line crossing (to 2032; renewable).
- Computed: gap to final maturity plus two years (March 31, 2047): water permit short by about 10 years with discretionary renewal; foreshore permit short by about 15 years but renewable as a matter of course (writer states the distinction); three unregistered leases a perfection gap, not a term gap.
- Result: three findings graded with Framework 49.1: water permit (medium severity; cure by alternative water supply contract or covenant to renew plus reserve); unregistered leases (high for security; CP to registration); foreshore (low; covenant).

**Example 49.2 Business interruption cover against debt service and spare lead time (Illustrative)** (`ex:49.2`, in 49.2.2)

- Scenario: Mekong Delta Power Company Ltd (fictional), a 431 MW CCGT in Ba Ria-Vung Tau province, southern Vietnam, in operation and selling to the state utility under a USD-indexed PPA (terms Illustrative); at the July 2026 renewal the lenders' insurance advisor, Harbourside Risk Consulting (fictional), tests BI cover.
- Inputs: annual senior debt service USD 61.3 million; fixed operating costs that continue during an outage USD 14.8 million a year; BI daily indemnity USD 172,000; time deductible 60 days; indemnity period 12 months; critical spare: generator step-up transformer, lead time 14 months plus 2 months installation (16 months, about 486 days at 30.4 days a month).
- Equation 49.1 (`eq:49.1`): required daily indemnity $= (\mathrm{DS} + \text{fixed costs})/365$.
- Key results: required daily indemnity USD 208,493; shortfall USD 36,493 a day; uncovered months beyond the indemnity period 4 (cost at the required rate USD 25.35 million); for a 486-day outage, loss USD 101.33 million against recovery of 305 indemnified days times USD 172,000 = USD 52.46 million.
- Teaching points: lengthen the indemnity period to cover the longest critical-spare lead time, or hold a spare transformer; size daily indemnity to debt service plus fixed costs, not to the sponsor's budgeted margin.

**Example 49.3 A model error measured in debt capacity (Illustrative)** (`ex:49.3`, in 49.3.2)

- Scenario: Northbay Health Partners (fictional), the project company for an availability-payment hospital PPP in Sudbury, Ontario, Canada, before a March 2027 financial close; the model auditor (fictional) reports to the lenders. Figures in CAD million.
- Inputs: 12-year CFADS (CAD million): 48.6, 49.3, 50.1, 50.4, 51.2, 51.9, 52.3, 53.0, 53.6, 54.1, 54.8, 55.2; debt sculpted to 1.30x at an all-in rate of 6.55% (stylized: a real hospital PPP would be longer-dated; the 12-year profile keeps the arithmetic visible). The model auditor finds that tax depreciation is overstated, so cash tax is understated by CAD 1.4 million a year in years 4 to 12.
- Key results: debt as modeled CAD 322.91 million; corrected CAD 316.99 million; difference CAD 5.91 million (1.83%).
- Teaching points: express every finding in debt capacity and minimum DSCR, not only in cells; a finding above the materiality threshold agreed in the engagement letter (the concept is Ch 44's, ssec:44.2.1; example levels such as 0.5% of debt or 0.01x of minimum DSCR are indicative, D-011) must be corrected before the auditor signs.

**Example 49.4 Quantifying tax leakage (Illustrative)** (`ex:49.4`, in 49.4.2)

- Scenario: Tema Bay Power Ltd (fictional), a 342 MW gas-fired plant in Ghana's Greater Accra Region, refinanced in 2026 with a USD loan from three offshore banks; the lenders' tax advisor (fictional) quantifies leakage. All rates are Illustrative and are not statements of Ghanaian tax law or any treaty.
- Inputs: annual interest on the commercial loan USD 18.4 million; domestic withholding tax on interest 10%, grossed up by the project company; treaty rate 5% if the lending office qualifies; tax EBITDA USD 74.6 million; net interest expense USD 29.1 million; interest limitation 30% of tax EBITDA; corporate tax 25%.
- Equation 49.2 (`eq:49.2`): gross-up cost $= I/(1-w) - I$.
- Key results: gross-up cost USD 2.044 million a year at 10% and USD 0.968 million at 5%; interest limit USD 22.38 million; disallowed USD 6.72 million; extra tax USD 1.68 million a year (before any carry-forward of disallowed interest, a point the writer explains with a reference to Ch 41).
- Teaching points: tax DD turns rules (defined at ssec:7.11.4) into annual cash amounts and checks the model carries them; grandfathering and treaty eligibility are typical DD findings (the Case P interest limitation with grandfathering from 2025 is a Case Bible input, cited only as a pointer to Ch 67, sec:67.5).

**Example 49.5 A state utility's cash available for IPPs (Illustrative)** (`ex:49.5`, in 49.5.2)

- Scenario: Société Nationale d'Électricité de Valombe (SNEV; fictional), the state-owned single buyer of the fictional Republic of Valombe, a small West African coastal state (not Kessara); a lenders' credit analyst tests it in March 2027 for an IPP financing; monthly figures in USD million.
- Inputs: retail revenue billed 58.9; collection rate 81%; utility's own costs paid first 12.6; total monthly bills from IPPs and fuel suppliers 43.2; the project's own monthly bill 7.4; the project's LC covers two months of its bill (USD 14.8 million).
- Key results: cash available for IPPs USD 35.11 million; coverage 0.813; monthly shortfall USD 8.09 million; if the utility pays every IPP pro rata, the project's monthly shortfall is USD 1.39 million and the LC lasts about 10.7 months; if the utility stops paying the project entirely, the LC lasts 2 months.
- Teaching points: model the utility's cash, not its income statement; pro rata underpayment drains reserves slowly and is harder to call a default; forward reference to Ch 59 for payment-security design.

**Example 49.6 Tracing ownership through a sanctions screen (Illustrative)** (`ex:49.6`, in 49.6.2)

- Scenario: in June 2026 the lenders to Ili Steppe Wind (fictional), a 140 MW wind farm in Almaty region, Kazakhstan, screen its O&M subcontractor, which is owned by B Ltd (all entities and persons fictional; "designated" means listed on the OFAC SDN list and the UK consolidated list).
- Inputs: Person X (designated) owns 50% of A Ltd; A Ltd owns 30% of B Ltd; Person Y (designated) owns 25% of B Ltd directly. Variant: X owns 40% of A Ltd (A not blocked) and A owns 60% of B.
- Key results (OFAC 50% rule, aggregate, direct or indirect): A is blocked (50%); B is blocked because blocked persons own 55% in aggregate (A's 30% plus Y's 25%). Variant: A is not blocked, so under the OFAC rule B's blocked ownership is Y's 25% and B is not blocked; the UK control test may still catch B if X controls A or B; the bank's policy may treat B as high risk regardless.
- Verification flag: the writer must confirm against `t-anticorruption-sanctions` (and, if needed, OFAC's published FAQs) that indirect ownership is counted through blocked entities only; record the source in the status note.
- Teaching points: ownership tracing is arithmetic on a chart, but the answer depends on the regime; plan to the strictest applicable regime (`t-anticorruption-sanctions` teaching angle).

## 49.7 Walkthrough

**Walkthrough: reading a lenders' legal due diligence report** (Section 49.7). Artifact: an Illustrative 60-page lenders' legal DD report dated April 2026 for Sine-Saloum Power SA (fictional), a 126 MW gas-engine IPP in Senegal (civil law, OHADA Uniform Acts), prepared by an international firm with Dakar local counsel (both fictional), with a marked-up executive summary and red-flag table. Steps:

1. Scope, reliance and assumptions page (documents reviewed, versions, dates; local counsel used).
2. Executive summary: findings sorted by severity; check whether the grading matches Framework 49.1.
3. Corporate and authority: board and shareholder approvals, capacity for each document.
4. Permits and land: schedule against final maturity (Example 49.1 method).
5. Project documents: termination, assignment, direct agreements; gap-scan cross-check (Ch 28).
6. Government support: authority to issue guarantees within legal ceilings, approvals, budget appropriation risk.
7. Security: what can be taken under local law, registration and notice requirements, and how the security agent holds the security (OHADA's statutory security agent and its mandatory-content rule, `t-security-civil-law`; parallel debt and the mechanics forward-referenced to ssec:52.2.2).
8. FX and offshore accounts authorizations.
9. Litigation and regulatory issues.
10. Outstanding items, convert each to a CP, covenant or price adjustment; write the follow-up list.

Clause (Illustrative): Clause 49.1 Opinion paragraph on enforceability with qualifications, local-law legal opinion (`cl:49.1`), annotated paragraph by paragraph (what each qualification removes from the lender's protection).

## 49.8 Frameworks

- **Framework 49.1: The red-flag grid** (`fw:red-flag-grid`), original. Purpose: grade every diligence finding and route it to a cure. Steps: (1) state the finding in one sentence; (2) severity if it happens (loss to lenders or delay in USD and days); (3) likelihood (low, medium, high with a reason); (4) cure route: CP, covenant, structural change, price or insurance, sponsor support, accept, walk; (5) owner and deadline; (6) residual risk after cure. Demonstrated in Exhibit 49.1, Example 49.1 and the Case P installment.
- **Framework 49.2: The counterparty credit card** (`fw:counterparty-credit-card`), original. Purpose: a one-page view of each counterparty's ability and willingness to perform. Rows: exposure (amount and duration), replaceability and replacement cost, financial capacity (cash, not income), support (parent guarantee, sovereign guarantee, LC, bond), legal and political willingness (track record of paying other IPPs, disputes), triggers and remedies in the contract. Demonstrated on Example 49.5.
- **Framework 49.3: The ownership trace** (`fw:ownership-trace`), original. Purpose: decide whether an entity is caught by sanctions or is a politically exposed or high-risk party. Steps: (1) draw the full ownership and control chart to natural persons; (2) screen every node against the lists that apply to every lender and currency involved; (3) apply each regime's ownership test (aggregate, direct or indirect, control) separately; (4) record control rights (board, veto, management) in addition to equity; (5) refresh at signing, at close and on any change of ownership. Demonstrated on Example 49.6.

## 49.9 Real cases

| Slug | Use | Lesson |
|---|---|---|
| `sanctions-2022` | Opening; 49.6.5 | Sanctions diligence is a photograph that monitoring must keep current; corporate-style lenders to a complete pipeline lost principal and interest within weeks of designation |
| `t-anticorruption-sanctions` (topic sheet) | 49.6.2, 49.6.3 | OFAC aggregate 50% test versus UK ownership-or-control test; adequate and reasonable procedures as the diligence benchmark; enforcement priorities change while statutes do not (2025 DOJ guidelines) |
| `bujagali` | 49.6.3 | A project can collapse before close when integrity issues surround the sponsor; re-tendering cost years |
| `ivanpah` (optional, one sentence in 49.5.1) | 49.5.1 | PG&E's forbearance (December 2015) when the plant fell short of guaranteed energy production shows that a counterparty's willingness to work with the project is part of its credit |
| `odebrecht-2016` | 49.6.3 | Integrity diligence covers co-sponsors and EPC contractors as well as the borrower; a contract annulled for corruption can leave lenders without termination compensation |
| `t-security-civil-law` (topic sheet) | 49.1.5, walkthrough step 7 | Legal DD must confirm, jurisdiction by jurisdiction, how the security agent holds security and whether the opinion covers it |
| `t-insurance-market` (topic sheet) | 49.2.3 | The insurance cycle decides what cover is available at renewal; lenders write "commercially reasonable availability" into covenants because of it |

Observe each sheet's "do not state" list (for example: the OFAC rule does not cover control; the FCPA was not repealed or suspended; no renewable-specific insurance rates; no Cobre-style or Odebrecht details beyond the sheet).

No fact sheet covers legal-opinion market practice (the earlier request for `t-legal-opinions` was not delivered): 49.1.5 teaches opinions from principle and states no market figure; listed in the unit's Open requests as optional.

## 49.10 Running-case installment

**Section 49.8 Case P: the legal due diligence report and KYC on Groupe Talmé.** Story dates May to June 2018 (Laurent's report June 2018, before the July 17, 2018 financial close). Characters: Laurent Bécherel (lenders' counsel, Ashworth Quayle), Mariama Talmé; Adaeze Whitcombe (sponsors' counsel, Pemberton Hale) may appear briefly. Two scenes:

1. Scene (Ashworth Quayle, Paris, June 2018): Laurent presents the onshore legal DD findings to the lenders' working group, with Cabinet Aïdasso & Associés as Kessaran counsel (Annex P 2.3). He opens an objection with "En droit kessarais" (habit, once). The report's fifteen findings and Laurent's own green and amber ratings are Case Bible inputs (Annex P 4.2): (1) parallel debt untested in Kessaran insolvency, opinion qualified (amber); (2) business pledge registered and covering future receivables, with his advice, wrong as 2023 showed, that no notice to SEKA was needed for the pledge to bind SEKA (green as given); (3) emphyteutic lease (45 years, 62 hectares) registered, mortgage registration outstanding as a condition subsequent (amber); (4) Government Guarantee (cap USD 1,250 million, issued by MEF) valid within the Finance Act 2017 ceiling and registered, but payment needs a budget appropriation that does not exist (amber); (5) IA stabilization clause enforceable as a contract but cannot bind Parliament, damages only (amber); (6) immunity waiver valid, execution against public-domain assets barred (green); (7) Exchange Control Authorization No. 2018-114 to be issued before close (green, issued); (8) PPA LC replenishment default can be suspended by an OREK-approved payment plan, delaying the put option by up to 90 days (amber, flagged and accepted commercially); (9) hardship exclusion (Article 1135-1) valid between commercial parties but untested at the Supreme Court (amber); (10) withholding tax exemptions depend on Investment Code certificates, a condition subsequent (amber); (11) RAP corridor compensation incomplete (amber; link to Ch 50); (12) Bélanou Power SA validly incorporated in February 2017, articles amended (green); (13) local fronting requirement for insurance, cut-through enforceable as a contractual undertaking (amber); (14) New York Convention (1998) and ICSID membership, awards enforceable (green); (15) KYC: Talmé PEP association, enhanced due diligence clean (green). Narration explains, with Ch 10's principles and a forward reference to ssec:52.2.2 (R-089), why a civil-law jurisdiction without a security trust needs a parallel debt undertaking, and grades each finding again with Framework 49.1, adding the cure route, owner and residual risk the report itself did not give (Exhibit 49.6, `exh:49.6`, "Case P lenders' legal due diligence findings graded \casep"). For finding 2, the narration says only, with a one-sentence forward pointer, that Ch 52 shows what happened in 2023; for finding 8, that Ch 59 shows the payment plan in use.
2. Scene (Dabakro, May 2018): KYC on Groupe Talmé. The banks' compliance teams ask for the beneficial ownership chain and PEP declarations; Mariama answers with "Who else has seen this?" (habit, once), concerned about where family information will go. Narration teaches 49.6.1 on the scene using the Case Bible's KYC facts (Annex P 2.4): Groupe Talmé SA is 100% owned by Société Financière Talmé SA, owned by Ousmane Talmé (40%), Mariama Talmé (20%), her brothers Kwami and Séverin Talmé (15% each) and the Fondation Talmé (10%, a registered vocational-training charity); Groupe Talmé holds its Bélanou shares through Talmé Énergie SA (100%); Ousmane's brother-in-law, Daniel Fiogbé, sat in the National Assembly from 2012 to 2017 and on its energy committee, so the family is PEP-associated; enhanced due diligence found no role for him in the tender and no payments to him, and the ABDB's 2017 integrity review and the lenders' 2018 KYC were clean; the 1990s diesel IPP (48 MW at Dabakro port, PPA with SEKA 1997 to 2015, a 2009 fuel-indexation dispute settled in 2010); a 2011 customs penalty of KCR 290 million on the cement business, settled without criminal proceedings; source of funds for Talmé's equity (cement dividends and a KCR 12.0 billion UBK loan secured on the cement plant). One sentence notes that Kilnworth's July 2016 report of an intermediary's approach appeared in the 2018 KYC as a positive finding (Annex P 2.6; the story is Ch 87's). Kilnworth's own chain runs through Kilnworth Bélanou Holdings Ltd, a Mauritius company (Annex P 1.3.1), to Kilnworth Power International plc, a UK-listed company.

Figures: none from the ledgers (Case Bible Part 6, row 49). Inputs printable: Annex P 4.2 and 2.4 as above; Government Guarantee cap USD 1,250 million; lease 45 years, 62 hectares; Exchange Control Authorization No. 2018-114; law and dispute forums (Case Bible Section 1.6 Documentation and law).

Also in narration (one paragraph): the lenders' insurance advisor (Marchbank Risk Advisory, reviewing a program arranged by Fenwick Lowe Insurance Brokers and fronted by AGK with 95% reinsured offshore) and the model auditor (Ferrand Model Assurance, report June 28, 2018; its findings are taught in Ch 44, P-F17) as the other Case P workstreams, named with their roles only.

## 49.11 Practitioner's notebook (outline)

Checklist:
- Every finding in every DD report is on one red-flag grid with an owner and a cure.
- Permits and land run to final maturity plus a tail, or renewal is as of right.
- Opinions cover capacity, enforceability, choice of law, recognition, and each security document's creation and perfection.
- BI and DSU daily indemnity at least equals debt service plus fixed costs; indemnity period covers critical-spare lead times.
- Model audit findings expressed in debt and DSCR; sign-off letter covers the final close run.
- Tax leakage in the model matches the tax DD report line by line.
- Ownership chart traced to natural persons and screened under every applicable regime.
- Each workstream's request list (Exhibits 49.7 to 49.12) issued with its scope letter and closed out before credit approval.

Request lists (home exhibits cited by matter 93's "due-diligence request lists" row; appended labels so no existing label moves). Each is an `exhibit` table with columns No., Item requested, Why it is needed, Feeds (finding, CP, covenant or model input). Written for lenders' advisors on a cross-border project financing.

Exhibit 49.7 (`exh:49.7`, "Legal and regulatory due diligence request list (Illustrative)"), 26 items: (1) constitutional documents, registers and good-standing certificates of the project company and each obligor (capacity); (2) board and shareholder approvals for each document (authority); (3) shareholders' agreement and equity documents (control, transfer restrictions, Ch 26); (4) every project contract with amendments, side letters and notices (gap scan, Ch 28); (5) direct agreements, signed or in draft (step-in); (6) concession, IA and government support instruments with the authority under which each is issued (ceilings, appropriations); (7) permits and licenses with conditions and expiry (Example 49.1); (8) land title, leases and registrations (security and continuity); (9) security documents in draft and the registration and notice steps for each (perfection); (10) the security agent structure under local law (trust, parallel debt or statutory agent; `t-security-civil-law`); (11) FX, offshore account and repatriation authorizations (convertibility, Ch 59); (12) tax incentive certificates and conditions (condition subsequent risk); (13) regulatory license and tariff approvals (regulatory risk, 49.1.6); (14) change-in-law and stabilization provisions and the legal limits on them (protection actually available); (15) litigation, arbitration and regulatory proceedings (contingent liabilities); (16) insolvency law summary for the project company (enforcement); (17) treaty position of each sponsor's holding entity (protection, Ch 54); (18) dispute resolution clauses across contracts (consistency); (19) sanctions and anti-corruption representations already given (integrity, 49.6); (20) the local counsel's engagement and its reliance terms (who stands behind local-law opinions); (21) the form of every legal opinion and its assumptions (49.1.5); (22) court and registry practice notes on pledges of receivables (enforceability in fact, not only in law); (23) environmental permits and E&S legal requirements (Ch 50 overlap); (24) employment and immigration rules for expatriate staff (operations); (25) public procurement record of the award (legitimacy of the contract, integrity); (26) list of documents not yet available (outstanding items).

Exhibit 49.8 (`exh:49.8`, "Insurance due diligence request list for the lenders' insurance advisor (Illustrative)"), 20 items: (1) construction and operating insurance programs with policy wordings (cover in fact); (2) schedule of sums insured with replacement-value basis (adequacy); (3) DSU and BI daily indemnity, deductibles and indemnity periods (Example 49.2); (4) critical-spare list with lead times (indemnity period); (5) loss history of the plant and the technology (insurability and premium); (6) insurer and reinsurer security ratings (49.2.3); (7) fronting and reinsurance arrangements and the cut-through (local law); (8) broker's letter of undertaking in draft (ssec:27.6.3); (9) loss-payee, non-vitiation and lender endorsements (Ch 27); (10) the insurance covenant in the draft finance documents (fit); (11) market availability evidence for each required cover (`t-insurance-market`); (12) political risk and credit insurance policies, if any (Ch 27, Ch 60); (13) contractor-arranged covers and their interfaces with owner covers (gaps); (14) the risk register and the uninsurable risks (residual risk); (15) natural catastrophe and climate exposure studies (sums insured and deductibles); (16) cyber cover and exclusions (ssec:27.3.4); (17) claims procedures and authority to settle (lender control); (18) premium budget in the model (cost); (19) renewal calendar and the lenders' approval rights (annual test); (20) the advisor's previous reports on the project, if any (continuity).

Exhibit 49.9 (`exh:49.9`, "Model audit request list (Illustrative)"), 18 items: (1) the financial model in native format with all sheets unlocked (audit access); (2) the model book or user guide (intended logic); (3) the input book with a source for every input (reconciliation); (4) the term sheet and draft finance documents (debt terms to test); (5) the project contracts' payment terms (revenue and cost logic); (6) the IE, market and insurance advisors' reports (technical inputs); (7) the tax advisor's memo and rulings (tax logic); (8) the accounting policies assumed (lenders' reporting basis); (9) the scenarios and sensitivities required by the lenders (run book); (10) the materiality threshold and reporting format agreed in the engagement letter (ssec:44.2.1 applied, 49.3.2); (11) version history and change log (control); (12) prior audit reports on earlier versions (closing out findings); (13) the macro and circularity approach, which must be macro-free for supplied workbooks or with a documented iteration switch (stability); (14) the hedging and base-rate inputs with sources (interest cost); (15) the funding plan and drawdown profile (IDC and fees); (16) the sizing and covenant definitions as drafted (ratios); (17) the final close run's instructions and timetable (bring-down); (18) the reliance and liability terms requested by lenders (49.3.3).

Exhibit 49.10 (`exh:49.10`, "Tax and accounting due diligence request list (Illustrative)"), 20 items: (1) tax registrations and filings for five years (compliance); (2) incentive certificates, holidays and their conditions (value and condition-subsequent risk); (3) withholding tax analysis for interest, fees and dividends with treaty eligibility evidence (gross-up, Example 49.4; rules at ssec:7.11.4); (4) thin capitalization and interest-limitation computations (disallowance); (5) loss carryforward schedule with expiry (cash tax timing); (6) VAT registration, recovery history and refund lag (working capital, ssec:67.7.2); (7) transfer-pricing documentation for related-party O&M, LTSA and management fees (adjustment risk); (8) stamp, registration and notarial duties on security and transfers (closing cost); (9) tax rulings and their scope (certainty); (10) open audits and disputes (contingent liabilities); (11) the holding structure and its substance (treaty access, Ch 67); (12) Pillar Two exposure of the sponsors' groups (top-up tax, Ch 67); (13) indirect transfer tax exposure on a later sale (Ch 63, Ch 67); (14) audited accounts and management letters for three years (accounting quality); (15) accounting policies for revenue, concession arrangements and decommissioning (lenders' reporting basis against IFRS, Ch 66); (16) quality-of-earnings analysis for operating assets (cash against profit); (17) related-party transactions list (leakage and pricing); (18) the model's tax sheet (reconciliation to this list); (19) tax covenants and indemnities proposed in the documents (49.4.3); (20) the tax advisor's reliance terms (who may rely).

Exhibit 49.11 (`exh:49.11`, "Counterparty credit due diligence request list (Illustrative)"), 20 items: (1) audited accounts of the offtaker for five years (capacity); (2) monthly collections, billing and cash data for 24 months (cash, not income; Example 49.5); (3) payables to other generators and fuel suppliers by age (arrears pattern); (4) retail tariff history and cost-recovery analysis (willingness and capacity to pay); (5) government transfers and subsidies to the offtaker (dependence on the budget); (6) the payment-security package: LC, guarantee, escrow (support, Ch 59); (7) the offtaker's ratings or credit assessments (benchmarks); (8) disputes and arbitrations with other IPPs (willingness); (9) EPC contractor accounts, backlog and margin trends (contractor credit); (10) parent guarantees and bonds with the guarantors' accounts (substitutes for credit); (11) LTSA provider and O&M operator accounts (replaceability); (12) fuel supplier's reserves, deliverability and accounts (supply credit, Ch 48); (13) hedge counterparties' ratings and the rating triggers in the hedges (replacement); (14) account bank ratings and replacement provisions (cash safety); (15) insurers' ratings (49.2.3); (16) sponsors' accounts and their equity commitment support (equity risk); (17) concentration of exposures to one group across roles (wrong-way risk); (18) political and sovereign context for state-owned counterparties (Ch 59); (19) each counterparty's sanctions and integrity screen (49.6); (20) the counterparty credit card for each counterparty (Framework 49.2).

Exhibit 49.12 (`exh:49.12`, "KYC, sanctions, and integrity due diligence request list (Illustrative)"), 22 items: (1) certified constitutional documents of the borrower, sponsors and guarantors (identity); (2) ownership charts to natural persons with percentages and voting rights (beneficial ownership, Framework 49.3); (3) identity documents of beneficial owners and controllers (verification); (4) PEP declarations for owners, directors and their families and associates (49.6.1); (5) source of funds and source of wealth for equity (legitimacy); (6) directors and senior management lists (screening scope); (7) screening results against every applicable list for every node (49.6.2); (8) control rights: board appointment, veto and management agreements (UK control test); (9) agents, consultants and intermediaries used in development, with contracts and payments (anti-corruption); (10) joint-venture partners and their own ownership (associated persons); (11) the procurement record of the award (how the contract was won; `odebrecht-2016`); (12) payments to public officials or state entities outside contracts (red flags); (13) the sponsors' anti-bribery and sanctions compliance programs (adequate and reasonable procedures, sec:60.6); (14) adverse media searches with dates (reputation); (15) litigation and regulatory enforcement history (conduct); (16) subcontractors and suppliers above a stated value (screening reach); (17) hedge counterparties and account banks (screening reach); (18) goods and equipment origin for export controls (sanctions); (19) charitable and community payments (disguised benefits); (20) the integrity report of any DFI co-lender (consistency); (21) refresh schedule for signing, close and ownership changes (Framework 49.3 step 5); (22) representations, covenants and information undertakings proposed (49.6.4).

Red flags:
- A legal opinion with an assumption that the very issue in doubt is valid.
- A government guarantee issued without evidence of the legal ceiling and approvals.
- Insurance with a single local insurer and no reinsurance cut-through.
- A utility whose cash flow statement shows growing payables to other generators.
- An agent paid a success fee on the award of a government contract.
- A sponsor that cannot or will not identify its ultimate beneficial owners.

Rules of thumb and their limits:
- The longest-lead critical spare sets the minimum BI indemnity period; spares pooling arrangements can shorten it only if contractually committed.
- Withholding gross-ups on interest are a lender market standard; the cost sits with the project company and must be modeled.
- A state-owned offtaker's willingness to pay tracks its retail tariff's cost recovery; a frozen tariff is an early warning.

Common mistakes:
- Treating a clean legal opinion as a clean legal position.
- Reading insurance for perils covered and not for exclusions and deductibles.
- Accepting a model audit sign-off that excludes tax.
- Screening the borrower but not its subcontractors, agents and hedge counterparties.

Questions experts ask:
- What would a local judge do with this pledge in an insolvency?
- Which permit, if lost, stops the plant, and who can revoke it?
- How many days of outage can this project carry before the DSRA is empty?
- Who introduced the sponsor to the ministry, and how were they paid?

## 49.12 Judgment drill

Situation: You are head of compliance at the mandated lead arranger for the Port-Valombe container terminal concession in the fictional Republic of Valombe (Illustrative; a USD 318.7 million financing with a DFI co-lender). On Monday, April 12, 2027, two weeks before the planned signing, adverse media reports that the local sponsor's chairman's brother-in-law is a deputy minister in the transport ministry that awarded the concession. The sponsor confirms the relationship, says the brother-in-law recused himself from the tender, and offers board minutes. The DFI co-lender's integrity unit asks for a two-month investigation; the sponsor says the government's guarantee approval lapses in six weeks.

Reasoning outline: classify the issue (PEP relationship, not evidence of bribery); apply Framework 49.1 (severity high if procurement was tainted; likelihood depends on the tender's record); what evidence would satisfy you (tender evaluation report, recusal records, independent review of payments to agents); what structure mitigates (enhanced representations and audit rights, an independent integrity monitor, cure-and-exit rights); timing (seek a short extension of the guarantee approval rather than shortcut the review). Expert answer: do not sign until a scoped, time-boxed integrity review (two to three weeks) is complete; ask the government for a short extension; document the decision.

What would change the answer: evidence that the deputy minister participated in evaluation (walk away or require re-tender); a transparent, competitive tender with published evaluation (moves toward signing with enhanced covenants); a sponsor that refuses audit rights (walk).

## 49.13 Exercise set (17)

Tier 1 (4):
- 49.1: List what a capacity and authority opinion covers and what it does not.
- 49.2: Explain why the indemnity period, not the sum insured, often decides whether BI cover protects lenders.
- 49.3: Explain the difference between the OFAC 50% rule and the UK ownership-or-control test, citing `t-anticorruption-sanctions`.
- 49.4: Distinguish a model audit's logic review from its input reconciliation, and say which chapter owns each part of the engagement (R-140).

Tier 2 (8):
- 49.5: Rework Example 49.1 (Pantabangan Sun Power) with a loan final maturity of March 31, 2041; regrade the findings. Answer: the test date becomes March 31, 2043; the water permit (2037) is short by about six years, still with discretionary renewal (medium; same cure); the foreshore permit (2032) is short by about 11 years but renewable as a matter of course (low); the leases (2051), generation authority and grid agreement (2052) clear the test; the three unregistered leases remain a high-severity perfection gap with a CP cure, because term does not cure perfection.
- 49.6: Example 49.2 (Mekong Delta Power): find the daily indemnity and indemnity period that make the 486-day transformer outage loss fully covered after the 60-day deductible. Answer: daily indemnity at least USD 208,493 (debt service plus fixed costs over 365); indemnity period at least 486 days, 16 months at 30.4 days a month, counting the deductible inside the period as the policy does; recovery 426 days × USD 208,493 = USD 88.82 million against a loss of USD 101.33 million, the gap being the 60-day deductible (USD 12.51 million), which the DSRA must carry. Common wrong answer: adding the deductible on top of a 16-month period.
- 49.7: Example 49.3 (Northbay Health Partners): a second error, opex escalation applied twice, overstates opex by CAD 0.6 million a year from year 6. Compute its effect alone and combined with the first. Answers: correcting the second error alone raises sculpted debt by CAD 1.84 million (to CAD 324.75 million); with both corrections debt is CAD 318.83 million, CAD 4.07 million (1.26%) below the modeled CAD 322.91 million. Lesson: errors offset, so each must be reported on its own as well as combined. Common wrong answer: netting the two before testing each against materiality.
- 49.8: Example 49.4 (Tema Bay Power): compute the gross-up if withholding is 15% and the lenders refuse to rely on treaty relief, and the disallowance if net interest falls to USD 24.0 million. Answers: gross-up cost USD 3.247 million a year (18.4/0.85 − 18.4); interest limit USD 22.38 million; disallowed USD 1.62 million; extra tax USD 0.405 million a year.
- 49.9: Example 49.5 (SNEV): compute the LC's life if the collection rate falls to 74%. Answers: cash available for IPPs USD 30.99 million a month; coverage 0.717; total shortfall USD 12.21 million a month; the project's pro rata shortfall USD 2.09 million a month; the USD 14.8 million LC lasts about 7.1 months (from 10.7).
- 49.10: Trace this four-level chart (all fictional, June 2026) under the OFAC 50% rule and state whether each entity is blocked: Person P (designated) owns 60% of Q Holdings; Q Holdings owns 45% of R Ltd; Person S (designated) owns 10% of R Ltd directly; T Investments (no designated owners) owns the other 45% of R Ltd; R Ltd owns 50% of U Services, the project's spare-parts supplier; the other 50% of U Services is owned by an unconnected listed company. Answers: Q Holdings blocked (60% by P); R Ltd blocked (45% via blocked Q plus 10% by S, 55% in aggregate); U Services blocked (50% owned by blocked R, and the rule is "50% or more"); T Investments not blocked. Under the UK test, R Ltd is not caught by simple aggregation of Q's and S's holdings unless they act under a joint arrangement, but Q's 45% with P's control of Q is examined for control; plan to the strictest regime (Framework 49.3). Common wrong answer: treating U Services as clear because no designated person owns it directly.
- 49.11: Draft an anti-corruption representation and an information covenant for the Port-Valombe facility agreement (the drill's deal); annotate (Illustrative clause, English law).
- 49.12: Draft five questions to the insurance advisor on the Mekong Delta Power program (Example 49.2), each tied to an item in Exhibit 49.8.

Tier 3 (5):
- 49.13: Case P: using Annex P 4.2 and the Case Bible's Documentation and law, Land and Government Guarantee inputs, build the red-flag grid Laurent's team should have produced (Framework 49.1: severity, likelihood, cure route, owner, residual risk for all fifteen findings), including the cure for finding 8, the OREK-approved payment plan that can suspend an LC replenishment default (Case Bible Section 4.1, Adaeze). Model answer: a fifteen-row grid matching Exhibit 49.6 with a cure for each amber finding.
- 49.14: Case P: draft the KYC request list for Groupe Talmé (using Exhibit 49.12 as the template) and explain each item against the facts of Annex P 2.4.
- 49.15: Model task (macro-free): add a tax-leakage check sheet to the Case P model (`model/Case_P_Model.xlsx`, Tax sheet layout of Ch 41) that compares modeled withholding, gross-up and thin-cap disallowance with a diligence schedule entered as inputs, with a check row that flags any difference above USD 0.05 million in a period. Answer key gives the rows and formulas; values come from the workbook (P-F38 for thin cap).
- 49.16: Real case: using `sanctions-2022`, write a 300-word memo on which signals between 2017 and 2021 should have changed a lender's view of Nord Stream 2's credit, and what diligence and documents could have done.
- 49.17: Write the lenders' legal counsel scope letter for Sine-Saloum Power (the walkthrough's civil-law IPP in Senegal): scope by workstream (corporate, contracts, permits and land, security and the security agent structure under OHADA, government support, FX, tax, litigation), the split between international and Dakar counsel, the deliverables (red-flag report at credit approval, full report at signing, legal opinions as CPs, bring-down at financial close) dated against a close calendar (mandate January 15, 2027; credit approval March 26; signing April 30; financial close June 11), reliance and the liability cap, conflicts statement, and fee basis. Full model answer: a one-page letter with each paragraph annotated (capabilities review fix).

## 49.15 Glossary entries owned

Entries follow `bible/glossary-canon.md`. Removed by ruling and used here without bold, with a cross-reference: broker's letter of undertaking (ssec:27.6.3), materiality threshold (ssec:44.2.1), tax leakage (ssec:67.1.1) (R-121). The canon also carries "50 percent rule" at ssec:60.7.1 (Ch 60's law entry) beside this chapter's "OFAC 50 percent rule" (screening entry); see the Central changes list.

| Term | Abbreviation | Definition | Home section |
|---|---|---|---|
| legal due diligence report | – | Lenders' counsel's report on the legal validity, enforceability and risks of a project's corporate structure, contracts, permits, land and security. | 49.1.2 |
| red-flag report | – | A diligence report limited to material adverse findings, without a full description of each document. | 49.1.2 |
| red-flag grid | – | The book's framework for grading each diligence finding by severity, likelihood and cure route. | 49.1.2 |
| legal opinion | – | A law firm's formal letter to lenders on specified legal matters, such as capacity, enforceability and choice of law, given on stated assumptions and qualifications. | 49.1.5 |
| qualification (in an opinion) | – | A stated limit on a legal opinion's conclusion, such as insolvency laws or court discretion. | 49.1.5 |
| reasoned opinion | – | A legal opinion that sets out the analysis and the opinion-giver's view where the law is unsettled, instead of an unqualified conclusion. | 49.1.5 |
| lenders' insurance advisor | – | The adviser who reviews a project's insurance program for the lenders against the finance documents and the project's risks. | 49.2.1 |
| model audit sign-off | – | The model auditor's letter confirming, within a stated scope, that the financial model computes as described and reflects the documents. | 49.3.2 |
| gross-up | – | An obligation to increase a payment so that the recipient receives the full amount after a deduction such as withholding tax. | 49.4.2 |
| counterparty credit card | – | The book's one-page framework for assessing a counterparty's ability and willingness to perform. | 49.5.4 |
| know your customer | KYC | The checks a financial institution must make to verify the identity, ownership, control and source of funds of its customers. | 49.6.1 |
| beneficial owner | – | A natural person who ultimately owns or controls a legal entity. | 49.6.1 |
| politically exposed person | PEP | A person who holds or has held a prominent public function, or a family member or close associate of such a person. | 49.6.1 |
| sanctions screening | – | Checking parties and their owners against sanctions lists and ownership rules before and during a transaction. | 49.6.2 |
| OFAC 50 percent rule | – | Applied here in screening, not owned: the canon entry is "50 percent rule" (home ssec:60.7.1, R-088), with "OFAC 50 percent rule" as its recorded synonym (consolidation 2026-10-03). | applied (home ssec:60.7.1) |
| ownership trace | – | The book's framework for mapping ownership and control to natural persons and applying each sanctions regime's tests. | 49.6.2 |
| integrity due diligence | IDD | Investigation of the reputation, conduct and connections of sponsors, partners and agents, focused on corruption, fraud and sanctions risk. | 49.6.3 |

## 49.16 Neighbors

Before: Chapter 48, Technical, resource, and market diligence. Sets out how diligence is organized (the diligence scope matrix, scope letters, reliance and duty of care) and the report challenge protocol, then teaches the independent engineer's work (technology, capex and contingency, schedule and delay probability, operating assumptions), resource and reserves diligence (yield loss chains and uncertainty, hydrology and geothermal, reserves and the reserve tail test), market price curves, and traffic and demand studies, with a request list for each workstream. Case P: Gwen Treharne's May 30, 2018 IE report; Case T: Pellow's and Ridgeway's traffic studies.

After: Chapter 50, Environmental and social risk and standards. How E&S failure becomes credit risk; the IFC Performance Standards, the Equator Principles (EP4), the World Bank ESF and the OECD Common Approaches; the ESIA, ESMP and ESAP and how they become conditions and covenants; community engagement and grievance; land acquisition and resettlement; indigenous peoples and FPIC; biodiversity; labor and human rights; the E&S request list. Case P: the resettlement of 214 households at Bélanou, the twelve-item ESAP, the Category A review and the 2021 fishers' grievance.

- R-141: sec:50.1 "Why ..." made declarative; serial commas in list headings (sec:50.6, ssec:50.7.2, sec:50.8, ssec:50.8.2).

## 50.1 Header

| Item | Value |

| Chapter | 50 |

| Label | `ch:50` |

## 50.2 Purpose and capabilities

Purpose. Projects are stopped by people as often as by engineering: by communities who were moved without fair compensation, by indigenous peoples who were not consulted, by protests that close roads, by courts that annul permits, and by lenders who withdraw when a project becomes a campaign. This chapter teaches the reader to treat environmental and social (E&S) risk as credit risk: to know the rulebooks lenders apply (the IFC Performance Standards, the Equator Principles and the World Bank Environmental and Social Framework), to read the documents that implement them (ESIA, ESMP, ESAP), to cost and schedule resettlement and biodiversity commitments, to understand FPIC and labor and human-rights obligations, and to turn findings into conditions and covenants that let lenders act before failure.

What you will be able to do:

- Explain how E&S failure becomes default risk and price an E&S delay against contingency and debt service (Capabilities 7, 2).
- Determine which E&S standards apply to a financing and what each requires, including EP4 scope and categorization (Capabilities 7, 8).
- Read an ESIA and ESAP, issue an E&S request list, build a resettlement budget and a biodiversity offset test, and convert findings into conditions precedent and covenants (Capabilities 7, 6).
- Advise a sponsor and lenders on community engagement, FPIC and grievance mechanisms (Capabilities 7, 15).
