## 50.4 Locked table of contents

Opening (no heading). `\section*{What you will be able to do}`.

**50.1 Environmental and social failure as credit risk** (`sec:50.1`; retitled, R-141)

- 50.1.1 How E&S failure reaches the lenders (`ssec:50.1.1`). Framework 50.1 (E&S-to-credit transmission map): permit challenge or annulment, community blockade and construction delay, cost overrun from added compensation, offtaker or government withdrawal, lender default triggers and disbursement suspension, reputational exit of co-lenders, and refinancing exclusion. Exhibit 50.1 as a TikZ flow diagram.
- 50.1.2 Pricing an E&S delay (`ssec:50.1.2`). Example 50.1 (Illustrative 210 MW wind farm in Kutch district, Gujarat, India, October to December 2026): cost of a 63-day community blockade against contingency.
- 50.1.3 Cobre Panamá and compliance without legitimacy (`ssec:50.1.3`). Real case section from `cobre-panama`: Law 406 approved October 20, 2023 after three days of debate; nationwide protests and blockades; Supreme Court declared it unconstitutional on November 28, 2023; mine in preservation since November 2023; the SGS integral audit published by the environment ministry on June 19, 2026 (overall 87.7% compliance; three non-compliances on reforestation programs suspended since 2023); 30-day public consultation that did not secure consent. Lesson: technical compliance with ESIA and ESMP commitments does not secure the social license; lenders' E&S diligence must test legitimacy of the approval process. (Verification flag: the fact sheet's "361 of 370 fulfilled" and "seven partial, three non-compliances" do not sum to 370; writer prints only "overall 87.7%" and the three reforestation non-compliances unless the sheet is corrected.)

**50.2 The rulebooks lenders apply** (`sec:50.2`)

- 50.2.1 The IFC Performance Standards (`ssec:50.2.1`). PS1 to PS8 (2012 edition, effective January 1, 2012; IFC update under way, consultation expected from late 2026), the mitigation hierarchy (avoid, minimize, compensate or offset), ESMS. Exhibit 50.2: the eight standards with what each requires and its usual cost or schedule item.
- 50.2.2 The Equator Principles (`ssec:50.2.2`). EP4 (published July 2020; implementation by October 1, 2020; 126 signatories as of October 2026 per the EP website, cited with its date): five in-scope products and thresholds (project finance advisory and project finance at USD 10 million or more of total project capital cost; project-related corporate loans meeting three tests: majority of proceeds to a project under the client's effective operational control, total aggregate loan and the EPFI's individual commitment before syndication each at least USD 50 million, tenor at least two years; bridge loans under two years to be refinanced by in-scope finance; project-related refinance and acquisition finance of EP-financed projects before project completion with no material change); Categories A, B, C; Designated versus Non-Designated Countries (OECD member and World Bank high income); Principles 1 to 10 in outline (IESC review, covenants, monitoring, reporting); FPIC in all countries where PS7 triggers; climate change risk assessment triggers (Category A, and Scope 1 plus 2 above 100,000 t CO2e a year, which also triggers public annual GHG reporting) with a forward reference to sec:84.6 for the assessment method (R-091). Framework 50.2 (applicability tree) and Example 50.2 (six deals tested, 2026); Example 50.3 (emissions thresholds). Never give a count of Designated Countries without the EP website's date (`t-es-standards` "Do not state").
- 50.2.3 The World Bank Environmental and Social Framework (`ssec:50.2.3`). Approved August 4, 2016, applies to IPF projects initiated from October 1, 2018; ESS1 to ESS10; High, Substantial, Moderate, Low risk classes; the legally binding ESCP; applies to the borrowing government, not to IFC or MIGA.
- 50.2.4 Export credit agencies and the OECD Common Approaches (`ssec:50.2.4`). This chapter is the home (R-090). From `t-oecd-common-approaches`: the full title (Recommendation of the Council on Common Approaches for Officially Supported Export Credits and Environmental and Social Due Diligence, OECD/LEGAL/0393); a Council Recommendation, a political commitment and not a binding Decision, separate from the Arrangement that sets financial terms (Ch 29); adopted for the environment in 2003, revised 2007, readopted under the current name on June 28, 2012, revised 2016 and amended March 2024 (minor revisions, in the OECD's words); screening of every application with a repayment term of two years or more; classification A, B or C for projects in or near sensitive areas or where the ECA's share is SDR 10 million or more; benchmarking of Category A and B projects against all eight IFC Performance Standards and the World Bank Group EHS Guidelines by default since 2024 (with the World Bank ESS for sovereign obligors); public disclosure of Category A ESIAs at least 30 calendar days before final commitment; ECAs in a club financing exchange information to reach common classification. Before 2024 the text was built around the World Bank Safeguard Policies, with the IFC Performance Standards used for limited-recourse project finance (an inference from the OECD's note; say so). Case P link (Annex P 4.3): Exportgarant applied the 2016 revision and classified Bélanou Category A.
- 50.2.5 One action plan for several lenders (`ssec:50.2.5`). How the arranger and the IESC harmonize IFC PS, EP4, ESF and ECA requirements into one ESAP; what happens where they differ (the strictest applicable requirement usually governs).

**50.3 The assessment and management documents** (`sec:50.3`)

- 50.3.1 The ESIA (`ssec:50.3.1`). Scoping, baseline studies (and their seasons), impact assessment, analysis of alternatives, cumulative impact assessment, disclosure. Reading an ESIA with Framework 48.2 (cross-reference Ch 48). Chad-Cameroon pointer: Inspection Panel findings on cumulative impact assessment and indigenous peoples as the two most frequent gaps in pipeline E&S work (from `chad-cameroon`).
- 50.3.2 ESMS and ESMP (`ssec:50.3.2`). The management system, the management plans by topic (construction, labor, traffic, biodiversity, community health and safety, security), and the contractor's management plans flowed down through the EPC contract (cross-reference Ch 22).
- 50.3.3 The ESAP and how it becomes conditions and covenants (`ssec:50.3.3`). Gaps turned into dated actions with owners and evidence; which actions become CPs to first drawdown, which become drawdown conditions, which become covenants; Clause 50.1 (Illustrative E&S covenant with cure period, CTA) annotated. Forward reference Ch 51 for covenant drafting generally.
- 50.3.4 The independent E&S consultant and monitoring (`ssec:50.3.4`). IESC role at financing and during construction and operations; the IESC's terms of reference (scope against the applicable standards, site visits, deliverables dated against the close calendar, monitoring frequency, reliance) as the instrument that sets the review (practiced in Exercise 50.16); Panels of Experts on Category A projects; Nam Theun 2's Panel of Experts and the two-year extension of the resettlement implementation period (from `nam-theun-2`).

**50.4 Stakeholder engagement and grievance** (`sec:50.4`)

- 50.4.1 Stakeholder engagement plans and disclosure (`ssec:50.4.1`). Identifying affected communities and other stakeholders, timing and language of disclosure, informed consultation and participation; what "meaningful" looks like in practice.
- 50.4.2 Grievance mechanisms that work (`ssec:50.4.2`). Design (accessible, no cost, no retaliation, escalation, tracking), logs as early-warning data for lenders, and the link to the transmission map (a rising grievance count is a lead indicator of blockade risk).

**50.5 Land acquisition and resettlement** (`sec:50.5`)

- 50.5.1 PS5 principles (`ssec:50.5.1`). Avoid or minimize displacement; physical and economic displacement; compensation at full replacement cost; livelihood restoration to at least pre-project levels; RAP versus LRP; government-led land acquisition and the project's supplementary obligations.
- 50.5.2 Building a resettlement budget (`ssec:50.5.2`). Example 50.4 (Illustrative 86 households displaced by the reservoir of a 120 MW hydro plant in Central Province, Zambia, RAP budget of 2026): line-by-line budget at full replacement cost with contingency. Exhibit 50.3.
- 50.5.3 Resettlement and the construction schedule (`ssec:50.5.3`). Sequencing land access with the EPC schedule; Nam Theun 2 lesson (first wave of 600 families moved to temporary housing in 2006, two years earlier than planned and before permanent housing was ready; E&S costs well above appraisal); how lenders set land-access CPs.

**50.6 Indigenous peoples and free, prior, and informed consent** (`sec:50.6`)

- 50.6.1 When PS7 applies (`ssec:50.6.1`). Identification criteria in outline; ICP in all cases; FPIC triggers (impacts on lands and natural resources under traditional ownership or customary use; relocation; significant impacts on critical cultural heritage; commercial use of cultural heritage).
- 50.6.2 What FPIC requires and what it does not (`ssec:50.6.2`). Process, documentation, good-faith negotiation; PS7 says FPIC does not necessarily require unanimity; EP4 says it confers no veto on individuals or sub-groups; qualified independent consultant's evaluation under EP4.
- 50.6.3 FPIC in high-income countries under EP4 (`ssec:50.6.3`). EP4's extension of FPIC to Designated Countries and its origin in criticism of North American pipeline cases (attributed to law-firm commentary per the fact sheet).

**50.7 Biodiversity** (`sec:50.7`)

- 50.7.1 Habitat classification and critical habitat (`ssec:50.7.1`). Modified, natural, critical; the PS6 paragraph 17 conditions for proceeding in critical habitat.
- 50.7.2 Offsets, no net loss, and net gain (`ssec:50.7.2`). Example 50.5 (Illustrative: the Example 50.4 reservoir floods 312 ha of natural miombo woodland; offset site in the same Zambian district, 2026): habitat-hectare accounting with a multiplier; Equation 50.1.
- 50.7.3 Bujagali and an offset undone by the host government's own project (`ssec:50.7.3`). Real case from `bujagali`: the Kalagala Falls offset protected only by the 2007 Indemnity Agreement with IDA, threatened by the government's Isimba dam, redefined in January 2018; second Inspection Panel report (August 2008) finding non-compliance with five policies. Lesson: an offset needs legal protection that outlasts the loan and a check against the host government's own project pipeline.

**50.8 Labor, safety, security, and human rights** (`sec:50.8`)

- 50.8.1 Labor and working conditions (`ssec:50.8.1`). PS2: terms of employment, worker accommodation, child and forced labor bars, worker grievance, and primary supply chain risk where high; what lenders ask of the EPC contractor and its subcontractors.
- 50.8.2 Community health, safety, and security (`ssec:50.8.2`). PS4: traffic, hazardous materials, influx, security forces and their conduct; Bujagali's two fatalities in 2010 leading lenders to require occupational health and safety audits.
- 50.8.3 Human rights due diligence (`ssec:50.8.3`). EP4's reference to the UN Guiding Principles; how human-rights risks are scoped and reported.
- 50.8.4 Cultural heritage (`ssec:50.8.4`). PS8 in outline; chance-finds procedures; Bujagali's spiritual values issue.

**50.9 Compliance certified and outcome failed in Chad and Cameroon** (`sec:50.9`). Real case section from `chad-cameroon`: 19-volume EMP; ECMG Certificate of Compliance in October 2004; International Advisory Group; IEG rated the overall program outcome unsatisfactory (lack of government ownership); revenue-management law amended in December 2005; World Bank exit in September 2008 after prepayment. Lesson: lenders' E&S monitoring measures the project's commitments, not the host government's; tie development outcomes the project cannot control to separate instruments (also the IEG lesson from NT2 to separate measurable safeguard compliance from stretch development targets).

**50.10 Walkthrough: turning an ESAP into conditions precedent and covenants** (`sec:50.10`).

**50.11 Case P: resettlement at Bélanou and the Category A review** (`sec:50.11`).

**50.12 Practitioner's notebook** (`sec:50.12`). Includes Exhibit 50.6, the E&S due diligence request list (see 50.11 below). **50.13 Judgment drill** (`sec:50.13`).

**50.14 Every diligence finding has to land in a document** (`sec:50.14`). Close. Crystallizes: E&S risk reaches lenders through identifiable channels; the standards turn obligations into an action plan; resettlement and biodiversity commitments have costs and schedules like any other; the legitimacy of a project is diligence too. Last paragraph poses the problem Part X answers: diligence across Chapters 48 to 50 produced findings, cures and action plans; Ch 51 shows the documents that hold them, from the term sheet to the common terms agreement's conditions, representations, covenants and events of default.

**50.15 Exercises** (`sec:50.15`). **50.16 Solutions to exercises** (`sec:50.16`). `\section*{Sources}`.

## 50.5 Opening

Real deal moment from `cobre-panama`: on November 28, 2023, Panama's Supreme Court unanimously declared Law 406, the contract under which the Cobre Panamá copper mine operated, unconstitutional, after weeks of nationwide protests and road and port blockades that cut supplies to the mine's power plant; the mine, which had produced about 350,000 metric tons of copper in 2022, went into preservation. On June 19, 2026 the environment ministry published an independent integral audit by SGS that scored the mine's environmental-commitment compliance at 87.7% overall. Open on the audit score set against the shutdown; the chapter's question is how a project that meets most of its written commitments can still lose the right to operate, and what lenders measure to see it coming.

## 50.6 Worked examples

**Example 50.1 What a community blockade costs (Illustrative)** (`ex:50.1`, in 50.1.2)

- Scenario: Kutch Coastal Wind Pvt Ltd (fictional), a 210 MW wind farm under construction in Kutch district, Gujarat, India, with an Indian EPC contractor (fictional) and a 25-year PPA with a state distribution company (unnamed; terms Illustrative); the senior debt is a USD loan from two DFIs (so the example runs in USD). From October 12 to December 13, 2026, a dispute over access-road compensation leads villagers to block the site for 63 days during turbine erection.
- Inputs: senior debt drawn USD 148.6 million at an all-in 7.10%, so daily interest during construction USD 28,906; EPC contractor's standby charges USD 38,500 a day (the blockade is an employer risk under the EPC contract); owner's costs USD 11,200 a day; PPA scheduled COD float 30 days, then delay LDs to the offtaker of USD 41,700 a day; remaining construction contingency USD 9.8 million.
- Key results: daily cost USD 78,606; 63 days USD 4.952 million (50.5% of remaining contingency); PPA LDs for 33 days USD 1.376 million; total USD 6.328 million.
- Teaching points: the dispute that caused it would have cost a fraction to settle early (the writer may state that the access-road compensation claim totals USD 0.41 million, Illustrative); E&S contingency belongs inside construction contingency planning; an EPC contract that makes community blockades an employer risk pushes the cost to equity and lenders.

**Example 50.2 EP4 scope tests on six transactions (Illustrative)** (`ex:50.2`, in 50.2.2)

- Six transactions tested with Framework 50.2 (all Illustrative):
  All six are 2026 mandates of Banco Meridional (fictional), a Spanish EPFI.
  1. Project finance of USD 8.7 million total project capital cost for a rooftop solar portfolio in Morocco: out of scope (below the USD 10 million threshold).
  2. Project-related corporate loan of USD 63.8 million aggregate to a Mexican cement company, the bank's individual commitment USD 34.6 million before syndication, tenor 5 years, majority of proceeds to a kiln under the borrower's effective operational control: out of scope (the bank's individual commitment is below USD 50 million).
  3. Bridge loan of 18 months for a Peruvian wind farm, to be refinanced by in-scope project finance: in scope.
  4. Acquisition finance for a stake in an EP-financed Chilean solar plant after project completion: out of scope (acquisition finance is in scope only before project completion and with no material change).
  5. Project finance advisory mandate for a USD 238.5 million desalination project in Morocco: in scope.
  6. Project finance of USD 407.9 million for a toll road in Portugal, a Designated Country: in scope; Principle 3 applies host-country law, with the EPFI evaluating whether Performance Standards should guide specific risks; FPIC applies if PS7 special circumstances arise.

**Example 50.3 Emissions thresholds for a gas plant (Illustrative)** (`ex:50.3`, in 50.2.2)

- Scenario: Khulna South Power (fictional), a 318 MW CCGT in Khulna division, Bangladesh, seeking EPFI and ECA finance in 2026.
- Inputs: 318 MW, 60% capacity factor, emissions intensity 0.36 t CO2 per MWh (Illustrative).
- Key results: generation 1,671.4 GWh; emissions 601,707 t CO2 a year, above the PS3 quantification threshold (25,000 t CO2e) and above the EP4 threshold for a transition-risk climate assessment and public annual GHG reporting (100,000 t CO2e). Forward reference to Ch 84 for the climate risk assessment itself.

**Example 50.4 A resettlement budget at full replacement cost (Illustrative)** (`ex:50.4`, in 50.5.2)

- Scenario: Mulungushi Gorge Hydro Ltd (fictional), a 120 MW hydropower plant in Central Province, Zambia, financed by a DFI and two South African banks; its RAP (prepared in 2026 by a consultant, fictional) covers 86 households physically and economically displaced by the reservoir.
- Inputs and lines (USD): replacement housing 86 × 18,450 = 1,586,700; replacement agricultural land 86 households × 1.62 ha × 7,300 per ha = 1,017,036; crops and trees 86 × 2,140 = 184,040; transitional allowance 86 × 18 months × 165 = 255,420; livelihood restoration program 86 × 36 months × 95 = 294,120; support to 19 vulnerable households × 4,200 = 79,800; community facilities (replacement school, water points) 412,000; RAP implementation, monitoring and grievance 287,000.
- Key results: subtotal USD 4.116 million; contingency 15% USD 0.617 million; total USD 4.734 million; USD 55,041 per household.
- Teaching points: replacement cost, not book value or government schedule rates; the livelihood and transitional lines are where budgets fail; lenders test the budget against a census and asset inventory with a cut-off date.

**Example 50.5 Testing a biodiversity offset (Illustrative)** (`ex:50.5`, in 50.7.2)

- Scenario: the Mulungushi Gorge reservoir (Example 50.4) floods natural miombo woodland; the lenders' IESC tests the proposed offset on degraded woodland in the same district in 2026.
- Inputs: 312 ha of natural habitat lost at condition score 0.71; offset site 640 ha improved from 0.38 to 0.64; lenders require a gain of 1.5 times the loss (net gain plus an uncertainty multiplier).
- Equation 50.1 (`eq:50.1`): habitat units $= \text{area} \times \text{condition}$; gain $= \text{area} \times (c_{\text{after}} - c_{\text{before}})$.
- Key results: loss 221.5 habitat units; offset gain 166.4; requirement 332.3; shortfall 165.9 units, needing about 638 more hectares at the same uplift (or a larger uplift on the existing site).
- Teaching points: simplified metric (state that real metrics use species-specific and habitat-specific measures); offset size and permanence are cost and land items that must be in the budget and secured legally.

## 50.7 Walkthrough

**Walkthrough: turning an ESAP into conditions precedent and covenants** (Section 50.10). Artifact: an Illustrative 22-item ESAP agreed in 2026 for Khulna South Power (the Example 50.3 CCGT in Bangladesh), a Category A thermal plant with a 38 km gas pipeline corridor, financed by EPFIs, a DFI and an ECA. Steps:

1. Sort items by phase (before first drawdown, before land access, during construction, before COD, operations).
2. For each item, state the evidence that proves completion (document, IESC confirmation, census, signed agreements).
3. Assign each to a document mechanism: CP to signing, CP to first drawdown, condition to each drawdown, covenant with deadline, reporting obligation.
4. Set cure periods and consequences (drawstop, event of default after cure period) using Clause 50.1.
5. Map each to the monitoring calendar (IESC visits, quarterly E&S reports).
6. Identify items the project cannot control (government-led land acquisition, offset legal protection) and route them to the IA or a government undertaking.
7. Produce the lender-facing tracker; the chapter shows five rows filled in.

Clause (Illustrative): Clause 50.1 Environmental and social covenant with ESAP compliance and cure period, common terms agreement (`cl:50.1`), annotated paragraph by paragraph, with the sponsor's push (materiality qualifiers, longer cure) and the DFI's push (immediate drawstop on serious incidents) stated in annotations.

## 50.8 Frameworks

- **Framework 50.1: The E&S-to-credit transmission map** (`fw:es-credit-map`), original. Purpose: trace how an E&S failure becomes a loss to lenders. Steps: (1) name the E&S trigger (grievance, incident, permit challenge, campaign); (2) follow each channel (schedule, cost, revenue, legal right to operate, lender action, refinancing); (3) estimate size and timing on each channel; (4) identify the earliest indicator lenders can monitor (grievance log, IESC finding, court filing); (5) attach a contractual lever to each indicator (reporting, drawstop, covenant, cure). Demonstrated on Example 50.1 and the Case P installment.
- **Framework 50.2: The E&S applicability tree** (`fw:es-applicability`), original, built on EP4 scope rules. Purpose: decide which standards apply to a financing. Steps: (1) which lenders and their policies (EPFI, DFI applying PS, World Bank IPF, ECA); (2) EP4 product and threshold tests; (3) Designated or Non-Designated Country; (4) category; (5) triggers for FPIC, climate risk assessment, IESC review, public reporting; (6) harmonized standard. Demonstrated on Example 50.2.
- Established framework credited: the mitigation hierarchy (IFC Performance Standards).

## 50.9 Real cases

| Slug | Use | Lesson |
|---|---|---|
| `cobre-panama` | Opening; 50.1.3 | Compliance with written E&S commitments does not secure the social license; the legitimacy of the approval process is a diligence item |
| `t-es-standards` (topic sheet) | 50.2 throughout | The three rulebooks as layers; scope thresholds; FPIC without veto; IFC update as-of date |
| `t-oecd-common-approaches` (topic sheet) | 50.2.4, 50.2.5, Case P installment | ECAs bring IFC-based E&S standards into a financing through a non-binding OECD Recommendation; the 30-day disclosure rule and SDR 10 million trigger shape timetables |
| `nam-theun-2` | 50.3.4, 50.5.3 | Resettlement must be sequenced with the construction timetable; Panels of Experts gate close-out; separate measurable safeguards from development targets |
| `bujagali` | 50.7.3, 50.8.2, 50.8.4 | Offsets need legal protection beyond the loan and protection from the host government's own projects; safety incidents trigger lender audits; first-attempt collapse after Inspection Panel findings |
| `chad-cameroon` | 50.3.1, 50.9 | Monitoring certified compliance while the development outcome failed; cumulative impact and indigenous peoples are the usual gaps |

Observe each sheet's "do not state" list (for example: FPIC is not a veto; do not give a current count of Designated Countries without checking; do not call the 2024 Common Approaches changes a major overhaul, claim they are binding, or quote the 2016 benchmarking paragraph; do not state Cobre Panamá protest death tolls or that it was project-financed by banks; do not give a single NT2 debt figure or dam height).

Open request (in the unit's Open requests list): `cobre-panama` correction of the SGS audit counts (361 of 370 fulfilled, seven partial and three non-compliances sum to 371); until corrected the writer prints only "overall 87.7%" and the three reforestation non-compliances.

## 50.10 Running-case installment

**Section 50.11 Case P: resettlement at Bélanou and the Category A review.** Story dates 2017 to 2021 (stated in the first sentence as spanning development to construction). Characters: Thandeka Mabuza (ABDB, until her move to Lattimer in June 2021), Henrike Vosskamp (Exportgarant); Tomasz may appear; Adwoa Sarpong-Kumi (ABDB, on Case P from July 2021) may be named in the 2021 narration. Advisers named with roles only (Annex P 2.3): Whinmoor Environmental (ESIA and RAP lead), Lagune Environnement SARL (Kessaran baseline and consultation sub-consultant), Calder Hartmann's E&S team (lenders' E&S adviser within the IE engagement). Scenes:

1. Scene (ABDB, 2017): Thandeka ties E&S conditions to disbursement; she asks "What's the counterfactual?" (habit, once) when the sponsor proposes a cash-only compensation option for the 137 economically displaced households on the pipeline corridor; she fears a grievance turning into a campaign.
2. Scene (Exportgarant, 2018): Henrike classifies Bélanou as Category A under the OECD Common Approaches (2016 revision; Annex P 4.3). Her habit of citing OECD Arrangement provisions by number is not used here: the fact sheet `t-oecd-common-approaches` verifies only the 2024 text's paragraph numbers, not the 2016 revision's, so the writer cites no provision numbers in this scene.
3. Narration (2021): in March 2021 a fishers' cooperative from Bélanou lagoon complained, first through the project grievance mechanism and then to the ABDB's independent accountability mechanism, that the intake and outfall exclusion zone and the jetty had cut 96 fishing households off from inshore grounds, and that the RAP had excluded them because they held no land; the review (May to August 2021) upheld the complaint in part; the additional USD 3.27 million paid for a livelihood program for the 96 households (boats and engines, a new landing site with cold storage, and cash compensation for 18 months); these households are additional to the 214 (Annex P 4.3).

Figures (Case Bible inputs only; Part 6 row 50 and Annex P 4.3; Exhibit 50.5, `exh:50.5`, "Case P resettlement and E&S cost items (USD m) \casep"): resettlement of 214 households under a RAP prepared to the IFC Performance Standards, required by the ABDB, split as plant site (62 ha) 58 physically displaced, and pipeline lateral, access road and corridor 19 physically and 137 economically displaced only (77 physically and 137 economically displaced in total); RAP implementation USD 5.08 million in owner's costs, with the independent RAP completion audit done in September 2020; additional compensation after the grievance review USD 3.27 million (part of the 2018 to 2021 hard cost overrun; Ch 61 owns the overrun funding, so the narration states the amount and forward-references Ch 61). Other facts printable from Annex P 4.3: the twelve ESAP items agreed in March 2018 and monitored by the ABDB and Exportgarant; no indigenous peoples within the meaning of PS7; the site is modified habitat with patches of natural habitat (lagoon mangroves) and low-density olive ridley turtle nesting, assessed as natural, not critical, habitat, with a biodiversity action plan (turtle-sensitive lighting, a June to October beach construction exclusion, 12 hectares of mangrove offset planting); Castellan, Banque Raveau, Kaito Pacific, Hovland and Sterrenberg are EPFIs, the deal closed under EP III (2013) with Kessara a Non-Designated Country, so the IFC Performance Standards applied. The narration notes once that EP III, not EP4, governed this 2018 deal, and that EP4's changes (50.2.2) would apply to a deal mandated after October 1, 2020.

Narration applies Framework 50.1 (the grievance as an early indicator: the fishers' complaint reached the ABDB's accountability mechanism, a lender channel, before it became a cost) and the 50.10 walkthrough steps to the real twelve-item ESAP, mapping each item to a document mechanism (Exhibit 50.5 carries the cost items; the mapping is Exercise 50.12). BF-u10-7 is closed by Annex P 4.3.

## 50.11 Practitioner's notebook (outline)

Checklist:
- Applicable standards determined with Framework 50.2 and harmonized into one ESAP.
- ESIA baseline covers all seasons that matter; alternatives and cumulative impacts assessed.
- Resettlement census and cut-off date set; budget at full replacement cost with livelihood lines.
- FPIC triggers screened; independent evaluation where required.
- Habitat classification done; offset secured legally beyond loan life.
- Grievance mechanism operating before construction, with a log the lenders see.
- ESAP items mapped to CPs, drawdown conditions and covenants.
- Contractor E&S obligations flowed down in the EPC contract.
- The E&S request list (Exhibit 50.6) issued with the IESC's terms of reference and closed out before credit approval.

Exhibit 50.6 (`exh:50.6`, appended label; caption "Environmental and social due diligence request list (Illustrative)"). Home exhibit cited by matter 93's "due-diligence request lists" row. An `exhibit` table (No., Item requested, Why it is needed, Feeds) of 28 items for an IESC on a Category A project: (1) the ESIA with all annexes, baseline survey dates and seasons (completeness, 50.3.1); (2) the scoping report and terms of reference of the ESIA (what was left out); (3) analysis of alternatives (mitigation hierarchy); (4) cumulative impact assessment (Chad-Cameroon lesson); (5) the host-country approval and its conditions (legal status against lender standards); (6) the ESMS manual and organization chart (capacity, PS1); (7) ESMPs by topic and the contractor's management plans (implementation, 50.3.2); (8) the draft ESAP and any gap analysis against IFC PS, EP4, the ESF and the ECA's Common Approaches classification (harmonized standard, 50.2.5); (9) stakeholder engagement plan and consultation records with dates, languages and attendance (meaningful consultation, 50.4.1); (10) grievance mechanism procedure and the log since inception (lead indicator, 50.4.2); (11) land acquisition plan, census with cut-off date and asset inventory (eligibility, PS5); (12) RAP or LRP with the budget at full replacement cost (Example 50.4); (13) compensation payment records against land-entry dates (sequencing, 50.5.3); (14) livelihood restoration monitoring indicators (outcomes, not outputs); (15) indigenous peoples screening and, where triggered, the ICP or FPIC record and the independent consultant's evaluation (PS7, EP4); (16) biodiversity baseline, habitat classification and critical habitat assessment (PS6, 50.7.1); (17) offset design, land tenure and legal protection beyond the loan (Example 50.5, `bujagali` lesson); (18) labor management plan, worker accommodation standards and contractor labor audits (PS2); (19) supply-chain risk screening for primary suppliers (forced and child labor); (20) occupational health and safety statistics for comparable work and the contractor's record (PS2, PS4); (21) security arrangements and the code of conduct for security forces (PS4); (22) community health and safety plans, including traffic and influx (PS4); (23) cultural heritage survey and chance-finds procedure (PS8); (24) GHG estimate and, above 100,000 t CO2e a year, the climate change risk assessment (EP4; method sec:84.6); (25) pollution prevention: air, water, noise and waste permits and monitoring (PS3); (26) human rights risk screening against the UN Guiding Principles (50.8.3); (27) prior IESC, DFI or ECA reports and any accountability-mechanism complaints (history); (28) the E&S budget and schedule in the construction plan and model (cost and timing, Ch 40).

Red flags:
- Land access scheduled before compensation is paid.
- A government-led land acquisition with no supplementary plan by the project.
- An ESIA that never mentions alternatives.
- Grievances closed faster than any process could investigate them.
- Security forces deployed around the site without an agreed code of conduct.
- An offset site that is also earmarked for a government project.

Rules of thumb and their limits:
- Resettlement costs on Category A projects routinely exceed appraisal estimates (NT2 ran well above its appraisal estimates); budget contingency in proportion to the census's quality.
- The grievance count is a lead indicator of blockade risk; a sustained rise over two reporting periods warrants an IESC visit.
- E&S CPs that depend on government action take longer than those the sponsor controls; start them first.

Common mistakes:
- Treating the ESIA approval by the host regulator as satisfying lender standards.
- Equating FPIC with a veto, or with a single meeting.
- Budgeting compensation at government schedule rates instead of replacement cost.
- Letting the EPC contractor carry no E&S obligations.

Questions experts ask:
- Who was missed in the census, and how would we know?
- What did affected households earn before, and how will we measure it after?
- Which E&S commitment depends on the government, and what happens if it does not act?
- What would a campaign against this project say, and is any of it true?

## 50.12 Judgment drill

Situation: You are the DFI's E&S lead on the USD 418.6 million financing of Wahgi Falls Hydro (fictional), a 186 MW hydropower project in Jiwaka Province, Papua New Guinea, where most land is held under customary tenure (Illustrative). On Tuesday, May 4, 2027, two weeks before the first drawdown, the IESC reports that 23 of 140 households on the access road have not received compensation because of a title dispute between two clans, and that construction on that road is scheduled to start in 30 days. The sponsor proposes paying compensation into escrow and starting work. The commercial banks want to proceed; your institution's policy requires compensation before land access.

Reasoning outline: apply PS5 (compensation before access; escrow as a recognized solution where ownership is disputed if the process is fair and documented); test with Framework 50.1 (risk of blockade and campaign versus cost of delay, using Example 50.1's daily cost method); options: drawdown with a condition excluding the disputed section, re-sequencing the works, escrow plus an agreed dispute resolution process with the clans and a grievance commitment, livelihood support during the dispute. Expert answer: allow first drawdown with a condition that bars works on the disputed section until escrow is funded, a dispute process is agreed and documented with both clans, and the IESC confirms; re-sequence the EPC program; do not waive.

What would change the answer: evidence that the dispute is manufactured by a third party seeking payment (escrow plus process remains the answer, monitoring intensifies); the road being on the critical path with no re-sequencing possible (the cost of delay rises, but the answer stays: price the delay, do not skip the standard); one clan rejecting any process (escalate to the government under its land acquisition obligations in the IA).

## 50.13 Exercise set (16)

Tier 1 (4):
- 50.1: Name the eight Performance Standards and give one financing consequence for each.
- 50.2: Explain why FPIC is not a veto under PS7 and EP4, paraphrasing the standards' wording (`t-es-standards`).
- 50.3: Distinguish the ESMP, the ESAP and the ESCP.
- 50.4: Explain two channels by which E&S failure reaches debt service.

Tier 2 (7):
- 50.5: Rework Example 50.1 (Kutch Coastal Wind) for a 95-day blockade with PPA delay LDs capped at USD 2.0 million. Answers: daily cost USD 78,606; construction cost of the blockade USD 7.468 million (76.2% of the USD 9.8 million remaining contingency); PPA LDs for 65 days uncapped USD 2.711 million, capped at USD 2.0 million; total USD 9.468 million, leaving USD 0.33 million of contingency. Common wrong answer: charging LDs from day one instead of after the 30-day float.
- 50.6: Apply Framework 50.2 to four further 2026 mandates of Banco Meridional (fictional) and justify each answer: (a) a project-related corporate loan of USD 117.3 million aggregate to a Brazilian pulp producer, the bank's individual commitment USD 52.4 million before sell-down to USD 30.0 million, tenor 3 years, majority of proceeds to a mill under the borrower's effective operational control; (b) a project finance advisory mandate for a USD 9.4 million mini-grid portfolio in Nigeria; (c) the 2027 refinancing of a Chilean toll road financed under EP III in 2019 and completed in 2022; (d) project finance of USD 186.2 million for an onshore wind farm in Norway, a Designated Country, on land used for Sámi reindeer herding. Answers: (a) in scope (the three tests are met; the individual commitment is measured before syndication, so the later sell-down does not matter); (b) out of scope (below USD 10 million total project capital cost); (c) out of scope (refinance is in scope only before project completion); (d) in scope; Principle 3 applies host-country law with the EPFI evaluating PS guidance for specific risks, and FPIC applies because EP4 extends it to Designated Countries where PS7 special circumstances (impacts on lands under customary use) arise. The writer checks Norway's Designated status on the EP website and dates the statement.
- 50.7: Rework Example 50.4 (Mulungushi Gorge) with 104 households, replacement land at USD 9,100 per hectare and 23 vulnerable households (other unit rates unchanged). Answers (USD): housing 1,918,800; land 1,533,168; crops and trees 222,560; transitional allowance 308,880; livelihood 355,680; vulnerable support 96,600; community facilities 412,000; RAP implementation 287,000; subtotal 5,134,688; contingency 770,203; total 5,904,891; USD 56,778 per household.
- 50.8: Example 50.5: find the condition uplift needed on the 640 ha offset site to meet the 1.5x requirement without more land. Answer: required gain 332.3 habitat units, so the uplift must be 0.519 condition points, from 0.38 to 0.90, which is not credible for degraded woodland; the answer is more land, a different offset site, or redesign to avoid loss.
- 50.9: Draft an E&S covenant with a cure period and a serious-incident notification obligation for the Wahgi Falls Hydro common terms agreement (the drill's deal); annotate the sponsor and DFI positions.
- 50.10: Draft five scope questions for an IESC on the Khulna South Power gas pipeline corridor (the walkthrough's Category A project), each tied to an item in Exhibit 50.6.
- 50.11: Example 50.3: compute the emissions of a 150 MW OCGT peaker in Khulna division at an 18% capacity factor and 0.55 t CO2/MWh and say which thresholds it crosses. Answers: generation 236.5 GWh; emissions 130,086 t CO2 a year; above the PS3 quantification threshold (25,000 t CO2e) and the EP4 threshold for a climate change risk assessment and public annual GHG reporting (100,000 t CO2e).

Tier 3 (5):
- 50.12: Case P: using the twelve ESAP items of Annex P 4.3, map each to a CP to first drawdown, a drawdown condition, a covenant with a deadline or a reporting obligation, and state the evidence that proves completion. Model answer: for example, item 1 (RAP implementation and payment before land entry, with an independent completion audit) as a CP to the first drawdown for each land parcel plus a covenant for the audit (done September 2020); item 12 (annual E&S monitoring reports) as a reporting covenant.
- 50.13: Case P: explain, with Framework 50.1, how the 2021 fishers' grievance and the USD 3.27 million additional compensation reached the lenders (grievance mechanism, then the ABDB's independent accountability mechanism, then cost inside the hard cost overrun) and what earlier indicator they could have watched (the RAP's exclusion of landless fishing households; the exclusion zone's design; the grievance log). No new figures.
- 50.14: Real case: using `nam-theun-2` and `bujagali`, write a 400-word memo to a credit committee on what the two projects teach about sequencing resettlement and protecting offsets.
- 50.15: Model task (macro-free): add an E&S cost line set (RAP, offset, monitoring, contingency) to a construction budget sheet with monthly columns, with a check row that flags any month in which land-access dates precede compensation dates (Ch 40 layout). Use Example 50.4's budget phased over 18 months; answer key gives the rows, formulas and the check's expected result (no flags when compensation is paid two months before access).
- 50.16: Write the IESC's terms of reference for the Khulna South Power Category A gas pipeline corridor (38 km, 2026 financing by EPFIs, a DFI and an ECA): applicable standards (IFC PS, EP4, the ECA's Common Approaches classification, host law), scope by Performance Standard, site visits, deliverables dated against a close calendar (ESDD report before credit approval, ESAP agreed before signing, CP confirmations before first drawdown, quarterly monitoring during construction), the 30-day ESIA disclosure requirement for the ECA, reliance and cap, independence, and fee. Full model answer: a one-page terms of reference with each paragraph annotated (capabilities review fix).

## 50.15 Glossary entries owned

Entries follow `bible/glossary-canon.md`; every term below has its canon home in this chapter. "OECD Common Approaches" is confirmed here by R-090.

| Term | Abbreviation | Definition | Home section |
|---|---|---|---|
| environmental and social | E&S | The impacts of a project on the natural environment and on people, including workers, communities and indigenous peoples, and the risks those impacts create. | 50.1.1 |
| social license to operate | – | The ongoing acceptance of a project by affected communities and the wider public, without which legal rights to operate become hard to exercise. | 50.1.3 |
| IFC Performance Standards | IFC PS | The eight IFC standards (2012 edition) on environmental and social sustainability that IFC clients must meet and that other lenders adopt as a benchmark. | 50.2.1 |
| mitigation hierarchy | – | The sequence of avoiding, then minimizing, then compensating for or offsetting adverse impacts. | 50.2.1 |
| environmental and social management system | ESMS | A project company's system of policies, procedures, people and monitoring for managing E&S risks over the project's life. | 50.2.1 |
| Equator Principles | EP | A voluntary risk-management framework under which signatory financial institutions apply E&S standards to in-scope project-related financings; the current version is EP4 (2020). | 50.2.2 |
| Equator Principles Financial Institution | EPFI | A financial institution that has adopted the Equator Principles. | 50.2.2 |
| Designated Country | – | Under EP4, a country that is both an OECD member and on the World Bank's high-income list, where host-country law is the applicable standard. | 50.2.2 |
| Category A, B, C | – | EP4 and IFC classifications of projects by the severity of potential E&S impacts, from significant and irreversible (A) to minimal (C). | 50.2.2 |
| Environmental and Social Framework | ESF | The World Bank's framework (2016) of ten Environmental and Social Standards applying to its investment project financing to governments. | 50.2.3 |
| Environmental and Social Commitment Plan | ESCP | The legally binding plan of a World Bank borrower's E&S commitments under the ESF. | 50.2.3 |
| OECD Common Approaches | – | The OECD recommendation under which export credit agencies screen, classify and review the E&S impacts of projects they support. | 50.2.4 |
| environmental and social impact assessment | ESIA | The study that identifies a project's E&S baseline, impacts, alternatives and mitigation, and is disclosed to affected people. | 50.3.1 |
| cumulative impact assessment | – | Assessment of a project's impacts in combination with other existing and planned developments affecting the same resources or communities. | 50.3.1 |
| environmental and social management plan | ESMP | The plan setting out the measures, responsibilities, schedule and monitoring that implement an ESIA's mitigation. | 50.3.2 |
| environmental and social action plan | ESAP | The lenders' list of dated actions that close gaps between a project and the applicable standards, enforced through the finance documents (EPAP under EP4). | 50.3.3 |
| independent environmental and social consultant | IESC | The adviser who reviews E&S documentation and compliance for the lenders and monitors the project after financial close. | 50.3.4 |
| stakeholder engagement plan | SEP | A project's plan for identifying stakeholders, disclosing information and consulting affected communities. | 50.4.1 |
| grievance mechanism | – | A project-level process for receiving, investigating and resolving complaints from affected communities or workers. | 50.4.2 |
| physical displacement | – | Relocation or loss of shelter caused by project land acquisition. | 50.5.1 |
| economic displacement | – | Loss of assets or access to assets that leads to loss of income or livelihood, with or without relocation. | 50.5.1 |
| full replacement cost | – | Compensation sufficient to replace lost assets at market value plus transaction costs, without deduction for depreciation. | 50.5.1 |
| resettlement action plan | RAP | The plan for compensating and relocating physically displaced people and restoring their livelihoods. | 50.5.1 |
| livelihood restoration plan | LRP | The plan for restoring the incomes of economically displaced people who are not relocated. | 50.5.1 |
| cut-off date | – | The date after which people or assets newly present in a project area are not eligible for compensation. | 50.5.2 |
| informed consultation and participation | ICP | The in-depth consultation PS7 requires with affected indigenous peoples in all cases. | 50.6.1 |
| free, prior and informed consent | FPIC | Agreement of affected indigenous peoples reached through good-faith negotiation in the circumstances PS7 specifies; it does not require unanimity and confers no individual veto. | 50.6.2 |
| critical habitat | – | Habitat of high biodiversity value under PS6, where a project may proceed only under strict conditions including net gains. | 50.7.1 |
| biodiversity offset | – | Measurable conservation gains designed to compensate for residual biodiversity losses after avoidance and minimization. | 50.7.2 |
| net gain | – | A biodiversity outcome in which gains exceed losses for the values affected, required in critical habitat under PS6. | 50.7.2 |
| human rights due diligence | – | Assessment and management of a project's actual and potential human rights impacts, by reference to the UN Guiding Principles on Business and Human Rights. | 50.8.3 |

## 50.16 Neighbors

Before: Chapter 49, Legal, insurance, model, tax, and integrity diligence. Lenders' legal and regulatory due diligence and legal opinions, graded with the red-flag grid; the insurance advisor's test of cover against debt service and spare lead times; the model audit engagement and the application of materiality; tax leakage in diligence; counterparty credit analysis of state-owned offtakers and contractors; KYC, sanctions screening with ownership tracing, and anti-corruption diligence; a request list for each workstream. Case P: Laurent Bécherel's June 2018 legal DD report and KYC on Groupe Talmé.

After: Chapter 51, The finance documents. Opens Part X: the document set and how it flows (mandate and commitment letters, term sheets, the common terms agreement, facility agreements, fee letters, ISDA in overview), representations, conditions precedent, covenants, events of default, remedies, equity cures, permitted debt and distributions, change of control, amendments and voting, and LMA, LSTA and APLMA practice. Case P: the common terms agreement negotiated by Laurent Bécherel and Adaeze Whitcombe, in which the diligence findings and the ESAP become conditions and covenants.
